#35910. How Netflix Taught an LLM to Recommend Movies So That You Keep Watching

P99 CONF is the technical conference for anyone who obsesses over high-performance, low-latency applications. Leading engineers from today’s most impressive gamechangers will be sharing 60+ talks on topics like Rust, Go, Zig, distributed data systems, Kubernetes, and AI/ML. Sign up to get 30-day access to the complete O’Reilly library & learning platform, free books, and a chance to win 1 of 500 free swag packs!

Join 30K of your peers for an unprecedented opportunity to learn from experts like Chip Huyen (author of the O’Reilly AI Engineering book), Madelyn Olson (Valkey co-creator) & Carl Lerche (Tokio creator) and more – for free, from anywhere.

Bonus: Registrants get immediate 30-day access to the complete O’Reilly library, and attendees can enter to win 1 of 500 free swag packs.

Recommendations are one of the most important pieces of the Netflix experience. If you’ve watched Netflix, you would have noticed the Netflix homepage recommending a bunch of movies and TV shows. More often than not, they happen to be stuff that might be of interest to you. They also keep changing with time based on what you’ve watched.

The original Netflix recommendations engine was built on top of 1000s of hand‑crafted features across users, items, and interactions. It also used special architecture for various functions that contributed to the task of making recommendations.

Though a lot of changes have happened to this stack over the years, it’s still pretty complex. It’s costly to onboard new use cases. At the same time, large language models (LLMs) have made great strides. They have progressed a lot in their ability to recommend things to a user. Due to their broader world knowledge and understanding of language, LLMs do a good job of figuring out relationships between different things. They can also make recommendations using natural language, which is a plus point. However, you cannot just pick an LLM off-the-shelf to generate recommendations for a company like Netflix.

This is why the Netflix engineering team built GenRec.

The basic idea behind GenRec is to use an LLM to make better sense of a user’s viewing history to assign scores to the various movies and TV shows streaming on Netflix. However, you can’t just give a general-purpose LLM a list of movies or shows that a user has watched and expect it to come up with interesting recommendations. Netflix has to make a bunch of adjustments to the base model to make it work with their content and member behavior.

In this article, we will look at how GenRec was built. Here’s what we will cover:

How user behaviour helps create training examples

What GenRec learns and how it produces recommendations

Disclaimer: This post is based on publicly shared details from various sources. References at the end. Please comment if you notice any inaccuracies.

A recommendation system has to answer a basic question: What should appear first in the list of recommendations?

The answer to this question is the key to deciding which items to show a particular person and in what order. On Netflix, those items can include movies, series, games, and other content types.

The order of the recommendations is super important. A user’s attention span is limited. A viewer might check only a few titles before making a choice. If they don’t find something interesting in that time, they might even leave the platform to check elsewhere. It doesn’t matter if something that the user might like is present in the catalog. You can say that the recommendation system has failed to do its job.

The ordering of the recommendations is handled by a component known as the ranker. It assigns scores to various items based on how suitable they appear for a particular user in a particular situation. The better an item’s score, the higher it appears on the recommendation list. To decide this score, a ranker needs a lot of information. Here are some examples:

The member’s interaction history to understand what they like or dislike.

Item metadata to make sense of what a piece of content is about.

Details about the device the viewer is using. Time of day. Language or region.

Netflix’s GenRec is capable of ranking the full content catalog. It can also work with a smaller set of content choices that are provided to it. You can use the top-K ranking, which means the highest-ranked K items. Here, K is the number of items you need.

The goal goes beyond predicting what the user will click or play next. Netflix wants to recommend content that can satisfy a user so that they keep coming back for more. This means that a user playing a movie is a great indicator that the user was interested in that recommendation. But it doesn’t guarantee that the recommendation was valuable.

Turning new models into systems that work inside real customer operations is still hard.

That gap is creating demand for engineers who can move between code, customer context, and production outcomes. Enter: the forward deployed engineer.

The free State of FDE Jobs 2026 report maps the emerging labor market around this work.

Netflix already has some very sophisticated recommendation models. Its production systems use thousands of features, representations, and specialized model components. You can think of a feature as an input signal that helps a model make some prediction. For example, we can tell a recommendation model how many comedy movies or shows a user has watched recently. Similarly, we can tell the model how long ago a user has seen a particular piece of content. Or how frequently the user finishes a series. All these features can help the model predict what the user might want to see.

To create a new feature, you need to decide which observations are important and how to calculate their value. Also, how to make them available for training and prediction. This is known as feature engineering. A mature system also needs a way to make sense of a sequence of actions that a user might perform. For example, if a user has watched several episodes of a series in order, it tells something different from a user starting several unrelated shows, but not watching them after some time.

Over time, these systems keep getting more complex. If you want to support a new content type or a new product surface, you need to add more features. You also need to change the model architecture and perform infrastructure work. Also, you need to run multiple experiments to make sure everything runs fine.

The Netflix engineering team wanted to see whether a broadly capable LLM can take on more of this interpretation work. Instead of engineering so many separate signals, the system can describe relevant behavior and content in plain text. The model can be trained to interpret it.

An LLM has learned a lot of relationships between words, concepts, and descriptions during its training.

This means that the LLM has a much better starting point to make sense of movies and shows and how people interact with them. For example, you can have two movies that belong to different categories but they share themes. An LLM can understand this connection based on their descriptions. It can also make sense of the history of actions that a user has done for movie.

Netflix calls this process of expressing the information in text as verbalization.

However, a general-purpose LLM is not a good recommender. There are several problems. It may favor globally popular titles. It might suggest items that are not part of the catalog. Also, it doesn’t support personalization. This is why Netflix didn’t go for any off-the-shelf LLM. Instead, the engineering team built GenRec.

GenRec mixes the language understanding of an LLM with special training that helps it work with the Netflix catalog. The LLM provides a foundation for interpreting the data. But Netflix still has to teach it what a good recommendation looks like and control which items it can recommend.

Netflix trained GenRec in two phases with different purposes. Let’s look at both phases in detail.

This phase is to make GenRec familiar with the domain. The team makes the model learn and interpret Netflix-related information. Only then is it trained for the specific task of ranking recommendations.

The Netflix engineering team starts with an open-source foundation LLM and adapts it using proprietary Netflix data. You can think of a foundation model as a broadly useful model that can support multiple applications. After this step, the model gains knowledge of Netflix content and user behavior.

This newly trained model becomes a starting point for multiple lines of work. This model is quite stable. Netflix updates it only after some time period. Not frequently.

The second phase turns the foundational LLM into GenRec.

In this phase, the team trains the model on specific examples and objectives. The aim is to rank things properly based on reward signals. Also, while meeting the cost targets.

We can call this phase post-training, meaning additional training that makes an existing model better for a specific job. Netflix runs this phase more frequently because you need to keep evolving things as new movies and shows are released. Also, viewer choices change. Even a model with strong general knowledge has to keep itself updated based on the latest trends.

Netflix records many kinds of user interactions. For example, click pays, viewing time, thumbs-up or thumbs-down feedback, adding to watchlist, and abandoned viewing sessions. These records are rich material for creating training examples.

The team turns these examples into conversations. The “user message” contains the member’s history, context, metadata, and the recommendation task. The “assistant message” describes what action the user performed. For example, you can have the input talk about a user who recently completed several mystery episodes, gave positive feedback, and is now watching TV in the evening. The output could talk about the next show this user watched, how long they watched, and whether they gave any feedback.

As you can see, the output tells what happened next in a particular situation. This helps the model relate what happened before with what happened next. The conversations are just a format to make training easy. It’s not like the users must also chat with Netflix to get recommendations.

There is a big difference between training and actual use. While training, the conversation format makes it easy to achieve language objectives. While actual use, GenRec receives the context to score movies and TV shows based on the ranking component.

LLMs process text in the form of tokens. They are pieces of text such as words or parts of words.

The amount of tokens we send to the model have an impact on the cost. It also consumes the context budget. This means that describing a user’s entire activity history would be expensive. This leads to a new engineering problem. We need to decide what information to include in the prompt.

Netflix calls this context engineering. While building the context, they take into account how useful a particular event is and how much text might be required to put it into words. Some key factors are taken into account:

Strong evidence: Some actions provide strong evidence about a user’s preferences. A long viewing session or explicit positive feedback may deserve a detailed description. Other actions may be less informative. Let’s say a very short viewing or quick hover might reflect accidental or casual exploration. The team omits such events to avoid spending tokens on analyzing weak evidence.

Repetitive behavior: You can represent repeated actions in a more compact manner. For example, if the user has gone through many similar episodes, you can summarize this as a long viewing session of a specific series.

Selective details: Some items need more explanation than others. For example, let’s say we have a new movie with very little interaction history. This is like a cold start. In this case, we need to provide richer metadata.

Context length: The team tests how ranking quality is modified as more historical events are included. Initially, additional history helps a lot. But over time, the improvements become smaller.

Netflix adjusts how many words should be used to describe an event. Using techniques such as cleaning, compression, and word changes, they report that tokens have been reduced to roughly 1/3rd of the original amount.

GenRec brings together multiple training objectives. Each objective addresses a different requirement.

You can think of a training objective as a way to describe what the model should improve. Improvement is guide through a numerical loss value. Training adjusts the model’s parameters to reduce the loss value.

Let’s look at the key training objectives in detail.

This is the primary objective. It teaches GenRec to assign higher scores to each movie or show in the catalog.

Netflix identifies positive examples using engagements such as long playtime or strong feedback. They also apply thresholds and filtering to reduce noise in those labels. It’s important to do so because an action is not always a reliable sign of user preference. You cannot treat every brief play of a movie or show as equally important. They also penalize the model when it assigns too little probability to a relevant catalog item among the available alternatives.

Netflix also keeps a language-modeling objective over the text used in training.

This helps the model make sense of user history, understand more about movies and shows, and act on instructions expressed in natural language. In other words, recommendation training should preserve the language capabilities that make a foundation model useful.

The team has also kept open the possibility of future text applications. For example, explaining recommendations.

You also need the model to not give excessive influence to some specific events. For example, repeated binge-watching can produce multiple events. But this doesn’t mean that you give it excessive weight. Otherwise, the system could become too focused on one type of content.

Netflix addresses this using reward-weighted training.

They have separate reward models that provide signals about the value of certain engagements. There are signals that contribute to longer-term outcomes. For example, return behavior, exploration, continuous engagement, etc. Other signals help balance out behavior across different types of content, new movie releases, and other trending titles.

Once trained, GenRec can transform a text description into scores for movies and TV shows in the Netflix catalog.

First, a component known as the verbalizer brings together the user’s watch history, current context, and relevant metadata into a bunch of text. The LLM processes this text sequence and produces internal numerical representations.

Netflix also extracts a pooled hidden state. This is a compact numerical representation of the information that makes sense for a recommendation request. “Pooled” means that information from the model’s processing is collected in a way that another component can use. Don’t think of it as a written summary.

Each catalog item has a learned representation. It also has a learned embedding. You can think of an embedding as a list of numbers a machine-learning model can work with.

Therefore, the model has a clear idea of the user’s current situation and a representation of each item it needs to consider. A scoring head connects the two. You can think of the head as an output component attached to the main model. GenRec’s ranking head combines the user’s context representation with a movie or show’s embedding to generate a score.

Some scoring functions include a dot product or a small neural network. The key idea is that the scoring function learns how the two representations are related. GenRec converts the various scores into a distribution using softmax and uses them to order the recommendation items. Softmax also changes scores into nonnegative values. All of them have a sum of one across the scored set.

The Netflix engineering team trains the main LLM, ranking head, and catalog item embeddings together so that they learn to support the same task. Since the head scores only the Netflix catalog items, it cannot recommend something that is not available in the catalog.

GenRec doesn’t generate recommendations word by word. To understand how it works, you need to understand how a typical text-generating LLM works. It has two stages:

First, it processes the input prompt. This stage is commonly called prefill.

Next, it generates output tokens one after another. Each new token depends on the earlier context. This stage is called autoregressive decoding.

GenRec uses the first stage to understand the recommendation context. It then gets the scores from the ranking head.

Netflix describes this as prefill-only inference. In this approach, we process the prompt just once. The model scores the catalog or candidate set in a single forward pass without generating an answer token by token. There is no cost of making the LLM write out a recommendation list.

The engineering team at Netflix serves GenRec using vLLM. It’s software for running LLM workloads. It also keeps the costs in control through smaller or distilled models. You can think of a distilled model as a smaller model trained to learn the behavior of a larger model. The idea is to get high-quality results with lower computational costs.

Netflix also structures prompts to maximize shared prefixes so that prefix caching becomes easy. When requests start with identical text, work done for that shared beginning can be reused. In Netflix’s experiments, when context length was roughly reduced to 1/3rd, it also produced a similar reduction in serving cost.

The Netflix engineering team evaluates GenRec both offline and through a live A/B test. Both methods are for covering different angles.

Offline evaluation is to check whether GenRec ranks known relevant items well. To handle this, the team uses recorded data to measure ranking quality before running a live experiment. One metric that is used is the Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR). It rewards the model when it puts the first relevant item at the top of a ranked list.

Online evaluation checks if making a change has a positive impact on the usage.

In an A/B test, different groups gets different versions of a system so that we can compare the results. Netflix ran a test that covered approx 10% of traffic over 4 weeks. There was an improvement of 0.115% in a short-term homepage engagement metric and 0.006% in a long-term core metric. Netflix labels both results as statistically significant.

The best takeaway you can have from this case study on GenRec is a shift in how modern recommendation systems are being built.

If you look at Netflix, it’s clear that most of the input design work involves context engineering. Developers have to decide which user interactions are important. Also, how far back they should look for data. The metadata that should be used. And how to represent everything efficiently.

We can also have more applications that share a foundation model. Things that can vary are data, the objectives during post-training, reward signals, and the way they provide outputs. The infrastructure also changes with the model. Concerns like GPU serving, batching, caching, etc, are more important.

GenRec shows how all of these ideas can fit together in an enterprise working at scale:

The foundation model comes with language and domain understanding

Context engineering helps give the relevant data to the model.

Specialized training teaches recommendation quality.

Prefill-only serving keeps a check on a major source of inference cost.

GenRec: Towards LLM-Native Recommendation at Netflix

Effective context engineering for AI agents

The part most engineers miss is that this shift changes what's worth learning. Hand-crafted feature pipelines were a career for years; now the leverage sits in whoever can frame behaviour as text the model can interpret.

That reframing skill is quietly repricing engineering salaries far outside Netflix.

Worth watching as a comp signal, not just an architecture one.

Prefill-only scoring is the quiet hero here. Once you drop autoregressive decode, the cost model looks a lot more like a dense ranker than a chat completion — and prefix caching + aggressive context engineering suddenly matter more than model size. The cold-start path still feels underspecified to me: when a title is hours old and has almost no co-occurrence trail, do you lean harder on verbalized metadata in the prompt, refresh only the catalog embeddings, or keep a separate retrieval lane that bypasses GenRec until the title has enough engagement signal?

#35916. 堀江貴文氏の濃厚キス動画が物議 てんちむ「一瞬で食欲失せる」

この記事の見出しと要約は、ライブドアニュースが独自の機能で作成したものです。試験提供中の機能のため、記事本文と併せてご確認ください。

ホリエモンこと実業家の堀江貴文氏と女性インフルエンサー・丸の内ＯＬレイナの〝濃厚キス動画〟が物議を醸している。

堀江氏は２４日、自身のユーチューブチャンネルで「丸の内ＯＬレイナ・堀大輔カップルが会いに来た」を公開。動画ではレイナの交際相手である自称ショートスリーパーの掘大輔氏も同席。３人で焼肉を囲みながら、レイナが手がける恋愛リアリティーショーについて語り合った。

その流れでキスの話題となり、レイナは堀江氏とのキスを了承。堀江氏も確認を取った上で、レイナの顔をグッと自分の方に引き寄せキスの態勢に入る――。ここで堀江氏のユーチューブ動画は終了。すると翌２５日にレイナがＳＮＳで〝その先〟を映した動画を投稿。そこにはカレシの堀氏が見守るなか、レイナが堀江氏とねっとりキスする姿が映されていた。

この切り抜き動画がＸを中心に拡散。反応したのは、レイナと交流があるユーチューバーのてんちむだった。

２７日までに更新したＸで「どんなに腹ペコでも、切り抜きで流れてくる某ＯＬレイナのキス動画を見ると一瞬で食欲が失せる」とバッサリ。続けて「あれは究極のダイエット動画。すごい」と皮肉交じりに称賛した。

これにレイナ本人が「そんなキモかった？」とＸで問いかけ。てんちむは再び「むしろ感謝してる。痩せさせてくれてありがとう」とイジった。ネット上でも〝キス動画〟に様ざまな声が寄せられている。

#35917. Government turning to AI to solve the acknowledged problem of medication shortages

President Claudia Sheinbaum has introduced an Advisory Council for the Digital Transformation of the Health Sector, aimed at improving control, storage and distribution of medications.

The move was in response to supply shortages publicized during recent mobilizations by Mexican Social Security Institute (IMSS) workers. The president has acknowledged the legitimacy of the complaints.

According to an announcement made during Sheinbaum’s morning press conference on Friday, the council will be divided into two groups of experts.

The first group will be the Advisory Board composed of four researchers specializing in logistics, engineering, information systems, and resource distribution, while the second group — dubbed the Youth AI Team — will be made up of five individuals specialized in Artificial Intelligence.

The project will use digital tools and AI to detect medication shortages, track warehouse inventory and expedite delivery to public hospitals and health centers.

“What we want is to modernize the system to ensure that the entire process — from procurement, warehousing and arrival at the hospital pharmacy, to distribution across various hospital departments and delivery to patients — is automated, so that we know the exact traceability of every medication,” Sheinbaum said on Friday.

Members of the council are expected to present their initial projects in November. The federal government plans to begin implementing the new system in January 2027.

Deputy Health Minister Eduardo Clark said the program aims to identify medication needs in real time, improve inventory management, and optimize distribution routes to reduce both time and costs. If a medication is unavailable at one pharmacy, healthcare staff would be able to determine whether it is in stock at another health facility or warehouse and arrange for its delivery.

Public healthcare workers plan more protests this week to demand better pay and working conditions

The scale of the program’s challenge, however, is considerable.

According to figures presented by Clark, the health sector uses approximately 3 billion units of medicines and medical supplies each year. The products must be distributed among more than 1,000 hospitals and nearly 15,000 health centers and primary care units.

Sheinbaum explained that the lack of medication does not always stem from non-procurement. She noted that there are instances when medications have been purchased but fail to reach medical facilities on time, due to difficulties in the processes of storage, distribution and delivery.

Also, Sheinbaum’s administration recently said it has identified practices in which medications from the public sector end up being sold privately and, in some cases, outside of hospitals.

With reports from Latinus, Eje Central, La Silla Rota and Infobae

#35920. Rewinding the exponential: How we keep hardware usage down

Cathy Chi,Â Sergey Nuzhdin,Â andÂ Christian Endres

More than 20% of the web sits behind Cloudflareâs network, and our team, the Data team, owns the data pipeline responsible for plumbing that data (and more)Â from our global network to applicable central data centers. The Cloudflare network is a globally distributed system supporting tens of thousands of servers. Data that power Cloudflare Logs, SQL API, observability, internal analytics, and more flow through this networkÂ and use our data pipeline to serve customers.

In May of this year, we were providing data delivery at roughly two billion events per second. This includes customer traffic as well as data sent from internal teams. Year after year, we tackle the same problem â exponential traffic growth against linear hardware growth. We have to react to this increase in order to keep resource costs under control. Over the past five years, weâve made various improvements to compensate for the data volume increase in comparison to hardware allocation, equivalent to deferring two and a half years of hardware expansion.Â This post details those changes: breaking up our monolithic data schema in 2022, adjusting compression in 2024, rearchitecting pipeline replication in spring 2026, decentralizing sampling in summer 2026, and introducing aggregations into the pipeline in fall 2026.

Our pipeline starts with an internal service called Logfwdr, where we collect from highly distributed data sources (think: Workers, FL). We then feed this data to a set of centralized receivers, which eventually push them onto our buffering system, Buftee. Consumers fetch data from Buftee and distribute data to their final destinations, like ClickHouse, which we rely on heavily.

In 2022, we dismantled our one and only schema. Previously, there was a single stream packed with every product's fields, forcingÂ Cloudflare-internal consumersÂ to ingest everything and demux or discard records via filters. At the time, most of Cloudflareâs data products were built on HTTP, Security and CDN metadata. In 2021, our message schema looked like this:

Some fields here belonged under the same product, but some were completely irrelevant to each other. The hypothesis was that none of these teams require 100% of fields in the monolithic schema. In other words, we can create small messages for each productâs required fields. Although we were going to duplicate fields, some of them in every message, replacing this firehose with product-specific schemas actually reduced the backbone bandwidth usage by 35-40%, knocking out roughly a yearâs worth of compressed traffic growth.

ByÂ 2024, as traffic steadily grew, we were tackling network congestion issues on a daily basis. To react to this, we changed our compression algorithm from LZ4 to zstd. We had already experimented with zstd compression before, but we noticed a higher CPU usage at the time. Therefore, we were careful to track the metrics to ensure the trade-off was worth it.

Initially, we rolled this change out to a few servers. To our relief, we didnât observe a noticeable difference in CPU usage.

With more confidence, we gradually rolled out to our entire fleet, which also showed little impact to CPUs. In the end, there was nearly a 40% reduction in compressed traffic throughput.Â The uncompressed bytes remained at the same growth, meaning we could support more than 1.5x the amount of traffic with the same size pipe. Or another way to put it, the estimated growth-equivalent reductions put us at about 10 months prior.

Now, in 2026, we attempted three strategies to reduce uncompressed bytes (and thus, compressed bytes).

To make analytics resilient against regional disasters, our systems require redundant data to be stored in multiple centralized regions (data subject to Customer Metadata BoundariesÂ is storedÂ strictly within its designated region). Historically, the simplest way to achieve disaster resilience was double-writing: Logfwdr transmitted two full copies of every non-regional analytics event â one to each centralized region. Each region then independently processed a complete copy through to ClickHouse. We also apply samplingÂ in each region, an incredibly scalable technique that works for all volumes of data during any traffic conditions. We downsample high-volume traffic while rare events are preserved.

This infrastructure was manageable when only a handful of streams used secondary regions. However, as we expanded disaster resilience across almost all analytics streams, Logfwdr traffic doubled across the board. Sending every event, twice, over wide-area transit was unsustainable against our exponential traffic growth.

We had already tackled a similar challenge for Logpush. For Logpush, Logfwdr treats the centralized data centers as a unified, shared pool of receivers: each message is sent to only one region, failing over to another destination if a machine or facility is unreachable. That model had already proven its worth during large incidents, shifting traffic seamlessly from one location to another without human intervention. This automatic failover was possible because Logpush delivery is stateless, as customer destinations do not care which centralized data center pushes the logs.

Analytics was trickier. Unlike stateless logs, ClickHouse clusters in both regions still need a full copy of non-regional data to allow instant, independent query failover. The breakthrough was decoupling pipeline transit from database redundancy:

1. Shared Ingest Pool: We adopted Logpushâs model to the analytics streams. Logfwdr dispatches each event exactly once across wide-area transit to whichever centralized receiver is available.

2. Late Replication from centralized regions:Â Instead of replicating traffic at source, replication happens as late as possible in the pipeline. Data lands in our distributed buffering layer, Buftee, and remote inserters stream cross-data-center copies downstream.

3. Resilient Failure Handling: If a centralized region goes down, Logfwdrs immediately routes all non-regional traffic to the surviving region. If the inter-data center connection blips, each cluster continues ingesting its local share without backpressure; remote inserters catch up from Buftee's retention buffer once the backbone recovers.

For the datasets we migrated, the cutover eliminated approximately 43 GB/s of compressed transit traffic while adding only ~7 GB/s of cross-data-center replication on our pipeline. Two architectural factors explain this asymmetry:

1. Replication happens downstream of sampling: In the old model, Logfwdrs transmitted two copies of raw, unsampled events over wide-area transit. In the new model, Logfwdrs send a single copy to centralized receivers, which immediately apply sampling before buffering into Buftee, carrying only this already-compacted stream.

2. Better compression through uniform partitioning: Data at source is fragmented across thousands of individual servers. After centralization, streams are aggregated and partitioned into uniform batches over wider time windows. This structural uniformity allows compression algorithms to achieve dramatically higher compression ratios than Logfwdr transit batches ever could.

Overall, this was a net reduction of 36 GB/s across wide-area networks. We rolled this out initially to two datasets, HTTP analytics and Ready-Analytics, our in-house one-click analytics product.

Weâve been actively migrating more datasets to this load-balanced architecture since the spring.

Another strategy we employed was further expanding our sampling mechanism so that we pulled critical processing earlier into the pipeline. Sampling was already used in our centralized services, particularly the receivers, easing pressure on downstream systems. We can be more resource efficient if we send less data downstream (without sacrificing data quality).

The solution here was to simply sample in Logfwdr, in addition to maintaining the sampling in centralized receivers. How the sampling algorithmÂ works here, in short, is each dataset has a key that producer teams have chosen to index and sample the data. When sampling, we first shuffle the records for each key to eliminate bias. Then, for each group of records, we select a sample size equal to the square root of the total count of the records. The result is a representative sample, whether sampled once or twice. To produce estimates, we scale calculations against the sample rate. For example, if we sample 10 out of 100 records, the sample rate would be 10/100, and the unbiased estimate of the original count would be 10*10 = 100.

To ensure this approach was working well, we compared the number of rows written to ClickHouse against the estimated sum for total count using a variety of keys across a variety of traffic footprints, so that âleast seenâ keys to âmost seenâ keys were captured across the spectrum.

The result is that for keys with more data, the sample rate decreases. However, keys with less data were sampled comparably. Thus, we still kept a representative sample while delivering and storing fewer events. Weâre not compromising the quality of data delivered, even when we deliver less in quantity.

If we look at this more closely, using power laws, we can observe that the double-sampled (red) grows at roughly x0.319 v. sampled-once/control (blue) at x0.485. Another way to picture this is, a key that has 100x more data than another key would have stored roughly 10x the number of rows if single-sampled. Now, it will only store roughly 4x the number of rows. Concretely, a total of 11.6 million events is represented by 18k rows in ClickHouse as opposed to 79k. Double sampling attenuates storage of the âmost seenâ keys, while still retaining the same detail per event for âleast seenâ keys.

We initially implemented this strategy against three of our top datasets. Every single one at least halved the amount of compressed data we were originally sending on the wire to centralized receivers. Thatâs 25GB/s altogether, or over 2PB/day.

Cloudflare offers several analytics datasets built on rollups: aggregates of the raw events computed at ingest time. This makes the opposite tradeoff to sampling: rather than answering any question approximately, we answer a fixed set of questions exactly, provided we choose the dimensions and metrics up front.

For example, in the HTTP dataset, we measureÂ the number of unique occurrences of IP addresses during a given minute rather than the individual IPs themselves. Another example is we sum the total number of GET, POST, PUT, etc. requests for a given domain, for that minute, grouped by the HTTP method. None of these computations actually necessitate waiting until the tail-end of the pipeline to start calculating.

Historically, this aggregation occurred towards the end of the pipeline, inside the inserters which read from Buftee and insert into ClickHouse. Decentralizing this aggregation was an opportunity to spread out load and reduce the amount of data we centralize.

Hence, we built an aggregation functionality that is utilized every step of the way, creating repeated opportunities to combine matching partial aggregates as data converges. Much like sampling, records are grouped by a key, which determines which records can be combined. We aggregate on each instance of Logfwdr. We aggregate again after the partially aggregated events reach the centralized receivers, which push these re-aggregated events onto dedicated partitioned buffers. Because these buffers bring together records from multiple receivers, we aggregate another time after theyâve been read in batches by the ClickHouse inserters. Finally, we aggregate in ClickHouse with aggregating table engines.

The first dataset we rolled this out to was for the HTTP traffic. In just this one dataset alone, we dropped from 9+ GB/s to <2 GB/sÂ in compressed bytes traffic. Even though this is currently the highest cardinality dataset, the aggregations on it still gave us an approximate 80% reduction on the wire.

This aggregation feature is powerful for datasets that can collapse easily, especially for use cases like usage metrics where accuracy on the sums and counts is paramount. It also means we can offer producer teams another option to reduce resource usage besides sampling, which we did. Like Ready-Analytics, we created a generic in-house one-click aggregating analytics product, called Ready-Aggregates. As we continue to iterate on this new feature in the pipeline, we expect to see savings continue to mount.

The starkest proof of this exponential growth is how rapidly traffic growth consumes headroom.

Take Ready-Analytics for example, where two of the three mechanisms, load balancing and sampling techniques, were applied. In mid-May, load balancing dropped traffic by 50%, plunging from ~26 GB/s to ~13 GB/s. Two months later, traffic had already started surging back up to ~20 GB/s. Thatâs about half of the reduction, erased by organic traffic growth. We then created even more headroom by reducing this datasetâs throughput rate to the single digits, ~8GB/, when sampling rolled out.

Without both interventions, Ready-Analytics alone would have breached 60 GB/s by September. By processing less data in centralized data centers, we also save a significant amount of CPUs and memory. The following graphs show the impact of load-balancing and sampling in Logfwdr on the CPU and memory usage of just the Ready-Analytics receivers: a ~50% reduction, regaining ~750 CPUs and 3.5 TiB of memory.

Across the entire pipeline altogether, from spring to fall, load balancing (~38 GB/s saved), sampling (~25 GB/s saved across top datasets), and aggregating (~7+ GB/s so far), eliminated ~70 GB/s of compressed traffic from our backbone. These cliff drops provide immediate relief.

If we zoom out such that we look at five years from now, the changes made in this year alone have just as much impact as the short-term benefits. Weâve halved the amount of data weâd be processing in another half a decadeâs time. The structural and algorithmic optimizations we have and will continue to make are essential to keep hardware demands sustainable.

If youâre interested in helping us solve these challenges at scale, weâre hiring!

#35921. 50 Best 2026: un solo ristorante italiano nella classifica da 51 a 100

La classifica delle posizioni 51-100 di The 50 Best Restaurants 2026, svelata oggi, vede la presenza di un solo ristorante italiano. Si tratta del ristorante Uliassi di Senigallia, che troviamo alla 51a posizione, in discesa rispetto al 2025, dove era al 43° posto.

Mentre esce di classifica Al Gatto Verde, che era al 92° posto – anche se esiste la possibilità di una rimonta fra i primi 50 (che segnerebbe anche un incredibile balzo).

Il ristorante di Mauro e Catia Uliassi sulla spiaggia di Senigallia può vantare 3 stelle Michelin dal 2019. L’apertura risale al 1990 – per la precisione, era il 27 maggio. La filosofia di Uliassi? «Cucinare è avere la straordinaria possibilità di dare piacere agli altri».

Il nostro Fulvio Zendrini ci ha mangiato a luglio scorso: potete leggere il suo articolo qui, con la descrizione del menu Lab 26. Un piatto lo vedete qui sopra. Cannolicchio, fiori di pesco, sedano, sugo di cannolicchio e olio di pepe di Siltimur. Mentre in copertina potete vedere un altro piatto di questo menù. Per illustrare il suo piazzamento al #51 della classifica 51-100, The 50 Best 2026 ha scelto infatti il suo iconico colombaccio ai fichi e albicocche.

«Quello che un tempo era un modesto chiosco sulla “Spiaggia di Velluto” è oggi uno dei ristoranti di pesce più apprezzati d’Italia, capace di conservare intatto il calore di una gestione familiare.» Così The 50 Best 2026 descrive il ristorante di Mauro Uliassi e la sua posizione nella classifica 51-100. «Il menu affonda le radici nel gioco tra olfatto, memoria e gusto: la sua pasta affumicata alle vongole, ad esempio, nasce dal ricordo di una passeggiata accanto ai chioschi che preparavano pesce alla griglia sulle spiagge di Rimini.»

Rispetto alla classifica di The 50 Best del 2025, non troviamo più Al Gatto Verde, che nel 2026 esce dai primi 100 al mondo. Entrato in classifica proprio l’anno scorso, Al Gatto Verde si trova all’interno di Casa Maria Luigia, l’elegante residenza di campagna a San Damaso (Modena).

La struttura, creata da Massimo Bottura e di Lara Gilmore, fa capo alla loro Osteria Francescana. La stessa chef di Casa Maria Luigia, la canadese Jessica Rosval, viene dalle cucine della Francescana.

La classifica pubblicata oggi riguarda i posti dal 51 al 100: quella dei 50 Best 2026, sponsorizzata da S.Pellegrino & Acqua Panna, verrà rivelata durante la cerimonia del prossimo 4 novembre a Lima, in Perù. Che è la città dove si trova Maido, il ristorante di Mitsuharu ‘Micha’ Tsumura primo nella classifica dei 50 Best 2025.

Ristorante Uliassi. Banchina di Levante, 6, 60019 Senigallia AN. Telefono: 071 65463. Instagram

#35924. Here's what Microsoft's buyout offers actually include

As Microsoft sends out its buyout offers to more than a quarter of its workforce, specific offers reviewed by Business Insider reveal exactly what employees get if they choose to leave the company.

The offers are individualized for each employee based on their level, location, and tenure. Those reviewed by Business Insider confirm that the US buyouts align with the company's US standard severance offers. A Microsoft spokesperson said about 60,000 employees would be eligible for the buyout.

Microsoft uses a levels system to represent seniority, from entry levels starting at 59 to senior levels at 70, or vice president. The company offered buyouts to employees on select teams in the US, Canada, India, and the UK at level 70.

Offers reviewed by Business Insider show employees at internal levels 64 and below were offered one week of base pay per six months of service, and employees at levels 65 to 67 were offered two weeks of base pay per six months of service.

Buyout offers also include continued regular stock vesting and health insurance. The standard stock-vesting severance offer is six or 12 months for levels 67 and below, based on years of service.

Eligible employees will have until early December to decide whether to take the offer and exit at the end of the year, and there are no plans for another buyout at this time, a Microsoft spokesperson said.

Standard severance is capped at 39 weeks of base pay for levels 67 and below, while the standard severance offer for level 68 includes 12 months of salary.

Earlier this year, Microsoft rolled out a voluntary retirement program for employees. About 7% of Microsoft's US workforce, or about 8,750 people, were eligible at the time. As Business Insider previously reported, Microsoft's voluntary retirement program offer also aligned with the company's standard severance offer, including continued regular stock vesting for six or 12 months, based on years of service.

Earlier this year, another tech giant, Google, offered buyouts to staff in its business unit, Business Insider previously reported.

Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.

As more tech companies offer buyouts, some human resources professionals previously told Business Insider that this approach could soften job cuts, and also that when they're made available to workers of all tenures, companies risk losing talent.

#35926. Mining giant Grupo México plans to expand its US copper operations to the tune of US $4-6B

The mining and transportation conglomerate Grupo México announced on Thursday plans to double down on its investments in its U.S. copper mining unit, Asarco.

Grupo México plans to spend between US $4 billion and $6 billion over the next three years on Asarco projects, to expand the Ray and Silver Bell mines in Arizona and restart the Hayden smelter and refinery, the head of the mining division, Leonardo Contreras, told analysts on a call.

The investment is far higher than recent spending on the unit, which totaled just $101 million in 2024, and marks a significant expansion in the U.S. market.

It follows news of a 2% fall in copper output during the third quarter due to lower production in the U.S. and Peru. Even so, the firm’s profits grew by 56% during this period to a net profit of US $2 billion, owing to robust base metal prices. Overall revenue increased by 38% to $6.33 billion, surpassing consensus estimates from analysts polled by LSEG.

Corporate leadership has revised the company’s copper production target downward to 1.026 million metric tonnes in 2026, from the previously targeted 1.034 million tonnes.

Contreras emphasized that work is continuing on expanding the Ray facility as feasibility studies are conducted. In addition, the company is “increasingly optimistic” about the restarting of Hayden, which has an annual processing capacity of 1.4 million metric tonnes of copper concentrate.

“That would represent almost 350,000 metric ⁠tonnes of contained copper,” Contreras said. “If that feeds into our refinery, we could be looking at 500,000 metric tonnes of copper.”

The Silver Bell project is also in the feasibility stage, according to Contreras.

Grupo México plans to assess the potential to integrate Mexican concentrates and U.S. copper scrap into U.S. operations, according to Itaú analysts.

Grupo México will also continue expanding operations in Peru.

Its long-term growth focuses closely on its Peru operations through its Southern Copper subsidiary, with the delayed flagship Tia Maria project expected to finally come online in the first half of 2028.

Southern Copper is developing two other projects in Peru — Los Chancas and Michiquillay, both of which are expected to commence operations in 2032 and are expected to produce 130,000 and 225,000 metric tonnes of copper a year, respectively.

#35927. The LLM Blindspot: Why Models Forget What’s in the Middle of Your Prompt

AI agents are moving from demos into production, and they need more than a model. They need real-time data, fast answers, and strict limits on what they can read, write, and act on. At the Agentic Data Summit on December 9, the session Guardrails, Not Guesswork shows how to lock down agent access with the Agentic Data Plane. You’ll also see how streaming, SQL, and agents run on one platform, and get a look at where the Agentic Data Plane is headed next. It’s free, virtual, and built for engineers taking AI agents to production.

We can provide an LLM with exhaustive information through our prompts, but it can still fail to use it properly. When the required information sits somewhere in the middle of a long prompt, this type of failure becomes even more likely. We call it the “lost in the middle” effect or an LLM blind spot.

For example, imagine that we give an AI-based coding assistant a long collection of project documents. One specific paragraph explains that audit logs must be retained for 37 days. However, the coding assistant writes a cleanup function that deletes them after 30 days. The relevant rule was mentioned clearly. It was also part of the prompt and fit the model’s limits. Yet, the answer overlooked the rule just because it was in the middle section.

In this article, we’ll look at why LLMs have this bias against middle information. Here’s what we will cover:

What information is available to the model

Does moving the same information to different parts of the prompt change the answer

Why LLMs give more attention to the beginning and end of the prompt

Why larger context windows cannot solve the problem

Strategies to reduce the bias against middle information

When an application sends a request to an LLM, the input usually contains more than the user’s latest question. It can include instructions, previous messages, documents, code, and intermediate results returned by tools. Together, these form the model’s input context.

The model processes all of this information as tokens, which are basically small units of text. A token might represent a word, part of a word, punctuation, or another text fragment. The context window sets a limit on how many tokens the model can handle. This window must also be able to accommodate the generated response.

Let’s say a model supports a context window of 128,000 tokens. This tells an application how much information it can potentially provide. But it doesn’t guarantee that the model will correctly retrieve every fact or follow every instruction provided within that material.

This is similar to the difference between a system’s capacity and reliability. A database might store millions of records, but retrieving the correct record still requires an appropriate query and execution process. Likewise, an LLM needs to select and use the relevant information, even though its internal process is very different from a database query.

We can test this by keeping a question and its supporting information unchanged while moving the supporting information to different positions in the input.

For example, imagine a collection of project notes containing this fact: “The owner of Project Cedar is Adam.” The question is always, “Who owns Project Cedar?”

In the first test, the relevant note appears first within the input prompt. In another test, it appears halfway through the collection. In a third, the note appears at the very end. The other notes remain the same.

If accuracy changes substantially, the model is sensitive to where the evidence appears. A study named “Lost in the Middle”, released in 2023 and published in 2024, studied this behaviour and found that performance was often strongest near the beginning and end. However, performance in the middle was weaker. This produces a U-shaped accuracy curve. Here’s how it stacks up:

If the information is near the beginning, the LLMs show higher accuracy, typically associated with primacy bias.

If the information is near the end, the LLMs again show higher accuracy. But this is associated with recency bias.

Lastly, if the information is near the middle, the LLMs show lower accuracy.

Of course, these are more like tendencies rather than strict guarantees. We can get wrong answers even from beginning and ending positions. Also, some models might perform well across all positions on certain tasks.

Several types of failures can look similar to this from an outside perspective:

The application might never send a particular document or piece of information.

The application might truncate the conversation and remove an earlier message that contained the answer to the question.

The retrieval system might select the wrong passages.

Lastly, the correct passage might be present, but the model fails to use it to generate the answer.

Lost in the middle concerns this last situation. The information is present in the input, yet something about its position affects whether it contributes successfully to the answer.

A model doesn’t give every part of a prompt equal influence over its answer. Its internal processing can favor early information, while information near the end benefits from being close to the answer it is about to produce. The middle gets less of either advantage.

Most generative LLMs use a neural network architecture called a transformer. A central component of this architecture is attention.

Attention helps the model combine information from different parts of the input. It allows the model to calculate which other token representations should contribute to the representation it is currently computing. All of these contributions have different weights. Some receive more influence, while others receive less.

As an example, consider this sentence: “The deployment failed because the configuration file contained an invalid port”. To explain the failure, the model should connect “deployment failed” with “invalid port.” Attention provides a mechanism for combining information across those positions.

This happens repeatedly through multiple processing layers. Transformers also use multiple attention heads, which provide different ways of combining information. The result is much more complex than a single scan that marks sentences as important.

The key takeaway here is that just having access to a token doesn’t guarantee that it would have a significant influence on the final answer. Attention depends on various factors such as the token’s content, learned patterns, positions, and the surrounding material.

Causal masking creates an asymmetry between early and late positions.

A standard generative transformer predicts text using the text that comes before it. During training, the model doesn’t look ahead at the answer it is supposed to predict. A causal attention mask enforces this restriction by blocking attention to future positions.

Consider a prompt containing 6 tokens. Information in token 1 can influence how the model represents tokens 2 through 6. Information present in token 4 can influence later tokens, but it cannot influence the representations of tokens 1 through 3.

The model performs several layers of processing. Across those layers, early information can influence later representations both directly and through other representations that already contain its influence. This can give the beginning a structural advantage. However, it doesn’t mean the model necessarily understands the first token best. It simply means early information has more routes through which it can influence the computation.

The end of the prompt gets a different advantage. It is closer to the actual question and the answer. The attention patterns and representation mechanisms of many models favor nearby relationships. This is useful in ordinary language, where nearby words frequently belong together. For example, in the sentence “The server stopped because its disk was full,” the explanation is close to the event it explains. Models learn to make extensive use of such nearby information.

When a question comes after a long document, the final paragraphs are nearby, while the middle paragraphs are much farther away. Depending on the model, those nearby paragraphs can be easier to use.

However, there are two important qualifications to the simplified “token 1 accumulates massive attention” explanation:

First, permission to attend doesn’t determine the actual attention weight given to a token. The model doesn’t maintain one continuously accumulating importance score for each token.

Second, when generating an answer after the prompt, standard full causal attention can access all earlier prompt positions, including the middle.

Therefore, the middle is not directly hidden from the answer by the causal mask. The issue involves how information is represented and combined throughout the network.

Larger context windows are great at increasing the overall capacity of a model. But they don’t guarantee uniform reliability when it comes to making every position equally useful.

We can distinguish between the two in terms of maximum context size and effective context size for a task. The first concerns the overall supported input size. The second concerns how much context the application can actually use while maintaining acceptable performance.

Effective context size depends on the task. For example, the task of finding one distinctive identifier within some documents is different from comparing several documents, resolving contradictions, or tracking changes across a long conversation.

This is where the RULER benchmark becomes relevant. The original benchmark evaluated 17 models using tasks that went beyond simple retrieval, including following chains of information and aggregating results. It found widespread degradation as input length increased. However, its limitations explicitly state that it reported scores by input length without controlling for and reporting evidence position.

While it is difficult to completely remove the bias against middle information from a model, there are a few strategies that we can use to mitigate it.

Prompt organization helps make important material easier to use. A good starting point is to make the task, essential constraints, and final questions easy to identify for the model. We should not bury important information inside a large block of unrelated material.

For a coding task, a short opening statement might clearly mention a compatibility requirement. The relevant source files can follow, with a precise request at the end. For document question answering, putting the question after the documents is another useful arrangement to test.

Clear boundaries to mark different types of content also help. Markdown headings, document labels, and XML-style tags can distinguish instructions from source material and separate one document from another. However, these are practical starting points whose effectiveness should be checked on the chosen model.

For example, an application could assemble a prompt like this:

Here, the constraint comes from the supplied source. The documents have distinct identities, and the final request specifies what evidence the answer must use.

Adding these tags doesn’t change the model’s attention mask or guarantee compliance. Their main purpose is to reduce ambiguity about the organization of the input. Brief repetition can also help keep a crucial constraint visible. However, repeating the entire prompt several times adds length and can introduce contradictions if the copies diverge.

It is much more useful to reduce unnecessary context than enforcing an arbitrary token limit.

For example, model providers would often advise staying below a certain number of tokens, such as 20K tokens. This is more of an application-specific budget rather than a scientific boundary. There is no universal threshold at which 19,999 tokens are reliable and 20,001 become unreliable. A short prompt can fail if its instructions conflict. On the other hand, a much longer prompt can succeed when the evidence is clear, and the task is straightforward.

A better approach is to add the information needed for the task while removing material that adds little value. We should select relevant information and manage context as an application resource. Let’s say an AI assistant is investigating a database connection error. Supplying months of logs, every configuration file, and an entire deployment history creates considerable unnecessary material. A more focused input might contain the error, the relevant connection settings, the affected code, and recent changes.

As you can notice, the main difficulty is deciding what is unnecessary. If we remove an exception, dependency, or earlier decision, it will make the prompt shorter but also make it less accurate. Therefore, context selection needs to preserve the evidence required to answer the question while removing useless information.

The same care should be applied to long conversations. An application can maintain a concise record of current requirements and decisions, with references back to the original material. Summaries are useful, but they can omit details, so they should not become the only surviving source when exact information matters.

Retrieval can reduce the amount of searching the model must perform inside its prompt.

A retrieval system searches a larger collection and selects relevant material before the LLM answers. This approach is commonly called retrieval-augmented generation (RAG).

Consider an AI-based support application with thousands of documentation pages. For a question about webhook retries, the system could retrieve the retry policy, relevant configuration documentation, and applicable exceptions. The LLM then answers from that smaller collection.

Retrieval can use keyword search, database queries, semantic search, or combinations of these methods. The key change is that the application takes responsibility for finding likely evidence instead of always placing the entire collection in the prompt and hoping for the best.

To be clear, this approach also has its own problems. The retrieval search may still miss the relevant passage. A retrieved excerpt may omit a necessary exception. Retrieving too many passages may recreate the original problem.

For that reason, RAG doesn’t eliminate “lost in the middle”. The retrieved material still needs sensible selection, ordering, and evaluation.

The lost-in-the-middle problem reveals a blind spot for LLMs. Availability of information doesn’t guarantee that it will be used effectively. A fact can remain inside the context window and still contribute very little to the answer. What looks like forgetting is often a quirk of the attention mechanism to give relevant information enough influence during generation.

This happens partly because different positions can receive different advantages. Early information has more opportunities to influence later processing, while information near the end can benefit from its proximity to the question and the answer. Information in the middle does not benefit from both. However, the strength of this effect varies with the model, its training, and the task.

We can try to have a larger context window to expand capacity, but reliable use of that capacity still needs testing. Clear instructions, well-organized source material, and careful placement of essential details can help generate better results. Removing unnecessary context and retrieving relevant passages can also make the task easier, provided important details and exceptions are preserved. These techniques reduce the chances of overlooking information. But they don’t guarantee perfect answers.

One thing the Adam test can't settle: with the question in one fixed spot, "middle of the document" and "far from the question" are the same address. Move the question to the top and the curve might flip. The post's own two explanations — early tokens getting more routes through the layers, late ones sitting near the answer — point to different fixes: reorder the documents, or move the question.

The capacity vs reliability framing is the useful one. A 128K window is storage; attention is the query plan. One practical pattern that has helped for coding agents: put the critical constraint twice — once in a short header block and once as an explicit check in the final request — rather than relying on a single middle mention. Curious whether you've measured how much brief repetition helps vs just relocating the fact to the edges.

#35930. Blocco dei Tir, supermercati vuoti al Sud, ma è peggio del Covid

La mobilitazione degli autotrasportatori contro il caro carburante, che sta mettendo sotto pressione la distribuzione delle merci, merita più attenzione. La protesta non riguarda soltanto chi guida un camion o gestisce un’azienda di trasporti, ma chiunque faccia la spesa, lavori nella produzione alimentare o si occupi di ristorazione. Perché quando si interrompe il trasporto, il problema non è più soltanto di chi trasporta: diventa di chi produce, di chi vende e, infine, di chi mangia. Che trova i supermercati vuoti.

Non solo al Sud, in realtà: anche dal Veneto arrivano le prime avvisaglie.

Eppure il rischio è relegare la mobilitazione a una questione di categoria, una vertenza da seguire attraverso gli incontri con il Governo e le dichiarazioni delle associazioni di settore. Poi sono arrivati gli scaffali sguarniti, i reparti del fresco con disponibilità ridotte, le difficoltà nei rifornimenti di alcuni supermercati. E il problema è diventato improvvisamente visibile. Come se il lavoro necessario a far arrivare il cibo sulle nostre tavole esistesse soltanto nel momento in cui smette di funzionare.

Com’è possibile che chi lavora non riesca più, in alcuni casi, a sostenere economicamente il proprio lavoro?

Il caro carburante è il fattore che ha alimentato la protesta, ma il nodo è più profondo. Un’impresa di autotrasporto deve sostenere il costo del gasolio, la manutenzione dei mezzi, le assicurazioni, il personale, i pedaggi e gli altri oneri di gestione. Se i costi aumentano senza che le tariffe consentano di assorbirli, il margine si riduce fino a mettere in discussione la sostenibilità stessa dell’attività.

Non è normale che un lavoratore o un imprenditore debba scegliere tra partire rischiando di lavorare in perdita e fermarsi rinunciando ai propri guadagni. Non è normale che una funzione essenziale dell’economia venga considerata scontata finché qualcuno è disposto a sostenerne i costi. E non è normale che la fragilità di un’intera filiera diventi una questione pubblica soltanto quando produce conseguenze visibili per i consumatori.

Il punto non è sostenere che ogni richiesta di una categoria debba essere accolta senza discussione. È riconoscere che il prezzo di un servizio deve essere compatibile con i costi necessari a erogarlo. Altrimenti il mercato continua a pretendere efficienza da chi, economicamente, non è più nelle condizioni di garantirla. E i supermercati diventano scatole vuote.

La protesta degli autotrasportatori mette in evidenza una contraddizione che riguarda molti settori. Chiediamo a chi lavora di essere competitivo, puntuale e affidabile, ma spesso trattiamo le condizioni economiche come un problema secondario. Finché il camion parte, la consegna arriva e il prodotto è disponibile, il sistema sembra funzionare. Ma se per mantenerlo in movimento chi lavora deve assorbire costi insostenibili, quella normalità è soltanto apparente.

C’è poi una questione che in Italia non può essere separata da quella economica: la geografia dell’attenzione. La mobilitazione ha interessato diverse aree del Mezzogiorno, dalla Campania alla Puglia, dalla Calabria alla Sicilia. In alcune zone sono segnalate difficoltà nei rifornimenti, soprattutto per i prodotti freschi, che richiedono consegne frequenti e hanno tempi di conservazione limitati. Le conseguenze non sono uniformi, ma il disagio è concreto e riguarda territori nei quali la distribuzione alimentare dipende da collegamenti regolari con piattaforme logistiche, mercati e centri di lavorazione.

Eppure resta la sensazione di un Paese che non riesce a riconoscersi fino in fondo come un sistema comune. Il Mezzogiorno sembra dover arrivare all’emergenza per conquistare (forse) spazio nel dibattito nazionale. Le difficoltà economiche, le fragilità infrastrutturali e le conseguenze delle interruzioni logistiche diventano notizia quando producono immagini inequivocabili. Cioè un reparto vuoto, un mercato senza merce, una consegna che non arriva.

Il problema non è soltanto quanto spazio venga riservato alla protesta nei mezzi di informazione. È quanto le questioni che attraversano il Sud siano considerate questioni nazionali. Perché se una filiera alimentare si inceppa in Campania o in Puglia, non siamo davanti a un inconveniente circoscritto a quei territori. Siamo davanti a un sistema che mostra una vulnerabilità capace di propagarsi lungo tutta la catena di approvvigionamento fino ai supermercati vuoti.

Il Mezzogiorno non è una periferia economica che può essere ignorata finché il disagio resta confinato entro i suoi confini. È un territorio di produzione, trasformazione e distribuzione alimentare, con imprese e lavoratori che partecipano a pieno titolo all’economia nazionale. La protesta degli autotrasportatori dovrebbe ricordarcelo senza bisogno di aspettare che gli effetti arrivino altrove.

C’è un’altra contraddizione. Mentre in alcune aree del Sud i rifornimenti incontrano difficoltà e i supermercati sono vuoti, le segnalazioni descrivono una situazione meno compromessa al Nord. Nel frattempo, la grande distribuzione continua a basarsi su reti logistiche capaci di movimentare prodotti provenienti anche da Paesi molto lontani.

Non è una questione di contrapporre il prodotto italiano a quello straniero. Le importazioni sono parte integrante del sistema alimentare contemporaneo e rispondono a esigenze di mercato, stagionalità, disponibilità e prezzo. Il punto è: come può una filiera capace di movimentare merci da una parte all’altra del mondo diventare così vulnerabile quando si interrompono i collegamenti interni?

La domanda è rilevante quando riguarda frutta, verdura e altri prodotti freschi. Merce che attraversa confini e percorre migliaia di chilometri per raggiungere un punto vendita, mentre in un’altra parte dello stesso Paese il sistema di approvvigionamento rallenta o si blocca. Non significa che ogni prodotto importato continui ad arrivare regolarmente al Nord, né che le difficoltà del Sud dipendano soltanto dalla protesta. Significa, però, che la crisi invita a interrogarsi sulla resilienza delle reti distributive e sulle differenze territoriali nella capacità di assorbire uno shock logistico.

I vuoti sugli scaffali dei supermercati non dipendono soltanto da quanta merce viene prodotta. Dipende da come viene movimentata, da dove sono collocati i centri di distribuzione, dalla continuità dei collegamenti e dalla possibilità di sostituire rapidamente un percorso interrotto. È una questione economica, ma anche una questione di organizzazione del sistema alimentare.

Ed è qui che il confronto tra Nord e Sud smette di essere una contrapposizione geografica e diventa una domanda politica. Quanto è davvero integrato il mercato alimentare italiano, se una crisi che investe una parte del Paese può tradursi in difficoltà così evidenti nella disponibilità dei prodotti?

Nel dibattito gastronomico utilizziamo spesso parole come territorio, filiera corta, stagionalità, sostenibilità, valorizzazione delle produzioni locali. Rischiano di diventare formule vuote se non consideriamo le condizioni materiali che permettono ai prodotti di arrivare a destinazione.

Una filiera non è soltanto il campo in cui cresce un ortaggio, il caseificio che trasforma il latte, il laboratorio che produce pasta o il ristorante che lavora una materia prima. È anche il magazzino, il mezzo che trasporta la merce, la rete che collega produttori e distributori. È il lavoro di chi carica, guida, scarica e consegna. Senza questi passaggi, anche una produzione eccellente rischia di non raggiungere il proprio mercato. Infatti il Consorzio della Mozzarella di Bufala Dop ha annunciato il fermo della produzione. L’effetto non è solo nei supermercati con gli scaffali vuoti.

Per questo la sostenibilità alimentare deve comprendere anche la sostenibilità economica e sociale della filiera. Se il trasporto non è economicamente praticabile, l’intero sistema ne risente: il produttore rischia di non collocare la merce, il distributore di non rifornire i punti vendita, il consumatore di non trovare ciò che cerca.

Il problema riguarda anche il mondo della ristorazione. Un locale può costruire la propria identità sulla qualità delle materie prime, sulla stagionalità e sul rapporto con i produttori, ma non è immune dalle difficoltà della logistica. La disponibilità degli ingredienti, la regolarità delle consegne e i costi di approvvigionamento incidono sulla programmazione della cucina e sui margini dell’impresa. Il cibo che arriva al tavolo è il risultato di una catena di attività che raramente il cliente vede.

Parliamo molto di ciò che rende un prodotto buono, autentico o sostenibile. Molto meno delle condizioni che consentono a quel prodotto di arrivare sul mercato. La protesta degli autotrasportatori ci obbliga a riportare l’attenzione su questo passaggio.

Una mobilitazione non dovrebbe essere giudicata soltanto in base al rumore che produce o all’entità del disagio. Il fatto che una protesta sia civile non la rende meno importante; il fatto che non occupi ogni giorno le aperture dei telegiornali non significa che sia marginale. Al contrario, dovrebbe essere un’occasione per affrontare il problema prima che le conseguenze diventino ancora più pesanti.

Il l Ministero delle Infrastrutture e dei Trasporti ha annunciato risorse per il settore ma la tecnica sembra attendista. In Sicilia è stata annunciata una parziale revoca del blocco dei Tir, mentre in Campania, Puglia e Calabria la mobilitazione continua. Gli impegni istituzionali sono un passaggio importante, ma disponibilità di fondi ed effettiva soluzione dei problemi non sono la stessa cosa. Occorre capire se le misure saranno sufficienti a rendere sostenibili i costi e a ripristinare condizioni di lavoro compatibili con la continuità del servizio.

Il rischio è che si parli degli autotrasportatori soltanto per gli scaffali vuoti. E che il disagio dei consumatori venga considerato il problema principale, mentre le condizioni economiche di chi tiene in movimento la filiera restano sullo sfondo.

Gli scaffali vuoti sono l’effetto visibile di una crisi che comincia molto prima: nei costi che aumentano, nei margini che si assottigliano, nelle attività che non riescono più a sostenersi. Aspettare che il disagio raggiunga il supermercato ormai vuoto significa intervenire quando il problema è già diventato collettivo.

Il Sud non dovrebbe aver bisogno di scaffali vuoti per diventare una notizia nazionale. E chi trasporta il cibo non dovrebbe essere costretto a fermarsi perché ci accorgiamo del suo lavoro soltanto quando la merce non arriva più.

C’è la corsa a fare scorte alimentari: sembra di essere tornati ai tempi del Covid. Ma è peggio: il virus questa volta è sistemico.

Sembrava una nostra provocazione. Invece Coldiretti ha fatto un confronto proprio con l’emergenza Covid e ha chiesto all’Europa di mettere in campo un nuovo Pnrr straordinario per l’agricoltura, con risorse aggiuntive rispetto alla Pac. Fondi necessari per fronteggiare l’aumento dei costi di produzione, sostenere gli investimenti e garantire così la salute dei cittadini e la sopravvivenza delle imprese agricole. Secondo Coldiretti la crisi energetica e produttiva rischia di essere peggiore di quella causata dal Covid.

L’elaborazione del Centro Studi Divulga sostiene che se l’aumento medio dei prezzi dell’energia e degli input agricoli osservatonella prima metà del 2026 dovesse perdurare verrebbe a mancare dalle tavole una produzione di cereali pari a oltre 230 miliardi di pasti. Una cifra altissima che andrebbe a colpire soprattutto i Paesi più poveri.

Curiosa per natura, diffido dalle certezze assolute. Preferisco capire piuttosto che seguire, osservare piuttosto che giudicare. Non mi interessano le mode, mi interessano le idee. Osservo, faccio domande e cerco risposte. Credo che il senso critico sia il miglior ingrediente di ogni racconto. Se questo passa attraverso il cibo, tanto meglio.

#35937. Morelia festival wraps 24th edition; ‘Six Months in a Pink and Blue Building’ takes top honors

A childhood memory reshaped into fiction won big at the recently concluded Morelia International Film Festival, with “6 meses en el edificio rosa con azul” (“Six Months in a Pink and Blue Building”) taking both the judges’ top prize and the audience award.

The double win — the Ojo for best Mexican feature and the audience award in the same category — marked a meeting of jury and moviegoer tastes that the newspaper El Universal described as rare on the festival circuit.

The 104-minute Spanish-language drama is the first fiction feature by Bruno Santamaría Razo, a documentary filmmaker born in Mexico City in 1986.

Planned for a 2027 theatrical release in Mexico — with no streaming dates yet announced — the film follows an 11-year-old boy discovering his feelings for his best friend as his family confronts his father’s HIV diagnosis in 1990s Mexico City.

Reconstructing family memories through imagination rather than strictly retelling events, the film premiered at Cannes Critics’ Week, becoming the first Mexican feature to compete in the section in 20 years.

The 24th edition of the Morelia International Film Festival (FICM) was held in the Michoacán capital from Oct. 16 through Sunday.

For its 25th anniversary next year, FICM officials are promising a landmark edition with major guests, bold retrospectives and a stellar lineup — and it will move the dates to the week immediately before the Day of the Dead.

This year’s festival included 96 Mexican films in competition, alongside international screenings, retrospectives and tributes.

The guest list was strong, as usual, with appearances by directors Guillermo del Toro (“Pan’s Labyrinth”) and Alfonso Cuarón (“Roma”), along with “Y Tu Mamá También” stars Diego Luna and Gael García Bernal — underscoring Morelia’s standing as a gathering place for Mexican cinema.

International guests included U.S. director James Gray, who presented “Paper Tiger,” which competed for the Palme d’Or at Cannes this year. The winner at Cannes, “Fjord,” also played in the FICM.

Festival president and Cinépolis CEO Alejandro Ramírez described this year’s event as a particularly Mexican celebration.

Mexican Nicolás Pereda won best director for “Lo demás es ruido” (“Everything Else Is Noise”), an absurdist comedy about a musician preparing for her first television interview. Its star, Teresa Sánchez, also a Mexican, won best actress.

Sam Louwyck of Belgium took best actor for playing a gay hairdresser in Mexican Nathalia Acevedo’s “Salón de belleza” (“Beauty Salon”).

Mexican Joaquín del Paso’s “El jardín que soñamos” (“The Garden We Dreamed”), about a Haitian family traveling north, earned a special mention and the cinematography award.

Mexican Fernanda Tovar received the first-time director award for “Chicas tristes” (stylized as “Sad Girlz” in translation), about two teenage girls supporting each other after one is sexually abused. Pablo Vaca’s “Forensita” won best Mexican documentary feature.

With reports from El Universal, Variety, Animal MX and Proceso
