#35971. Mexico in 10 objects: Stone heads, silver dollars and tacos

In January 2010, Neil MacGregor of the British Museum started to broadcast his new series, “A History of the World in 100 Objects.” It was a magnificent success, and a bestselling book followed. Since then, there have been numerous other titles, from “The History of Football in 100 Objects” to “A History of Food in 101 Objects.”

The idea is such fun that MND asked contributor Bob Pateman to put together his very own “A History of Mexico in 10 Objects.”

The Olmecs, the Mexican mother culture, thrived in the lowlands of southeastern Mexico between 1500 and 400 B.C. San Lorenzo and La Venta became the biggest communities on the continent, the builders of palaces and pyramids.

The most striking of their artwork was the giant heads carved from boulders that had been brought from the distant Sierra de los Tuxtlas mountains. Mysterious and haunting, 17 of these heads have been discovered.

Item selected: Colossal Head 1 was the first to be discovered and is the largest, at 2.84 meters (9 feet 4 inches) and 25.3 metric tons. With its direct stare and elaborate headdress, it is also one of the most haunting.

Where it is: Colossal Head 1 can be seen in the Xalapa Museum of Anthropology in the city of Xalapa, Veracruz.

Pre-Hispanic Mexico was a mosaic of different societies that interacted through trade, politics and war. The Mexica flourished for little more than 200 years, but during that time they acquired previously unheard-of levels of wealth and power.

There is no lack of amazing objects to represent them, with gems ranging from feather headdresses to giant stone calendars.

Item selected: The giant statue of the earth and mother goddess Coatlicue. This was carved around A.D. 1500 at the height of Mexica power. It stands 2.5 meters tall and weighs between 2 and 3 metric tons. It is an interwoven collage of snakes, claws, skulls, and human hearts and hands. Threatening and powerful, Coatlicue was clearly a god you didn’t mess with!

Where it is: The National Museum of Anthropology in Mexico City.

See it for yourself: 16th-century encyclopedia on Mexica life made public online

Although the Mexica did not have an alphabetic writing system, they produced wonderful books that recorded their myths, economic records, history and laws in pictograms and glyphs. Written on bark, skin or cloth, they were targeted by the Spanish, and only a handful escaped the flames.

Item selected: Ironically, the Spanish, who tried so hard to burn all the Mexica “books,” produced one of the finest. Franciscan friar Bernardino de Sahagún worked with Nahua elders and artists to document the culture and history of the Mexica people. It was a mammoth work, 2,500 pages organized into 12 books, recording the story of an empire that was in the process of being destroyed.

Where it is: The Florentine Codex is housed in the Laurentian Library of Florence.

The Spanish colonies in the Americas became rich from their silver mines, and for 350 years Mexico led the way. Much of the raw metal was turned into coins known as the Spanish dollar or, later, the Spanish peso. The amount of silver in each coin was equal to eight Spanish reales, and for smaller purchases, a coin could be cut into bits, which is why pirates referred to them as pieces of eight.

Mexico sat at the center of an international trade network that stretched from China to the Ottoman Empire, and the Spanish dollar became the world’s first international currency.

Item selected: Produced in the millions, the coins can be acquired for as little as US $100. However, everything depends on rarity and the history of the coin, and the highest auction price is US $587,500 for a coin minted in Mexico in 1538. This was the first dollar-sized silver coin ever struck in the New World, and this particular example was salvaged from the underwater wreckage of the Golden Fleece, a vessel that sank in the northern Caribbean around 1550.

Where it is: Only three coins of this standard are known to exist, and all are in the possession of unknown private collectors.

On Sept. 16, 1810, the Roman Catholic priest Miguel Hidalgo y Costilla entered the parish church in Dolores and rang the bell to gather the villagers. This is seen as the symbolic start of an 11-year war that would bring Mexican independence from Spain.

Every year, the bell is rung on the night of Sept. 15 as the president of Mexico starts Mexico’s Independence Day celebration.

Item selected: The bell dates to 1768 and was cast in bronze in the small town of Dolores. It is 1.77 meters in height and weighs approximately 750 kilograms.

Where it is: Since 1896, the bell has hung above the central balcony of the Palacio Nacional.

Tequila can trace its roots back to the drink pulque. The Spanish brought in the technology to distill a clearer, more alcoholic version, which became known as mezcal.

This was displayed at the 1893 World’s Fair in Chicago, where it was labeled as “mezcal from the village of Tequila.” The name was reduced to simply tequila, and a legend was born.

Today, the tequila industry is worth around US $12 billion, and the drink is deeply interwoven into Mexican history and culture.

Item selected: The oldest known tequila bottle. Before 1880, tequila was sold in large wooden barrels to bars or to regional distributors, and you took it home in your own bottle or jug. A José Cuervo bottle dating to 1905 is the oldest verified surviving bottle of tequila.

Where it is: Displayed in The Tequila House in Parral, Chihuahua, as part of a collection of more than 3,000 vintage bottles.

Mariachi music traces its origins to Jalisco, emerging from a combination of Spanish strings and Mexican folk music with just a trace of African influence.

The first mariachi bands performed in the simple white cotton shirts, trousers and leather sandals of the farms, but during the early 1900s, musicians started to adopt the dandy dress of the Mexican cowboy. Costumes reached a new height in the 1940s when mariachi bands appeared in Mexican movies.

Item selected: The iconic mariachi costume is the black charro suit and tejana hat that Pedro Infante wore in the 1955 movie “Los Gavilanes.”

Where it is: A collection of authentic costumes, hats and cinematic memorabilia — including the famous mariachi outfit — is on display in the Museo El Rincón de Pedro Infante, in the actor’s hometown of Mazatlán.

Mexico has produced some wonderful artists, from the masterful landscapes of José María Velasco to the blending of styles and cultures in the work of Rufino Tamayo. However, nobody has captured the love of the country more than Frida Kahlo and Diego Rivera, and, as time goes by, it is Frida who is emerging as the real superstar of the partnership.

Her paintings have pride of place in art galleries around the world, and the auction record for one of her paintings stands at US $54.7 million for her 1940 self-portrait “The Dream (The Bed).”

Item selected: “Self-Portrait with Thorn Necklace and Hummingbird.” Painted in 1940, this is a complex work full of symbols. The thorn necklace is a reference to both Christ and her own pain. While the hummingbird is usually a symbol of love, this one is dead and still, a reflection of Frida’s own disastrous relationships. With its colors, animals and plants and the artist’s simple peasant dress, there is also something notably “Mexican” about the work.

Where it is: “Self-Portrait with Thorn Necklace and Hummingbird” is in the permanent collection of the Harry Ransom Center at the University of Texas at Austin.

The origins of the taco are debated. Some argue that they predate the arrival of the Spanish; others that the idea came across from Spain, perhaps with miners or even with Hernán Cortés himself.

Whatever the origins, there is nothing more Mexican than a small corn or wheat tortilla filled with a piping hot and juicy filling. It is the food that fuels the country every morning.

Item selected: What represents Mexican street food better than small, semi-mobile taco stands? It is not only a place to eat, but a spot to stand and chat.

Where it is: We are looking for a small stand with umbrellas, a few plastic chairs and a bright advertising sign. Shouldn’t be too hard to find!

A post shared by World Boxing Council (@wbcboxing)

Sport has a special place in Mexican society, from bullfights to lucha libre to the passion generated by big soccer games. Sport has also helped Mexico stake its place on the world stage, hosting the 1968 Olympics and three World Cup tournaments.

If there is one sport that is special to Mexicans, it is boxing, and for many, the greatest Mexican fighter of them all is Julio César Chávez. Over a 25-year career, he was a six-time world champion across three different weight classes. To get there, he had to go through many personal challenges, the kind of background that endears a fighter to Mexican fans.

Item selected: A world championship belt. When British fighter Tom Cribb defeated his American challenger, Tom Molineaux, in the early 1800s, King George III presented Cribb with the first championship belt in boxing history. There are no pictures of the gift, but it is said to have been made from lion skin with a silver buckle. This started a tradition that continues today, with boxers receiving a championship belt on winning a world title.

Where it is: Two of Chávez’s six belts are believed to have gone to private collectors. Four remain in his private collection.

Bob Pateman lived in Mexico for six years. He is a librarian and teacher with a Master’s Degree in History.

#35975. How Voting Is Like a Klein Bottle

Get the latest news delivered to your inbox.

An editorially independent publication supported by the Simons Foundation.

Create a reading list by clicking the Read Later icon next to the articles you wish to save.

Popular Searches

Click to tell Google to prioritize Quanta and see more of the best coverage of science and math in your search and AI results.

Mathematically speaking, democracy is next to impossible.

That may sound like the premise of a Silicon Valley billionaire’s six-hour podcast, but it’s actually a statement about a remarkably stubborn problem: How do you translate a group of individual preferences into a single choice?

In 1785, the French mathematician and philosopher Nicolas de Condorcet discovered that a majority can sometimes vote itself into a logical loop. In elections with three or more candidates, each voter might rank the candidates in a perfectly clear and consistent way — yet when all the votes are tallied, every candidate loses in a head-to-head contest against one of their rivals. Changing the rules only gets us so far: As the economist Kenneth Arrow later proved, any method for combining individual rankings into a collective order must sacrifice at least one seemingly reasonable democratic requirement.

Now three researchers have shown that Condorcet’s 240-year-old paradox has a shape, and it’s suitably peculiar: a Klein bottle, a surface with no distinct inside or outside. The result gives mathematicians a geometric way to explore why seemingly reasonable rules for collective decision-making can become impossible to reconcile.

When there are only two choices, the will of the people is easy to divine: Barring a tie, the candidate preferred by a majority wins. Add a third contender, however, and things get weird. Suddenly, even agreeing on what it means to win becomes a problem.

You might simply elect whoever receives the most votes, as in a typical American election. But suppose Alice receives 35% of the vote, Bob 34%, and Carol 31%. Alice wins. Yet if Carol’s supporters all prefer Bob to Alice, then 65% of the electorate would rather have Bob. By one perfectly reasonable definition of majority rule, the wrong candidate has won.

Before anyone reaches for a tinfoil hat, let’s be clear: Every ballot is counted correctly. The question is what should count as winning.

One solution is to ask voters to rank the candidates, then choose whoever would defeat every rival in a head-to-head contest. But consider the following scenario:

Every voter has a perfectly coherent ranking, yet together they have produced a result that runs in circles.

Nicolas de Condorcet, a French mathematician, philosopher, and politician, championed women’s rights and helped spark the abolitionist movement in France in the late 18th century. He died in prison during the French Revolution.

Lithograph by Joseph-Bordes – Musée Carnavalet, Paris

This is the paradox that Condorcet, a passionate believer in democracy, encountered in the late 18th century. As the secretary of the French Academy of Sciences, he’d been looking for ways to make sure the group was choosing new members fairly. (More enlightened than most, Condorcet would also question the democratic arithmetic of excluding half the population, explicitly defending women’s political rights in a 1790 essay. A few years later, during the Reign of Terror, the revolution he had supported put him in a prison cell; he was found dead two days after his arrest.)

Over the centuries, mathematicians, political scientists, and reformers have devised an astonishing variety of voting systems in hopes of breaking Condorcet’s impasse. Yet every method brings its own vulnerabilities. Change the system and you may well change the winner, even when the voters and their preferences remain exactly the same.

In 1951, the economist Kenneth Arrow showed how deep the problem ran. He sought a voting system that stayed consistent while meeting just a few simple democratic requirements. Voters should be free to rank the candidates in any order. No single voter should unilaterally dictate the outcome of an election. (Read: no kings.) If everyone prefers one option to another, the group’s ranking should reflect that preference. And the ranking of two candidates should depend only on how voters compare those two: Where voters place Carol on their ballots shouldn’t affect whether the group ranks Alice above Bob.

Each requirement sounds reasonable enough on its own. But, Arrow showed, no method can satisfy them all. “Quite often the will of the people doesn’t exist,” said Iain McLean of the University of Oxford.

Arrow’s impossibility theorem, as this result is now known, extended Condorcet’s paradox beyond majority rule, establishing a fundamental limit on how individual votes can be combined into a group decision. Different voting systems can change which of Arrow’s requirements fail, but no single system can ever preserve them all. “You have to make a choice as a system designer as to which of the conditions it’s most harmless to fail,” McLean said.

In 1980, to better understand this phenomenon, the mathematician and economist Graciela Chichilnisky turned to topology, an area of math in which shapes are considered equivalent if one can be stretched into another without tearing. In a footnote, she mused that Condorcet’s paradox in particular could be thought about as a topological problem.

Now mathematicians have proved her intuition true.

What, then, does a voting paradox look like?

To answer this question, the new work builds on a model that Yuliy Baryshnikov, now a professor at the University of Illinois, Urbana-Champaign, proposed in 1993. First, consider a single voter’s ballot in an election with three candidates. There are six possible ways to rank the options. Each one can be represented by a triangle:

Get Quanta Magazine delivered to your inbox

Each corner of this triangle represents one of the comparisons the ballot implies: Alice over Bob, Bob over Carol, and Alice over Carol.

A different ballot listing Alice first, Carol second, and Bob third agrees on two of those comparisons, since both ballots put Alice above her rivals. Their triangles therefore share two corners and can be joined along the edge connecting them.

Now join all six triangles (representing all possible ballots) along their matching edges. You get a strip:

The edges at its two ends also match. So bend the strip around and glue those edges together, matching the labels. You get a tube with an opening at each end:

Baryshnikov used this model to give a new topological proof of Arrow’s impossibility theorem.

But look at what happens at either end of the tube. Around one rim, the comparisons read Alice over Bob, Bob over Carol, and Carol over Alice. Around the other, the cycle runs in reverse.

We’re left with Condorcet’s paradoxical cycles — voting results that run in circles. Baryshnikov had ignored these rims. But 30 years later, in April 2025, Ori Livson, a doctoral student at the University of Sydney, reconsidered them. After all, sometimes such circular relationships are allowed, as in the rules of rock-paper-scissors. The trouble arises in situations like elections, when those relationships are expected to form a consistent ranking. Livson wanted a topological model that could distinguish cases where the cycles are allowed from cases where they aren’t.

Together with his adviser, Mikhail Prokopenko, and Prokopenko’s longtime colleague Siddharth Pritam of the Chennai Mathematical Institute, he set out to model the troublesome case.

Each cycle around the tube’s rims leads to the same impossible conclusion: Every candidate ranks above every other. In that sense, both rims are saying the same thing. And in topology, “once you realize two things are the same, you can glue them together,” Livson said.

Graduate student Ori Livson (center), his adviser Mikhail Prokopenko (right), and Siddharth Pritam showed that a famous centuries-old voting paradox has a geometric shape: a Klein bottle, a strange surface with no inside or outside.

Srishti Saloni; Georgia Callinan; Natalia Kishigami

If you bend the tube until its two openings meet, you get a doughnut-shaped surface called a torus. But this gluing also connects individual edges from each rim in pairings that represent incompatible relationships. An edge representing “Bob over Alice over Carol,” for instance, gets glued to an edge representing “Bob over Carol over Alice.” The researchers needed a way to join the rims without creating such contradictions.

The solution? A twist. Instead of matching one rim to the other point by point, in the same order, the twist allowed the researchers to reverse the order of preferences around one of the rims — making the cycles equivalent. “Gluing with the twist is the only valid option,” Livson said.

If you twist a strip of paper and join its edges, you get a surface with only one side, called a Möbius strip. But what happens when you twist a tube? You end up with something remarkable and strange: a Klein bottle.

Like a Möbius strip, a Klein bottle has only one side, with no distinct inside or outside. Unlike a Möbius strip, it has no edge. (If it looks as if this surface crosses itself, it does — in three dimensions. Mathematicians avoid this by constructing the Klein bottle in four dimensions, where it can close up without self-intersecting.)

The trio had their model. Just as each voter can supply a coherent ranking, and yet the electorate’s preferences refuse to fit into a single order, each small patch of a Klein bottle appears to have two sides, yet those sides cannot be distinguished consistently across the whole surface. In both cases, something that makes sense piece by piece breaks down when we try to put the pieces together.

For Livson, Pritam, and Prokopenko, that resemblance raises a larger question: Can topology help explain other fundamental limits on what mathematics can accomplish? Perhaps these different impossibilities, when given a shape, will turn out to share a deeper geometric explanation.

Topology makes the paradox tangible, but choosing a voting system still means picking your poison. Mathematics can tell us what the trade-offs entail. Our priorities determine which ones are worth making. And that’s something no theorem can settle for us. “It’s not really a mathematical debate,” McLean said. “It’s a debate about values.”

#35981. Kane prematurely announced as Ballon d'Or winner - but he wasn't only one

Harry Kane has become the first Englishman since 2001 to win the Ballon d'Or

Harry Kane was crowned winner of the men's Ballon d'Or on Monday night, but a few hours earlier that confirmation appeared to have been announced prematurely.

At 17:15 GMT, an article from the award's official website titled 'Harry Kane wins the 2026 Ballon d'Or' began to circulate online.

The widespread sharing of the article caused confusion online, with some wondering whether France Football - the organisers of the award - had fallen short at the final hurdle of keeping the winner, who was voted for by journalists at the end of September, under wraps.

That was until further digging on the Ballon d'Or website revealed that some of the other favourites to win the award - including Barcelona's Lamine Yamal, Real Madrid's Kylian Mbappe - also had similar pieces penned and published about them.

When asked about the articles by BBC Sport, a Ballon d'Or spokesperson said: "Some profiles of nominees prepared by BallonDor.com were inadvertently published online.

"These articles had been prepared for all nominees and should in no way be considered as an indication of the final results or rankings."

By 17:40, the articles about Kane and Mbappe had been removed from the website.

Similar articles were also written about London City Lionesses star Alexia Putellas and Barcelona's Ewa Pajor. Putellas went on to be officially confirmed as the winner of the women's Ballon d'Or.

That premature announcement may have sparked some excitement in Kane that this could be his year, but he only had to wait a little longer to find out for sure.

#35982. I tried Bobby Flay's trick for perfect scrambled eggs, but found a cheaper way to get similar results

Scrambled eggs can be a difficult breakfast staple to master, but according to celebrity chef Bobby Flay, you can avoid a rubbery or dry texture by using crème fraîche, a dairy product similar to sour cream.

Since I don't often buy crème fraîche, however, I tried Flay's recipe alongside two two cheaper, similar alternatives — sour cream and Greek yogurt — to see if they would yield similar results. Here's how it went.

I began with testing out sour cream, which isn't as rich as crème fraîche but offers a similar tangy flavor and texture.

Following the basic steps of Flay's recipe, I put a pat of cold butter and sour cream in a pan. I was careful not to use too much since I wanted enough to moisten the eggs without making them too wet.

Then, I turned the burner to medium-low heat and added two whisked eggs. I constantly stirred the mixture with a rubber spatula, adding black pepper once the eggs began to hold their shape.

I turned the heat off shortly before the eggs looked done to keep the moisture and prevent overcooking, and added the salt. I let the eggs cook for about a minute in the hot pan with the gas burner turned off.

The eggs definitely looked smoother than they usually do when I make them, and they smelled buttery and even slightly cheesy.

When I took a bite, they tasted good, too — a little buttery and no tang from the sour cream.

However, despite my constant stirring, I thought they had an uneven texture, as a few bites were slightly rubbery and dry, or a little too wet for my taste.

Every time Paige publishes a story, you'll get an alert straight to your inbox!

Stay connected to Paige and get more of their work as it publishes.

Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.

This ingredient is the cheapest of the three options I tried so, even if the eggs weren't the best, I'd still add a little sour cream if I had it on hand and wanted scrambled eggs.

Next, I followed the same recipe and steps using a similar amount of Greek yogurt, which blended into the eggs seamlessly while cooking.

I knew after the first bite that this ingredient was not a good alternative to Flay's recommended crème fraîche.

The dish looked creamier than the eggs made with sour cream, but the non-fat Greek yogurt created a uniform rubbery texture.

I think one issue is that I accidentally used a yogurt without fat. Because Greek yogurt is so tangy, even more so than sour cream, I think the flavor masked the taste of the butter, salt, and eggs, and resulted in an overall blandness.

The dish also was slightly watery and left a small pool of liquid on my plate.

The crème fraîche had the thickest texture of the three, so I expected the eggs to be pretty rich.

I followed the same steps, noticing how effortlessly the crème fraîche melted into the whisked eggs.

This dish with crème fraîche took slightly longer to cook, but the eggs seemed moist and fluffy without looking underdone.

Out of the three recipes, this one resulted in the best flavor and definitely didn't feel undercooked.

When I took a bite, I thought the flavor was amazing. They tasted soft, rich, and buttery and nearly melted in my mouth.

The creamy eggs would work so well with salty, crispy hash browns, roasted veggies, or a really good crusty bread.

Though I thought the eggs made with crème fraîche tasted the best, I would use the cheaper alternative, sour cream, if I tried Flay's recipe again.

I don't often have crème fraîche on hand, so it would also be easier to use the sour cream since I usually buy it anyway.

That said, if I wanted a more indulgent version of this breakfast staple, I'd totally add crème fraîche to my grocery list.

This story was originally published on March 5, 2022, and most recently updated on October 26, 2026.

#35983. Why is Wilcox in line of fire from Man Utd fans?

Jason Wilcox (fourth from left) watched United's 3-0 defeat by Bournemouth alongside chief executive Omar Berrada

As the clock ticked down during Manchester United's 3-0 defeat by Bournemouth at Old Trafford on Sunday, the home fans made it clear what they thought about director of football Jason Wilcox.

Singing his name along with an obscenity, the message was brutal and to the point. It was sung by a majority of the United supporters, and on more than one occasion.

It came less than two weeks after United defender Lisandro Martinez had launched a blistering attack on the club's hierarchy for not strengthening the squad, with a particular message for Wilcox.

"I expect more from our technical director," said Martinez.

Pressure is growing on United boss Michael Carrick after eight games without a win - their worst run since 2015-16 - and two three-goal defeats in a week.

Howe and Nagelsmann high on Man Utd's list if Carrick sacked

End approaching for Carrick but Man Utd fans make clear who they blame

Wilcox is a former England winger who won the Premier League with Blackburn in 1994-95.

He joined United in April 2024 - initially as technical director after 15 months in a similar role at Manchester City, where he had previously been in charge of the academy.

At City he worked with current United chief executive Omar Berrada and also Stephen Torpey, who was appointed United's head of academy in August 2025.

When he arrived, it was said by United officials that Wilcox would work across all technical areas of the club to achieve "highest standards of performance" and would be answerable to the sporting director.

That changed the following December, when that sporting director - Dan Ashworth - was sacked amid a difference of opinion over who should replace Erik ten Hag as manager.

Ten months on, a poor series of results and dissatisfaction with transfer business has put Wilcox in the spotlight.

There are some who have sympathy with the position Wilcox finds himself in. The 55-year-old is not in charge of transfer budgets, which are set at a higher level.

Wilcox was also - obviously - not responsible for the injury Manuel Ugarte sustained playing for Uruguay at the World Cup, which wrecked a plan to sell the midfielder in the summer to bolster the transfer budget.

He, too, was powerless to prevent Barcelona backing out of a Â£26m deal to sign England forward Marcus Rashford.

Both issues affected the amount United could spend in the summer - both on transfer fees and wages.

Wilcox rarely speaks to the media, although in England that is not uncommon when managers do the vast majority of news conferences.

However a general lack of communication means we don't know why United decided to focus exclusively on strengthening their midfield in the summer, rather than bring in competition at left-back for Luke Shaw and striking support for Benjamin Sesko.

With both players injured, that looks like a major error.

The general consensus is Carrick has been sold badly short, and Wilcox's job title - and the fact he is often photographed with United's new signings and quoted in official press releases - means he is being blamed.

Wilcox was pictured with Â£70m signing Carlos Baleba when he joined in August

MNC: No smiles for the cameras at Manchester United

Since arriving at United from Southampton, Wilcox has twice been given very public backing by his bosses.

On the first occasion - around Ruben Amorim's recruitment as successor to Ten Hag - it was Ashworth who lost his job while Wilcox kept his.

Thirteen months later, when Amorim railed publicly against interference around United's formation - identifying Wilcox as one of those responsible - the Portuguese coach went, and Wilcox remained.

Minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe is yet to offer his thoughts on the present crisis engulfing Old Trafford, but the United fans delivered their verdict loud and clear during the match against Bournemouth.

There are many reasons why United find themselves 16th in the Premier League and without a win in their past eight matches, but recruitment is one of the bigger ones - and for that, rightly or wrongly, Wilcox is getting the blame.

Since joining United, Wilcox has given one extensive interview - to the in-house Inside United podcast in October 2025.

A month before that, he spoke at the 40th anniversary gala dinner of United's past players' association.

There have been a couple of instances in the past 12 months of him stopping for the briefest of chats with journalists when he walked past an area they were waiting in to speak to a United player after a game.

After the defeat by Bournemouth, Carrick was asked directly if Wilcox and Berrada should speak directly to supporters.

"It's not for me to say who speaks and who doesn't speak, when they speak, and how they speak," said Carrick.

"I know my role here is to speak to you guys and represent the team and football club as best as I possibly can from this position."

There has never been an opportunity to ask Wilcox about the parameters of his role.

But with Carrick's situation as it is - his popularity intact and his status as a legendary figure at the club secure - the focus is moving to Wilcox.

United fans have made it clear they want him out - but does Ratcliffe feel the same?

#35984. Michael Carrick’s future: Analysing possible candidates if Man United choose to replace him

Manchester United could well be in the market for an eighth permanent manager since Sir Alex Ferguson retired in 2013

#35985. Amy Duggar Says Cousin Josh Should 'Rot' in Prison After Appeal Attempts

Amy Duggar appears to have had enough of cousin Josh Duggar’s multiple attempts to appeal his child pornography conviction and subsequent prison sentence.

“Give it a rest already,” Amy, 40, shared on the Monday, October 26, episode of the “Fire Chats: The Mindfire Pod,” hosted by Carrie Berk. “Here’s what I think: Narcissistic people don’t like to be wrong. They don’t like someone questioning their character and making them look like they’re this horrible person, and they don’t think they are. This is all what narcissistic people do. This is textbook, exactly the way a narcissist handles the situation.”

Josh, 38, was convicted of receiving and possessing child sexual abuse materia in December 2021. He was later ordered to serve 12 years behind bars.

Josh has maintained his innocence despite his conviction, alleging there were “errors in the admission of forensic evidence” at his trial. As a result, he continues to file appeals with his legal team.

“I find it comical at this stage of his sentence. Just rot in there,” Amy continued of Josh’s many — and unsuccessful — appeals. “Will you just stay in there? What do you think is going to happen when you get out? What does he think? Does he think he’s going to go back to normal life and have a job? The whole thing is a train wreck that I can’t look away from.”

When asked if she believes Josh will be released from prison earlier than expected, Amy admits she finds the odds to be unlikely.

“There’s no way he needs to be out,” she said. “There’s no way there needs to be any kind of freedom for that man. No shot.”

Josh is tentatively scheduled to leave prison in  February 2033. His release date, however, has been pushed back on multiple occasions.

In June, Josh and his legal team filed another appeal after a judge denied his motion to vacate his conviction.

While his cousin is less than enthused about his many legal filings, Josh continues to have the support of his wife, Anna Duggar, while he remains incarcerated. The couple share seven children together, with Anna, 38, raising all of the children back home in Arkansas.

Recalling the family’s highly-publicized legal  drama, Amy appears to have no regrets that she has distanced herself from a majority of her relatives.

“It’s a circus. These are not my monkeys. It’s horrible,” she claimed. “I’m related to them. Yes, we’re related by blood, but they don’t know my life. They don’t know me at all.”

If you or someone you know is experiencing child abuse, call or text Child Help Hotline at 1-800-422-4453. If you or someone you know has been sexually assaulted, contact the National Sexual Assault Hotline at 1-800-656-HOPE (4673).

#35986. Watch the goals that won Kane the Ballon d'Or

#35987. From forgettable EFL loans to Ballon d'Or winner - Kane's 'Cinderella story'

Harry Kane wins 2026 Ballon d'Or to become first Englishman to land award in 25 years; Bayern Munich and England captain's Ballon d'Or victory called 'Cinderella story' by Tim Sherwood; watch Bayern vs Borussia Dortmund on Saturday, live on Sky Sports, kick-off 5.30pm

Football Journalist @declanolley

From forgettable EFL loan spells to being crowned the best player in the world, it is no wonder Harry Kane’s former boss thinks of his Ballon d'Or win as a fairy tale.

"This is a Cinderella story we've never seen before," ex-Tottenham boss Tim Sherwood, who gave Kane his full Premier League debut, tells Sky Sports.

"He keeps breaking records, and I keep thinking, 'Are we talking about the same kid?'

"There was always a chance, because he felt like there was no ceiling - he would always just break through and go further.

"I mean, not even Harry would say he could believe it, I would suggest."

The Bayern Munich striker has become the first English Ballon d'Or winner in 25 years and just the fifth in its history.

The last English winner, Michael Owen, had a simpler route to the top, winning it aged 22 in 2001, just four years after his senior debut at Liverpool.

Kane's win has come at the age of 33, some 15 years after his senior debut on loan at Leyton Orient in League One.

"I think we have to highlight his journey," says Sherwood, who Kane thanked on stage when collecting the Ballon d'Or.

"Even when we go back to Michael (Owen), he was a superstar as a kid, and he got the opportunities at a very young age at Liverpool, which is a giant football club.

"Kane came through the hard way, really. He was a player who wasn't universally loved.

"He's not like a Max Dowman, for instance, who is a superstar at 16 years of age.

"He (Kane) struggled to get some game time and to get a manager to really, really buy into what he had."

Kane even mentioned his tough path to the top in his Ballon d'Or acceptance speech.

He said: "My journey wasn't straightforward; I didn't go straight to the top, I had to experience a lot of loan moves and fight my way to get to where I am now."

For Sherwood, it was those low-key loan spells at Orient, Millwall, Norwich and Leicester that have turned Kane into the world's top player.

"I hear a lot of people talking about failed loans," he says, "but we never felt like that because we felt that him playing in U21 football was a waste of time.

"They were really good loans, and those loans were the reason why we've got a player like that."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Kane was out in the cold at Spurs until Andre Villas-Boas' sacking, when Sherwood's subsequent arrival opened the door.

He handed Kane his first-ever Premier League start - and the then-20-year-old took his chance as he scored the opener in a 5-1 home win over Sunderland in April 2014.

"He wasn't getting anywhere unfortunately," Sherwood says. "He wasn't around the first-team squad, so I suppose it's a sliding doors moment that I managed to take over until the end of the season and I managed to give him a stage where he could perform."

Kane has hit nearly 550 goals for club and country to become Tottenham's record scorer, England's all-time scorer, England's leading World Cup scorer and the top English scorer in Champions League history.

His incredible goalscoring feats hit new heights last season as he plundered 73 goals in all competitions.

Sixty-one of those came for Bayern in all competitions, 19 more than any other player in Europe's big-five leagues last season. In fact, it was the most by a player in a season since Cristiano Ronaldo scored 61 for Real Madrid in 2014/15.

For Sky Sports' Jamie Carragher, Kane's £100m move from Spurs to Bayern in the summer of 2023 has allowed him global recognition and respect.

"It's a move he needed to make," Carragher tells Sky Sports.

"I thought he might be obsessed with the Premier League goalscoring record, but he's obsessed with goalscoring records, not just that one.

"But it also gives him a great chance of winning the Champions League and being involved in Ballon d'Or selections.

"There's no way Harry Kane could've won the Ballon d'Or if he was playing for Tottenham."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

The Bayern move has delivered the trophies Kane so desperately craved.

Despite winning three Premier League Golden Boots at Spurs and becoming their all-time top scorer, personal honours were never the aim of the game for Kane, according to Sherwood.

His trophyless career at N17 is what drove Kane to join Bayern, where he has won back-to-back Bundesliga titles and a German Cup.

"The boy loves Tottenham," Sherwood says. "He wanted to win there, but he was winning individual accolades at Spurs, and it wasn't enough for him.

"When we all grow up, we all want to be footballers, and we don't want to be footballers because we want to be rich. There are possibly some players like that now, but that certainly wasn't his motivation.

"He wanted to go to a team that could win, and he picked Bayern, and we have to say it's been a good choice."

Sherwood adds: "He's a 100 per cent team man. If I told you he would rather win the Champions League and the Bundesliga title than the Ballon d'Or for himself, that would be the truth.

"Not every player would say that, but I genuinely believe that that is the measure of the man and that's what you're talking about, and that's why he's such a dream to manage.

"The work rate and the example he shows to any young kids coming through, and to genuinely go around the houses to try and make it, and he certainly made it."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Kane continues to break record after record in Germany.

He is the fastest player to 100 Bundesliga goals, has the most goals in his first 100 Bundesliga appearances and is the fastest player to reach 100 Bundesliga goal contributions.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

It is that last stat where Kane has proven he is more than just a goal scorer. His excellent vision, awareness and superb passing range make him an exquisite playmaker.

This was on show recently in Bayern's 2-2 Champions League home draw against Arsenal as Kane, who spent much of the game dropping deep, sumptuously set up Alphonso Davies for an equaliser with a pinpoint ball from inside the centre circle.

His assist ability has been on display for years, where at Spurs he claimed the Premier League Playmaker of the Season award in the 2020/21 campaign.

So, does his all-round game combined with his records and this Ballon d'Or prize make him England's greatest-ever player? Not for Carragher, who puts a fellow English Ballon d'Or winner ahead of him.

"I would still always put Bobby Charlton as the greatest England player ever," he says.

"He's won the World Cup. He's won the Champions League with Manchester United. As a midfield player, he was the leading goalscorer for a very long time for both United and England.

"I'd still always say Bobby Charlton, because right now this England team, even though it's been a golden period for us, Harry Kane and the others still haven't got their hands on a trophy, and that's what Bobby Charlton did."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Kane, who needs just one cap to become England's most-capped player, and although there are plenty of trophies to be won, Sherwood believes there is a longer-term goal in Kane's thinking.

"I believe he will come back for that individual accolade to be the Premier League top scorer in the future, maybe in a couple of years' time," the ex-Premier League-winning captain says.

Kane is 47 goals behind Alan Shearer's all-time Premier League record of 260 goals.

"He will only come back to a team he believes can win," says Sherwood. "At the moment you're looking at Man City with the troubles they've got, so Liverpool possibly, that could be a chance for him.

"Tottenham could be in the frame if they start progressing. Manchester United could be in the frame, but they've got a long way to go to prove that they can win anything as well.

"I'm not sure it's ever going to be Chelsea or Arsenal because of the Tottenham connection.

"So, it's on a positive note for Tottenham fans if they can start progressing and you can prove to him that he can come back and he can possibly have a good chance of winning at your club as he will have at Liverpool, Man Utd and Man City - but we don't know what's going to happen with them - then there's an opportunity he might come back and see out his career where he started it."

It seems a fitting final chapter in Kane's Cinderella story.

#35988. Malaria Parasite Caught in the Act of Breaking and Entering

Get the latest news delivered to your inbox.

An editorially independent publication supported by the Simons Foundation.

Create a reading list by clicking the Read Later icon next to the articles you wish to save.

Popular Searches

Click to tell Google to prioritize Quanta and see more of the best coverage of science and math in your search and AI results.

Plasmodium, the parasite that causes malaria, invades and proliferates inside red blood cells. Researchers imaged the proteins (dark purple ovals) it places on the cell membrane that let it slip through unnoticed.

In medieval Italy, it was accepted wisdom that anyone walking near a swamp risked falling ill from “bad air” — mala aria. Victims were struck with fever, headache, nausea, and chills, and occasionally the illness escalated to vomiting, jaundice, and death. Today, we still call this affliction malaria. For much of human history, it has ravaged populations in humid tropical and subtropical regions, killing untold numbers. In the 20th century, it was responsible for up to 5% of deaths worldwide; it still kills approximately 600,000 people each year.

What malaria was and how it infected humans was deeply mysterious until barely 150 years ago. In 1880, the French physician Charles Louis Alphonse Laveran peered at a sample of a soldier’s blood through a crude microscope and discovered the true cause of the disease: not miasma, but a genus of shape-shifting single-celled protists called Plasmodium. Since then, scientists and doctors have described in precise detail the parasites’ life cycle, hosts, and disease progression, and even the nuances of various Plasmodium species, which present slightly different forms of the disease. But some crucial aspects have remained a mystery.

Chi-Min Ho is a microbiologist and structural biologist mapping the proteins that malaria parasites use to invade our red blood cells.

For example, Plasmodium invades human red blood cells as part of its life cycle. To get in, the microbe creates a ring-shaped structure called the moving junction, which briefly creates a gap in the cell membrane it can slip through. The gap then disappears without a trace. It opens and closes so rapidly, at such a small scale, that imaging technologies couldn’t capture it. Researchers had no clue what the portal structure actually looked like. “Because of that,” said Chi-Min Ho, a microbiologist and structural biologist at Columbia University, “we didn’t really know anything about how it works.”

Now, Ho’s lab has imaged the moving junction right at the moment of invasion, in unprecedented detail. Her team found that when the Plasmodium parasite installs molecular gaskets in the host cell, it radically remodels the cell membrane to slip through without a trace. This knowledge could help inform the next generation of malaria treatments and influence the drug-discovery process for other parasitic diseases, such as cyclosporiasis and toxoplasmosis.

“It’s a totally mind-blowing paper,” said Jake Baum, a parasitologist at the University of New South Wales, who was not involved in the research. “At the end of the day, this is not molecular biology. This is biophysics.”

The history of malaria research has paralleled advances in microscopy, from the development of simple light microscopes like the one Laveran used to the cutting-edge cryo-electron techniques of Ho’s lab. This is because the cells the microbes invade are extremely tiny — and the parasites are even smaller. At about 8 micrometers across, red blood cells are among the smallest cells in the human body, and all species of Plasmodium are a fraction of that size during the invasion stage.

Attempts to image the parasites in the act of breaking and entering got off the ground in the 1970s, when pioneering microbiologists first applied electron microscopy. These scientists observed a distinct thickening of the cellular membrane near the parasite’s access point. Then the tiny, round Plasmodium seemed to simply merge with the cell, like a water droplet coalescing into a larger pool. It would be decades before techniques developed enough to isolate the proteins that make this possible, much less image them.

Get Quanta Magazine delivered to your inbox

James Dvorak’s lab at the National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases took the first microscopic videos of Plasmodium parasites entering a red blood cell. This one was filmed in 1978. Arrows point to parasite entry points.

Another hurdle is that malaria parasites have a very complicated life cycle. They are most at home in the gut of a mosquito where they can reproduce sexually. Humans are what’s known as a reservoir host — a place for the parasite to live while it waits to be taken up by an insect.

“The food supply happens to be our red blood cells,” said Kirk Deitsch, a microbiologist and immunologist at Weill Cornell Medicine in New York. “That’s why they’re invading us.”

To this end, the Plasmodium parasite assumes a variety of shapes and sizes as it moves through its life stages: Sometimes it’s shaped like an egg, sometimes a spindly thread, sometimes a gangly starfish. It can move through this cycle in rapid succession or hang out in one stage (the hypnozoite phase in the human liver) for years. When it enters its tiny, round merozoite stage, the parasite is ready to invade a red blood cell.

If you’re trying to push an orange into a balloon, the balloon mustn’t pop, right?

Plasmodium pulls up to the scene of the crime with all the proteins and other biological equipment it needs. The entry procedure is more delicate than simply punching a hole in the host cell. “If you’re trying to push an orange into a balloon, the balloon mustn’t pop, right?” Baum said.

Based on previous research, scientists knew the broad strokes of this process. The parasite approaches a host red blood cell and inserts a ring of protein anchors into the cell’s membrane. Collectively these anchors are known as the moving junction. Plasmodium locks into them with proteins expressed on its surface — and then pushes forward. The moving junction forms a continuous portal in the host cell membrane for the parasite to travel through. Once the parasite is fully inside, the cell membrane seals up behind it. The whole process takes less than a minute.

Ho and the other authors of the new paper used cryo-electron microscopy to probe the four individual protein units in each anchor. What they learned allowed them to propose a more detailed model for how the moving junction actively remodels the host cell’s membrane. The parasite injects into the red blood cell three proteins, which form a dense wedge beneath the membrane surface; a fourth protein connects this structure to the invader. Plasmodium places many of these anchors on the red blood cell surface. The proteins produce electrostatic forces and pressure in the cell membrane, which crowd and displace its lipid molecules. This deforms the membrane and reverses its curvature. It bows slightly inward around the parasite — in fact, some lipids slough off and reassemble into a protective membrane for the invader — before re-forming and sealing behind it.

“It’s a really nice example of how a field has built step by step by step toward a really powerful observation,” Baum said.

A visualization of four proteins in the moving junction anchor complex. The electrostatic properties of these proteins destabilize the membrane’s lipid bilayer and deform it, letting the parasite slip through.

Once inside, Plasmodium approaches its remodeling work more comprehensively. Red blood cells are essentially bags of hemoglobin; they have no nucleus or organelles to speak of. So Plasmodium builds from the ground up. It secretes proteins that allow it to feed on hemoglobin, remove waste, and stretch the cell’s cytoplasm. Ho compared it to the conversion of an empty warehouse floor into a stylish lofted apartment.

“It’s putting in plumbing,” she said. “It’s putting in the floors. It’s literally building everything for itself.”

In this process, the parasite also redecorates the cell’s exterior. It is equipped with genes that allow it to make a series of distinct but closely related adhesive proteins that it can express on the red blood cell’s surface. These proteins cause the cells to stick to the walls of the blood vessels around them — one of the main causes of malaria’s painful symptoms. In 2025, research from Deitsch’s lab found that by periodically switching between proteins, the parasite can avoid tipping off the immune system to its presence for days or weeks at a time. Such extensive exterior cellular remodeling serves a purpose: It helps Plasmodium avoid a trip to the spleen, an organ that filters out dead, defective, or broken red blood cells as well as any undercover parasites.

Protrusions called knobs cover the surface of an infected red blood cell. Plasmodium, the parasite that causes malaria, expresses these proteins after it invades the cell. They make cells clump to the walls of blood vessels, causing the disease’s most dangerous symptoms including organ distress, seizures, and coma.

Rick Fairhurst and Jordan Zuspann, National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases, National Institutes of Health

The parasite itself undergoes a radical change inside the cell. It transforms from a roundish merozoite into an amoebalike trophozoite. It grows several cytoplasm “arms” and flattens out, said Christiaan van Ooij, a molecular parasitologist at Keele University in the United Kingdom. This also helps it evade the immune system. The trophozoite shape is more flexible than that of the spherical merozoite, and the immune system clocks supple cells as healthy and stiff cells as dead.

The parasite then replicates asexually for about 48 hours, dividing inside the red blood cell until it’s so full of Plasmodium that it bursts. Like house flippers, the parasites move on to other blood cells. Within days, millions could be lurking in the bloodstream.

In this way, malaria can quickly overwhelm a human host. It is all enabled by the moving junction that allows the parasites to set up shop without tipping off the authorities. The structure of the moving junction could help researchers intervene, with drugs to block the parasite from invading a host blood cell in the first place.

Malaria treatment has long been something of a hit-or-miss proposition. Without a granular view of how Plasmodium interacts with host cells, researchers had to test various compounds by trial and error. If a given drug seemed to fight the illness, they had little idea what part of the infection process it had disrupted. “All that work was done essentially blind,” Deitsch said.

This has made developing new malaria treatments notoriously difficult. The former workhorse antimalarial drugs, such as chloroquine, sulfadoxine, and pyrimethamine, are now known to target the parasites in the bloodstream. But the Plasmodium species that infect humans are rapidly evolving resistance to these compounds.

Invasion offers a tantalizing choke point for new medicines. “If you block invasion, then you stop the parasite in its tracks,” Ho said. To make her point, her team used an AI-powered protein-design platform to craft an inhibitory protein that could block the moving junction. In test tubes, it prevented Plasmodium from entering red blood cells. But its safety and efficacy haven’t been tested in animal models.

It’s possible that the moving junction could be something of a skeleton key that could be used to treat other parasitic diseases. Plasmodium is a member of the apicomplexans, a large phylum of parasitic protists that also includes Toxoplasma gondii, a cat parasite, and Cyclospora, which causes explosive diarrhea. These single-celled organisms appear to enter using the same biochemical equipment as Plasmodium. “All of these are parasites that have this invasion machinery,” Deitsch said. The study of those conditions could in turn inform malaria treatment. A 2026 paper described the mechanism by which Toxoplasma senses the right moment to deploy its moving junction, and malaria may operate the same way.

But the moving junction may prove to be too much of a moving target. “Some people have always argued that invasion is a really bad drug target because it’s a very short process,” van Ooij said. Any drug with the ability to stop invasion would have a roughly 60-second window in which to do so, which means it would need to remain stable in the body for a long time or act extremely quickly, or both.

Ho’s team already has their sights set on another target. They plan to use similar cryo-electron microscopy techniques to solve the structure of other protein complexes involved in malaria infection, such as those adhesive proteins that keep infected blood cells from journeying to the spleen.

“We’ve caught one white whale,” Ho said. “So we want to go after the other remaining white whales.”

#35991. England-Republic of Ireland U15 World Cup game ends over alleged racism

England players gathered at the side of the pitch after the alleged incident

England walked off the pitch during a match at the Under-15 World Cup after an allegation of racism by a Republic of Ireland player, which resulted in the game being abandoned.

Following an altercation between players from both sides, shortly before the end of the match in Azerbaijan on Monday, an England player approached his bench and made a complaint to coaching staff.

Match footage then showed England staff members relaying the complaint to the referee, alleging a racist slur had been said, and calling their players off.

The Republic of Ireland were leading 4-1 at the time of the incident.

World governing body Fifa told BBC Sport it is investigating and gathering reports.

"Fifa is aware of an incident at the U-15 World Cup and Festival match between England and the Republic of Ireland," a spokesperson said.

"The match was abandoned by the referee following England's refusal to continue.

"The official reports are being gathered, and the matter will be referred to the independent disciplinary committee, who will decide in due course."

The Football Association (FA) told BBC Sport it is awaiting more information and was in touch with Fifa, adding the wellbeing of players is a priority.

The Football Association of Ireland (FAI) has also been contacted for comment.

The alleged incident happened in the final minute of the Group C match in Baku.

Immediately following the allegation, England head coach Matt Thorpe was seen on the match coverage on DAZN asking for the referee to come over, before calling his players off the pitch.

After an initial report from Thorpe, the referee told England's coaching staff, "I cannot hear".

One of Thorpe's assistant coaches can then be heard telling the referee "we are reporting racism", before adding "we do not want to play".

When another official came over, talking about what would happen after the match, the assistant told him: "No, our players do not want to play".

He was later heard saying that a player was "upset".

Heated words were then exchanged between each set of coaching staff, with both saying they were trying to protect their players.

This is the first time the U15 World Cup and Festival has been played, and involves 189 countries and territories.

Matches are played in an eight-a-side format, on smaller pitches, with 20-minute halves.

Later on Monday, England beat Zimbabwe 2-0, while the Republic of Ireland were 4-1 winners over Cambodia.

Teams from 189 of Fifa's 211 member associations are taking part in the tournament.

#35993. Harry Kane was not a born Ballon d'Or winner. That's what makes him so special

#35994. Here's how Trump's new Air Force One, a luxury jet from Qatar, compares to the outgoing presidential plane

Since his first term in the White House, President Donald Trump has displayed a model of a new Air Force One plane on his desk.

On July 1, his long-held vision became a reality when the aircraft the US accepted as a gift from Qatar, designated the VC-25B Bridge, flew as Air Force One for the first time.

Speaking to reporters before boarding the aircraft at Joint Base Andrews in Maryland, Trump called it the "best plane ever built."

He later landed in Medora, North Dakota, for the dedication of the Theodore Roosevelt Presidential Library.

Retrofitted with state-of-the-art protective measures and painted in his favored colors of red, white, and blue, the new presidential plane is bigger, faster, more fuel-efficient, and more luxurious than the outgoing VC-25A fleet, which has served presidents since the 1990s.

It has also been brought in and out of service amid operational mishaps and security concerns, such as when Trump secretly switched to a C-32A to fly back from Turkey in July due to threats from Iran, and when the VC-25B Bridge's emergency slide was accidentally deployed in September.

A senior administration official previously told Business Insider that the VC-25B Bridge is "the greatest presidential plane ever built," and added, "We maintain a consistent posture that ensures a safe, secure, and reliable capability for the President of the United States and routinely evolve the capability as required to maintain that posture."

An Air Force spokesperson told Business Insider, "We do not provide specific details on capabilities or provide comparisons between aircraft."

Here's how Trump's new VC-25B Bridge compares to the old VC-25A.

The first VC-25A, known as SAM 28000, began serving as Air Force One in 1990, and is flying alongside the Qatari-donated plane.

The outgoing Air Force One, a second, identical VC-25A known as SAM 29000, entered service in 1991. It completed its final presidential flight in June 2026.

The VC-25A measures 231 feet and 10 inches long with a wingspan of 195 feet and 8 inches.

The VC-25B Bridge — so called because it is serving as an interim presidential aircraft before Boeing's customized VC-25Bs are delivered starting in 2028 — is larger than the VC-25A at about 250 feet long, with a wingspan of 224 feet and 5 inches.

Jacqueline Kennedy chose the shades of blue and white for SAM 26000, a Boeing VC-137C that entered service in 1962 under President John F. Kennedy.

The first lady was also responsible for adding the words "United States of America" to the presidential plane.

An American flag was featured on the tail, with the stars facing forward per the US Flag Code.

The Air Force initially rejected the navy paint color Trump proposed during his first term for the forthcoming VC-25Bs, saying a thermal study found that the darker shade would require additional testing to avoid overheating issues. However, Trump got his preferred colors in his second term. He also reimagined the tail's flag design.

"As you know, we've always gone with a straight little noodle and I never loved that," Trump said of the tail's new waving flag design after touring the plane at Joint Base Andrews on June 19. "Now we have a wavy, beautiful flag. We're waving it proudly because our country is doing so well."

While traveling at 630 miles per hour, the VC-25A had a range of 7,800 miles without refueling.

The VC-25A is also capable of midair refueling.

The VC-25B Bridge can fly faster and farther than its predecessor, with a range of 8,900 miles at 660 miles per hour.

The VC-25B Bridge is not equipped for in-flight refueling. The forthcoming VC-25B planes that Boeing is building will also not be capable of aerial refueling to cut costs, Air and Space Forces Magazine reported.

Some furniture and decor had been updated under various presidents — for example, President Bill Clinton's Air Force One conference room featured tan leather chairs, while President Barack Obama opted for a darker chocolate brown. However, the plane's beige walls and wood paneling didn't change much throughout its 36 years of service.

Trump called the new Air Force One "the world's most luxurious plane." It features a primary bedroom with an en-suite bathroom that includes a walk-in shower, as well as a common area with couches and private office spaces.

The press section is also outfitted with luxurious touches, including reclining massage chairs.

Boeing is building new, customized VC-25Bs to serve as Air Force One, but the $3.9 billion project — which has since increased to more than $5 billion — is behind schedule. The planes were initially scheduled for delivery in 2024, but the timeframe has been pushed to 2028, prompting Trump to seek his own means of acquiring a new presidential jet.

The original contract was fixed-price, so any additional costs must be absorbed by Boeing.

Secretary of the Air Force Troy E. Meink told Congress in June 2025 that retrofitting the new Air Force One would cost less than $400 million, while some lawmakers expressed concerns that it could cost up to $1 billion in taxpayer funds.

In July 2025, The New York Times flagged a $934 million transfer of funds from a nuclear modernization budget to update aging ground-based weapons systems to a classified project, which it reported could be the new Air Force One, since the presidential plane is equipped to order nuclear strikes. The Air Force declined to comment.

#35995. How Molly Ringwald is Using Her 'Gen X Poster Girl' Status to Raise Awareness

Although we’ve bid farewell to side ponytails, power suits and popped-up collars, one ‘80s icon is looking to remind the generation that grew up beside her that their health matters just as much as nostalgia.

After years of playing characters who helped define an era of teen angst, Molly Ringwald, 58, is using her voice for a new cause, partnering with Pfizer and its VaxAssist platform to raise awareness of pneumococcal pneumonia and remind Gen X they shouldn’t play hooky on visiting their doctor’s office.

“One thing that really struck me was the fact that after the age of 50, we are six times more likely to contract pneumococcal pneumonia,” Ringwald exclusively told Us about the prominence of the illness. “I felt as the ultimate Gen X poster girl, it was a good idea to use my platform to just let other people know that this vaccine is available, that it can help save your life.”

But the conversation around health isn’t one the actress is reserving for audiences who watched her navigate the growing pains of adolescence on screen (including the occasional Saturday detentions and juvenile love triangles). Ringwald says she has plenty of “frank” conversations with her own children.

“I’m just really straight with them. I think you know they have agency over themselves and their bodies, and we talk about everything from vaccines to sunscreen to anything for their health,” she told Us. “It’s their health, it’s their bodies and their futures, and I think they care about it just like I care about my health and my future.”

The mother of three — daughter Mathilda, 22, and 17-year-old twins Adele and Roman, whom she shares with her husband, Panio Gianopoulos — admits that although she doesn’t revisit the coming-of-age classics on her own, she has introduced her children to the films that made her a household name.

“I don’t actually have cable, so anything that I watch is a choice,” she candidly shared. “And the only time that I’ve really watched the John Hughes movies was when I watched them with my kids, and that was a choice.”

As the actress prepares to play Van Palmer’s mother in the Paramount+ series Yellowjackets, Ringwald says her character, Vicky, is much more “flawed” than the poignant parent she played in the past to Shailene Woodley in The Secret Life of an American Teenager or KJ Apa in Riverdale.

“I was already a fan of the show,” she said, recalling a chance encounter with a cast member at the Sundance Film Festival that led to her receiving a role. “I think it has a lot of great actors in it and it’s fun watching women my age being fierce and bad and naughty. I love it.”

Beyond her return to TV, the actress also revealed she isn’t done writing her own story. As for what else is in the works, Ringwald says she’s currently penning a memoir called The Paris Years, which she says will take readers down memory lane and chronicle her years in France during her 20s and says directing is something she’s “always wanted to do.”

“I spent sort of coming of age in Paris because I kind of came of age a little bit later because I was working when I was a teenager. So the 20s were really when I kind of came of age,” she told Us, adding she plans to begin writing and directing once her book is finished.

#35996. 'Cutting doubles would destroy careers' - angry players urge ATP rethink

Finland's Harri Heliovaara (left) and Britain's Henry Patten - the world number ones and reigning Wimbledon champions - have signed the open letter

More than 150 men's doubles players - including British world number one Henry Patten and the legendary Bryan brothers - have urged the ATP Tour to rethink "drastic" changes which they believe could kill the format.

In a show of collective action, the players have written an open letter to the men's governing body as they fight proposals which would cut draw sizes and reduce prize money from 2028.

"We're at risk of losing a discipline of tennis which is historic - but has a big future," Dutch player Sem Verbeek, one of the architects of the letter, told BBC Sport.

"These proposals would destroy careers, livelihoods and dreams."

The ATP proposed halving the doubles draws at its flagship Masters 1000 events to 16 teams, with smaller tournaments reduced to eight teams.

The percentage of prize money devoted to doubles could fall from 20% to 10% - with the difference redistributed to singles players.

'You're taking our jobs' - doubles players angry at proposed cuts

On Monday, a group of 137 current players, 28 legends and 20 national federations signed a letter which they hope will pressure the ATP into rethinking the proposals.

The tour's board - comprised of chairman Andrea Gaudenzi, four tournament representatives and four player representatives - will vote on the plans at next month's ATP Finals in Turin.

The doubles reform is part of a wider review by the ATP, which it says is designed to "strengthen" the tour from 2028.

The ATP aims to create "a more sustainable long-term model", insisting it wants to "maintain the important role" of doubles.

"Commercial success matters. Fairness, player welfare and the long-term health of tennis matter just as much," the players' letter says.

"Let us grow tennis together, without leaving doubles players behind."

The proposals provoked an angry response from the men's doubles cohort when they were revealed in July.

British pair Lloyd Glasspool and Julian Cash, who won Wimbledon in 2025 and became world number ones, accused the ATP of trying to "kill part of the sport".

Others within tennis believe reform is needed. They suggest a 20% share of the prize money for doubles is disproportionate to the interest shown by fans.

The doubles players argue the ATP should do more to promote their discipline and attract greater audiences, pointing out it is the format played by most club members and therefore more relatable.

The ATP says the proposals are based on data-led analysis centred on costs, revenue and viewership.

With fewer singles players now competing in doubles, it believes the current size of the standalone doubles events puts severe strain on tournaments.

"We support progress. We also understand the need to grow the sport, strengthen tournaments and increase commercial value," the players' letter says.

"Still, the proposed reforms go much too far."

Reigning Wimbledon champion Patten recently told BBC Sport the players were facing "an uphill battle", but remained "optimistic" the tour would consider counter-proposals. Since Wimbledon there have been several meetings between both sides, including recently at the Shanghai Masters.

The players still believe there is little appetite for negotiation from the ATP. But ATP sources insist a compromise is likely to be found.

Mike and Bob Bryan - the identical twins considered the greatest doubles pair of all-time - said they would support the group considering legal action to protect their discipline.

In 2005, the American brothers led players in a lawsuit against the ATP, when it planned radical changes to doubles.

"The tour's in the healthiest financial state it's ever been - in our opinion it should be creating more opportunities," Bob Bryan told BBC Sport in an interview at Wimbledon in July.

Mike Bryan added: "These proposed changes are anti-competitive. That's the stance we had to take in 2005. We still believe in doubles and we believe in the players fighting for this."

#35997. See Inside Cells Like Never Before in These Vivid Microscopic Images

Get the latest news delivered to your inbox.

An editorially independent publication supported by the Simons Foundation.

Create a reading list by clicking the Read Later icon next to the articles you wish to save.

Popular Searches

Click to tell Google to prioritize Quanta and see more of the best coverage of science and math in your search and AI results.

For this image of neurons in mouse brain tissue, Kaelin Wulf and Rachel Flannery of the Allen Institute used a home-built light-sheet microscope. In light-sheet fluorescence microscopy, a thin sheet of light illuminates a sample, which can then be scanned plane by plane. “I hope people can take away a sense of awe at how intricate, beautiful, and unimaginably vast the brain is,” Wulf said.

Kaelin Wulf, Rachel Flannery/Allen Institute; all photos courtesy of Nikon Small World

On a frigid February morning in Edinburgh, Scotland, Patrick Hickey had run out of living things to put under his microscope. The plants and fungi in his garden had dried up, and summer puddles, with their flamboyant biological complexity, had long frozen over.

Hickey, a mycologist at Hypha Research Ltd., was using plant leaves to study mitochondria, cellular organelles that generate energy. He was planning to sprout mustard seeds for fresh samples, but he was impatient. While making coffee in his kitchen, he noticed a cold-hardy turtle vine hanging from the windowsill. “I snipped off an inch or so, put it in my pocket, took it into the lab, and was just amazed at what I saw,” Hickey said.

Under the microscope, mitochondria darted around the cells. They nimbly maneuvered around slower chloroplasts, like motorcyclists dodging traffic. “It was absolutely alive with activity,” Hickey said.

His time-lapse video of this microscopic dance, which leads our gallery below and was recently honored in Nikon’s 2026 Small World in Motion Competition, reveals the close partnership between the organelles: chloroplasts (red) spin sunlight into sugar, and mitochondria (green) convert sugar into energy.

The Nikon Small World competition, established in 1974, honors excellence in microscopy. The annual contests have charted not only the evolution of creative photomicrography but also the progress of imaging technology.

Hickey — and many of the other honorees featured here — relied on a technique called confocal laser scanning microscopy.

Unlike a standard microscope, which illuminates an entire sample at once and can produce blurry images, a confocal microscope focuses on one depth level at a time. Researchers first “label” a sample with fluorescent dyes that attach to specific structures within the cell. A laser then illuminates a thin layer of the sample, and the microscope detects the resulting fluorescence. Scanning line by line, the system builds a detailed image.

As imaging technology advances, so too does our understanding of cellular processes. Hickey’s video shows that mitochondria are more dynamic than previously thought, which suggests that they are “more than just a ‘powerhouse of the cell,’” he said. “They are likely involved in cytoplasmic streaming, signaling, and interactions with other organelles.”

The full list of awardees can be found here for the photo contest and here for the video contest. We’ve chosen to feature a several winners and honorable mentions that reveal the intricacies of the cellular world.

Get Quanta Magazine delivered to your inbox

Mitochondria (green) navigate around chloroplasts (red) in the leaf cells of a turtle vine. “Their movement looked like rivers of electricity flowing within the cells,” Hickey said.

To study diseases, Gauri Kulkarni of Wake Forest Institute for Regenerative Medicine grows miniature organs in her lab, including the 1.3-millimeter human gut tissue shown in this image. The glowing network reveals the connections between epithelial cells that make up a healthy gut barrier. This barrier acts like a microscopic security guard, keeping harmful microbes and food toxins from leaking into the bloodstream. “In science, we often rely on cold data and numbers to understand disease, but photomicrography bridges the gap between logic and emotion,” Kulkarni said.

Gauri Kulkarni/Wake Forest Institute for Regenerative Medicine

This video shows how heart muscle cells contract and how calcium regulates their beating. The green chains are sarcomeres, the smallest contractile units of heart muscle. The red flashes show calcium inside the cell. At the start of a beat, an electrical impulse sends calcium into the cell, which triggers the release of more calcium. All this calcium tells the sarcomeres to contract. The calcium then leaves the cytoplasm, which allows the cell to relax in preparation for the next beat. “The same video can reveal the beauty of living heart cells while providing quantitative information about their structure and function,” said Yasaman Maaref of Simon Fraser University, who created the video.

This video of a mouse’s brain cells shows actin (blue), a protein that helps the cell change shape, and lysosomes (orange), organelles that break down cellular waste. While both structures move, the lysosomes are much faster, making the actin network appear almost stationary in comparison.

Bruno Cisterna, Halli Lindamood, Eric Vitriol/Medical College of Georgia at Augusta University

The top layer of this image of a mouse trachea shows the epithelium, the outer layer of skin cells. Each green dot is the nucleus of a single cell. Beneath, a layer of black basement membrane, mostly made of collagen, provides structure and acts as a filter. Next, a thick layer of yellow reveals the actin packed inside smooth muscle cells that control the movement of air through the trachea. And that faint layer of dark blue at upper right? That’s “the kind of mucus you cough and spit out,” said Julien Resseguier, who created the image to study the interplay between neural and immune systems.

This image of a rat liver endothelial cell shows actin filaments (brownish orange) and microtubules (white). Both of these protein structures work together to give the cell its shape. Intracellular gaps are visible in the image; these holes can disrupt the liver’s protective blood barrier and might indicate injury, inflammation, or cancer.

Francisco Lázaro-Diéguez/Albert Einstein College of Medicine

Embryonic cells from the skin of Xenopus, a clawed frog, have cilia, seen here in green, that beat in unison to generate a wave of fluid movement past the cell surface. Confocal laser scanning microscopy allowed the researchers to distinguish individual cilia, the centrioles that anchor them (red), and cell boundaries (white) that help the cells stick together.

Pierre Nouaux, Laurent Kodjabachianan, Camille Boutin/IBDM Marseille

Trypanosoma cruzi, the parasite that causes Chagas disease, is an intracellular pathogen that can infect almost all mammal cell types. Richard Atherton of the London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine captured this image of a T. cruzi nest inside a host cell. “If my image can raise even a little more awareness about Chagas disease, then I will be happy,” Atherton said. “I also hope my image can get people excited about science, nature, and the infinite microscopic worlds hidden all around us.”

Richard Atherton/London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine

This video, by Andrew Moore of the Howard Hughes Medical Institute, illuminates how actin (white) and mitochondria (blue) interact during cell division. Moore found that the actin seemed to be involved in positioning and organizing the mitochondria; the actin cycles through the mitochondrial network in a wave and forms “comet tails” that drive bursts of motion. Over the course of mitosis, these comet tails “mix up the mitochondrial network, shuffling their spatial position, almost like a card dealer shuffling a deck of cards,” Moore said.

Andrew Moore/Howard Hughes Medical Institute
