#36094. Sweet Potato Souffle

This sweet potato souffle recipe is light, fluffy, and sweet without overdoing it. An easy make-ahead holiday side dish!

This Sweet Potato Souffle has the distinct habit of stealing the side dish show. With velvety roasted sweet potatoes, plenty of butter butter, and a crispy-crunchy pecan topping, how could it not?!

Move over turkey, this sweet potato souffle will be the star of your Thanksgiving dinner!

We all have our sweet potato preferences when it comes to what to pair with Thanksgiving Turkey.

While I prefer something more savory (like Glazed Sweet Potatoes), I know others are more inclined to a sweeter dishes like Sweet Potato Casserole and Candied Sweet Potatoes. This souffle is the perfect middle ground.

It’s sweeter than Mashed Sweet Potatoes but it’s nowhere near as cloying as the standard casserole topped with marshmallows.

Though the word “souffle” suggests a dish that’s fussy (and you can allow your friends to assume as much), this simple sweet potato souffle recipe is EASY (even easier than Cornbread Stuffing). Despite its uncomplicated prep, this dish feels special!

“This sweet potato souffle is absolutely delicious! The BEST sweet potato side dish ever! It’s also great as a healthy dessert. I’ve made it twice and will certainly make it again and again.”

You’ll find the full list of ingredients in the recipe card below, but here are some notes to keep in mind.

Roast the Sweet Potatoes. Bake until tender. (See How to Bake a Sweet Potato for more.)

Scoop out the Flesh. Add it to a mixing bowl.

Add the Remaining Ingredients. Get ready to mix!

Assemble the Casserole. Transfer the mixture to a baking dish.

Prepare the Pecan Streusel Topping. Sprinkle it over the sweet potatoes.

Bake. Bake the sweet potato souffle in a 350 degrees F oven for 40 minutes. Let rest for 5 minutes, then ENJOY!

While most recipes require that you boil, drain, and mash the potatoes first, I found I could cut down on the sugar significantly if I roasted the sweet potatoes, AND the dish tasted better! Roasting concentrates the sweet potatoes’ natural sugars, making the souffle taste sweeter and working with what this vegetable gives us naturally (I also use this trick for Roasted Sweet Potatoes).

This is a great make-ahead side dish for Thanksgiving or any holiday.

#36095. Pork Stew

This pork stew is so hearty and comforting, with tender pork, root veggies and apples simmered in a savory broth. Perfect for chilly nights!

Rustic and hearty, this Pork Stew is the kind of meal that sticks to your ribs! With tender chunks of pork shoulder, potatoes, carrots, parsnips and a rich broth with cider, sage and a hint of cinnamon, it’s the perfect dinner for a chilly evening.

Move over beef, I’m all about pork stew now!

As a recipe developer, there is a constant rotation of new and in-progress recipes in my kitchen. While they’re all good (I wouldn’t post them if they weren’t!), some steal my heart a little more than the others. And this pork stew is one of those.

Originally, I’d planned to simply swap in pork for the beef in my Beef Stew, but the more I thought about it, the more I realized I was missing out on a chance to pair pork with one of its greatest complements: apples! (As seen in my Pork Chops With Apples recipe.)

I started by swapping the red wine for a dry cider (this needs to be alcoholic and DRY—no Redd’s). Rather than add a pinch of sugar or honey to offset the acid in the stew, I grated an apple, which dissolves into the broth.

Let’s talk about what makes it a standout:

You’ll find the full list of ingredients in the recipe card below, but here are some notes to keep in mind.

If you prefer not to cook with hard cider or white wine, you can use non-alcoholic apple cider.

Because regular apple cider is sweeter than dry hard cider, I’d start with a mixture of 3/4 cup apple cider and 3/4 cup additional chicken broth in place of the 1 1/2 cups hard cider.

Taste the finished stew and add a little extra apple cider vinegar or Dijon if it needs more acidity.

Season the Pork (photo 1). Place the pork chunks in a large bowl. Add the flour, salt, and black pepper, then toss until every piece is evenly (but lightly!) coated.

Brown the Pork in Batches (photo 2). Heat a Dutch oven or large soup pot over medium heat. Add 1 tablespoon of the oil and let it warm for about 30 seconds. Arrange half of the pork in a single layer and cook undisturbed for 3 minutes, until the bottom is golden. Then, turn and brown the other side. Transfer to a plate and repeat with the remaining oil and pork.

Cook the Onions (photo 3). Reduce the heat to medium-low. Add the diced onions and cook for about 10 minutes, stirring occasionally and scraping the bottom of the pot.

Add the Apple and Garlic (photo 4). Stir the grated apple and garlic into the onions. Cook for about 30 seconds, just until fragrant.

Deglaze the Pot (photo 5). Pour in the hard cider and apple cider vinegar. Bring to a simmer, scraping the bottom of the pot with a wooden spoon or spatula to release the browned bits left behind by the pork. Let the liquid simmer for 2 minutes to reduce slightly.

Simmer the Pork (photo 6). Return the pork and any accumulated juices to the pot. Add the broth, Worcestershire sauce, sage, bay leaves, and cinnamon stick. Bring to a boil, then reduce the heat to low. Partially cover and simmer gently for 90 minutes (photo 7), stirring occasionally.

Add the Vegetables (photo 8). After the pork has simmered for 90 minutes, stir in the vegetables. Cover the pot partway and continue simmering for 45 minutes to 1 hour, until the vegetables are tender. Stir occasionally and add more water if needed.

Finish (photo 9). Remove and discard the bay leaves and cinnamon stick. Stir in the Dijon mustard and cook for another 2 minutes. Then, using a potato masher, gently mash the stew a few times. This thickens the broth slightly while leaving plenty of chunks. Taste and adjust the salt, pepper, and Dijon mustard. Ladle into bowls and finish with chopped fresh parsley or sage. ENJOY!

#36096. Taco Rice

Taco rice combines seasoned beef, rice, beans, salsa, and cheese in one pot. This easy, flavorful dinner is perfect for busy weeknights.

This Taco Rice recipe is an easy one-pot dinner with seasoned ground beef, fluffy rice, black beans, salsa, green chiles, and plenty of cheese. Add your go-to taco toppings for a hearty, family-friendly meal with hardly any cleanup.

And if you don’t believe me, let me point you to my Meal Prep Taco Rice recipe. The word “obsession” is over-used in the online food space, but I think it really applies here.

Think of this recipe as a mashup between Ground Beef Taco filling with Mexican rice and Black Beans. It’s crack-a-lackin’ with Tex-Mex seasonings, super cheesy, incredibly easy—I mean, if this doesn’t have family-friendly weeknight dinner written all over it, I don’t know what does!

Everything cooks together in one pot, so the rice soaks up the salsa and taco spices as it simmers. And much like actual tacos, taco rice is even better once you make it your own with all your favorite toppings!

You’ll find the full list of ingredients in the recipe card below, but here are some notes to keep in mind.

Brown the Meat (photo 1). Heat a Dutch oven or another large, deep skillet over medium-high heat. Once it’s hot, add the olive oil. Add the ground beef or turkey along with the seasonings. Cook, breaking the meat into crumbles, until it’s completely browned and cooked through. Adjust the seasonings to taste.

Add the Remaining Ingredients (photo 2). Stir in the rinsed and drained rice, beef broth, salsa, black beans, and diced green chiles.

Bring to a Boil, Then Simmer. Increase the heat and bring the liquid to a boil. Then, cover the pan and reduce the heat to low; let it simmer for 15 minutes. (If your lid has a steam vent, cover the hole with a small piece of foil to trap in the steam.)

Let It Rest (photo 3). Once the rice is tender, remove the pot from the heat. Keep it covered and let it rest for 5 minutes.

Make It Cheesy (photo 4). Uncover the pot and gently stir in the shredded cheese until it melts. Divide into bowls and add your favorite toppings. ENJOY!

I tested this with a few types of rice and the winner is jasmine or basmati. Brown rice was too damp and I wasn’t happy with how long it took to cook through. The beans have plenty of fiber anyway!

#36097. How Long to Cook a Turkey

Home / Recipes by Occasion / Thanksgiving

Exactly how long to cook a turkey by weight at 325°F, or my high-low method for crispy skin. Plus how to tell when it's done for juicy, moist turkey.

Turkey timing is stressful, especially since most of us only roast one or two a year. Below is exactly how long to cook a turkey by weight, plus tips for telling when it’s done. For the full recipe, see my step-by-step guide for How to Cook a Turkey.

At 325°F the whole time, plan on 10 to 12 minutes per pound (22 to 26 minutes per kg).

With my method (450°F for 45 minutes to crisp the skin, then 325°F to finish), plan on 8 to 10 minutes per pound (18 to 22 minutes per kg).

The turkey is done when the breast, thigh, and wing joint reach 165°F.

Hey I’m Erin, and I’ve been cooking turkey for more than 15 years!

My preferred method is to start the bird at a high temperature (450°F) for 45 minutes to crisp the skin, then reduce the heat to 325°F for the remainder of the cook time.

That said, you can certainly roast at 325°F the entire time, and both options are in the chart below.

My times may look shorter than other charts available online. This is because I recommend letting the turkey sit at room temperature for 1 hour prior to roasting so you aren’t putting a cold bird into the oven, which can cause it to cook unevenly.

I also recommend pulling the turkey a few degrees early (155°F to 160°F), because the temperature will rise to 165°F and beyond as it rests.

Find your turkey’s weight below. Start checking the temperature at the low end of the range, and plan dinner for the high end plus about 30 minutes as a buffer. If it finishes early, it stays hot, covered, for 1½ to 2 hours.

For guidance on what size turkey to buy, see How Much Turkey Per Person.

This first chart is for a room temperature unstuffed turkey cooked in a roasting pan.

I recommend cooking the stuffing OUTSIDE of the bird, as the turkey is likely to overcook by the time the stuffing reaches 165°F. This Cornbread Stuffing Recipe is an all-time favorite!

If you must cook the stuffing inside the bird, refer to the chart below.

The best and only foolproof way to check a turkey for doneness is to use an instant read thermometer. This one is the best on the market and a worthy investment if you cook often. This affordable option works well also.

Your turkey is safe to eat when all of the following areas register at least 165°F:

Check in several spots and if they don’t agree, go by the lowest reading.

If you stuffed the turkey, the very center of the stuffing must also reach 165°F.

I personally take the turkey out of the oven when the breast reads between 155°F and 160°F, then tent it with foil and let it rest 20 to 30 minutes. The internal temperature will continue to rise to 165°F as it rests.

Juice color is not reliable for doneness, so do not use that as your only test.

Thawing takes days, and brining takes 1 to 2 more, so count backward from Thanksgiving day.

Plan on 24 hours of refrigerator thawing for every 4 to 5 pounds of turkey.

Thaw the turkey breast side up on a rimmed baking sheet in the refrigerator (40°F or below) so any juices are contained.

Never thaw a turkey on the counter at room temperature. It is unsafe.

Use the cold water method. Keep the turkey in its packaging, submerge it in cold water, and change the water every 30 minutes. Plan on about 30 minutes per pound, and roast it as soon as it’s thawed.

Short on time even for that? See “Frozen or partially frozen” above.

#36098. A simple touch can bring dying corals back to life in just 7 weeks

For the first time, researchers have demonstrated that severely bleached corals can fully recover after spending just a few days in contact with healthy, genetically identical corals.

In laboratory experiments, corals that had turned white and were close to dying regained their health within seven weeks. According to a study published on October 14 in Science Advances, this represents the fastest documented recovery from coral bleaching. The discovery could lead to new techniques for restoring coral reefs, which some estimates suggest could disappear within decades as climate change fuels increasingly severe and frequent marine heat waves.

Coral bleaching occurs when rising ocean temperatures cause corals to lose the beneficial algae living inside their tissues. These algae supply much of the energy corals depend on for growth and survival. Without them, corals lose their vibrant colors, struggle to obtain enough nourishment, and become more susceptible to harmful algae. Even when they survive, regaining their health can take months or years.

"While bleached corals may be dying, they are not dead yet," said lead author Danny Collins, an oceans PhD student at the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability. "The question we wanted to answer is, once a coral has bleached, what can we do?"

An Unexpected Discovery Reveals a Way to Rescue Dying Corals

Although they may resemble plants or rocks, corals are animals. In most coral species, individual animals reproduce by creating genetically identical copies of themselves. These clones grow together into large colonies that form the foundation of coral reefs.

The idea for the study emerged from an unexpected observation in 2025. Steve Palumbi, a study coauthor and the Jane and Marshall Steel, Jr. Professor in Marine Sciences, noticed something unusual in his laboratory. Two fragments taken from the same coral colony, one healthy and the other bleached, had fallen against each other in an aquarium.

Where the fragments touched, the bleached coral began regaining its color. The change suggested that beneficial algae were returning to its tissues.

At roughly the same time, Collins was investigating how coral diseases develop and spread. He was particularly interested in bacteria carried by plastic debris that lands on corals and can introduce harmful microorganisms through direct contact.

"Harmful bacteria enter corals through physical contact," said Collins, who is also a Knight-Hennessy Scholar. "We were curious about what would happen if we used contact to reintroduce something good - the beneficial algae from healthy corals."

To investigate, Collins designed an experiment using 10 pairs of coral fragments. Each pair consisted of one healthy fragment and one bleached fragment originating from the same coral animal.

Five pairs were positioned so that the fragments physically touched. The other five pairs were separated by gaps measuring only a few millimeters.

The results revealed a dramatic difference. Every bleached fragment that touched its healthy counterpart recovered completely within seven weeks. In contrast, the bleached fragments that were kept apart died.

How a Simple Touch Triggers Rapid Coral Recovery

The researchers discovered that the remarkable recovery depends on a special ability of genetically identical corals to connect their tissues.

"Genetically identical corals recognize one another when they come into contact," said Palumbi, who is a professor of oceans in the Doerr School of Sustainability and of biology in the School of Humanities and Sciences. "Their tissues fuse if they are from the same clone. We see something similar in humans. Organ transplants are often successful if the donor is your identical twin."

Using a powerful microscope, Collins and his colleagues examined what happened when the coral fragments touched. They found that tiny channels within their tissues quickly lined up, forming connections that allowed materials to move between the two fragments.

Through these connections, beneficial algae and the nutrients they produce could pass from the healthy coral into the bleached one. Within just three days, researchers observed algae transferring into the damaged fragments.

The team then investigated how long the corals actually needed to remain connected. By separating pairs after different periods of contact, they discovered that a single week was enough to transfer the algae required for complete recovery. In some cases, just five days of contact produced the same result.

Remarkably, the separated corals still returned to full health within seven weeks, the same amount of time required by corals that remained connected throughout the experiment.

This finding revealed that the rapid recovery was not primarily driven by a continuing supply of algae from the healthy coral. Instead, once enough algae entered the bleached fragment, they reproduced rapidly inside their new host and restored its energy supply.

"Once beneficial algae enter bleached corals, they don't just spread out," said Palumbi. "They flood in and start dividing at the fastest rates seen in corals."

During the second week of contact, the beneficial algae living inside the bleached fragments doubled in number approximately every four days. That growth rate was about ten times faster than what researchers have observed in wild corals.

A Promising Breakthrough for Coral Reef Restoration

Scientists have spent decades searching for ways to protect coral reefs from rising ocean temperatures, disease, increasingly acidic seawater, and other environmental pressures.

Their efforts have included selectively breeding corals that can withstand higher temperatures, raising young corals in nurseries to rebuild damaged reefs, and transferring communities of microorganisms between corals to improve their resilience.

The new findings suggest another possibility: helping bleached corals recover quickly enough to survive the dangerous period following a marine heat wave.

"Bleached corals are often considered a lost cause because recovery after a heatwave is very slow, if it happens at all," said Fiorenza Micheli, the David and Lucile Packard Professor in Marine Science and a faculty advisor of Collins. "We hope this new research will inspire solutions that keep corals healthy long enough to enable full recovery after heat waves through slower processes such as growth, reproduction, and recruitment of new colonies."

However, the researchers emphasize that their experimental approach cannot yet be used to restore reefs on a large scale. For the technique to work as demonstrated, every bleached coral would need access to a healthy, genetically identical counterpart. Finding and supplying those matches across entire reefs would be impractical.

Important questions also remain about how the recovery mechanism could be adapted for broader use.

Over the coming months, the team intends to investigate other ways to introduce beneficial algae into bleached corals without requiring them to remain physically connected to healthy fragments.

One possibility involves collecting beneficial algae from healthy corals well before marine heat waves occur. Researchers could then deliver large quantities of these algae to damaged corals after bleaching events, potentially allowing them to recover much faster.

"These new insights into how corals can naturally recover give us hope for the future," said Micheli, who also co-directs the Stanford Center for Ocean Solutions. "They open the door to what's possible and may lead to the breakthrough we need to accelerate reef restoration."

Micheli also serves as a professor of oceans and (by courtesy) of biology. Both Micheli and Palumbi are senior fellows at the Woods Institute for the Environment, which is part of the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability.

Additional Stanford researchers who contributed to the study include Jana Sipkova, Nathaniel Mollica, Tom Rolander, Courtney Klepac, Gabriel Calistro, and Vanessa Barone.

Mollica is a research scientist in Stanford's Oceans Department, where Calistro works as a research assistant. Rolander is a research engineer in the Fabrication Lab at the Harold A. Miller Library at Hopkins Marine Station, which belongs to the Doerr School of Sustainability.

Sipkova is a postdoctoral scholar, while Klepac is a research scientist. Barone is an assistant professor in the Biology Department of Stanford's School of Humanities and Sciences and is affiliated with Bio-X and the Maternal & Child Health Research Institute.

The Molecular and Cellular Biodiversity Imaging Center, operated by Stanford's Biology Department at Hopkins Marine Station, conducted the coral image analyses.

Financial support came from the National Science Foundation and Stanford University, including seed funding through the Big Ideas for Oceans grant program. The program is organized by the Oceans Department and the Woods Institute for the Environment within the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability.

Materials provided by Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#36099. Onion Tart

This upside down French onion tart layers puff pastry with caramelized onions, gruyere cheese, and fresh thyme for an easy, elegant appetizer that's perfect for a party.

This easy onion tart layers thinly sliced onions, Gruyère cheese, and thyme on puff pastry, then bakes them into an elegant appetizer perfect for the holidays, or any party you want to feel extra special!

Onion tart: humble ingredients, delicious results!

French cooking has the reputation of being fussy…and some of it is.

I’ve traveled all over France in the last 25 years, however, and I can tell you that much of it really is simple.

In fact, the French dishes I love most are the ones that transform a humble ingredient, like onions, into something special.

When we were in Alsace last year, I fell in love with tarte à l’oignon. It’s little more than pastry, onion, cheese, and cream, but WOW, the combined effect is spectacular.

French onion soup is another example. It doesn’t get more rustic than onions, cheese, and bread, yet the soup is so complex, delicious, and satisfying, it’s hard to want anything more.

(If you’d like to try French-style cooking at home, check out my French Onion Soup recipe. It takes some time, but it’s easy and sooooo worth it!)

The key to developing onions’ flavor is the caramelization.

Unfortunately, traditional caramelized onions are time-consuming. It can take 30 minutes to 1 hour, depending on your batch size.

To cheat the clock, I sliced the onions super thinly on a mandoline, layered them on a baking sheet with honey to mimic the caramelized sugar, then baked the puff pastry ON TOP of the onions.

The onions caramelize against the pan, then when the tart is done baking, you flip it over and slice.

It’s the same method I used for this Upside Down Apple Tart. It works like a dream!

You’ll find the full list of ingredients in the recipe card below, but here are some notes to keep in mind.

These are easy, but your friends will be very impressed!

#36100. Buffalo Chicken Pasta

This Buffalo chicken pasta is creamy, cheesy, and perfectly spicy. An easy 25-minute dinner that cooks in one pot for minimal cleanup!

This one-skillet Buffalo Chicken Pasta is a creamy, cheesy crowd-pleaser with a kick! It takes everything you love about Buffalo chicken and transforms it into an easy 25-minute dinner.

Buffalo chicken pasta gives a game day fave a weekday makeover!

We love Air Fryer Chicken Wings, and I won’t lie, Ben and I have totally made a weeknight dinner out of them. But sometimes you want that Buffalo flavor in something a little more dinner-appropriate.

Something like Buffalo Baked Salmon. Or Buffalo Chicken Chili. Orrrrr: Buffalo chicken pasta!

You’ll find the full list of ingredients in the recipe card below, but here are some notes to keep in mind.

Season the Chicken (photo 1). Place the chicken in a bowl. Add the garlic powder, onion powder, dill, cayenne, and salt, then toss until the pieces are evenly coated.

Brown the Chicken (photo 2). Heat a skillet over medium heat. Once hot, add the butter and let it melt. Arrange the chicken in a single layer and cook for about 4 minutes, turning so it browns on all sides. The chicken does not need to be completely cooked through; it will continue cooking while the pasta simmers.

Add the Pasta (photo 3). Pour in the can of diced tomatoes, including the liquid, along with the chicken broth and uncooked penne. Stir well, scraping the bottom of the skillet to loosen up the browned bits.

Simmer (photo 4). Cover the skillet and bring to a simmer. Once simmering, uncover and stir, then replace the lid and reduce the heat to medium-low. Cook for 10 to 12 minutes, uncovering and stirring every 3 to 4 minutes. (Regular stirring is important here because the pasta isn’t completely covered by liquid.)

Make It Delicious. Turn off the heat and stir in the Buffalo sauce and cream cheese (photo 5). Once the pasta is evenly coated and the cream cheese is smooth, add the mozzarella and cheddar a handful at a time until it melts (photo 6). Serve with green onions and additional Buffalo sauce and ENJOY!

The big challenge with this recipe was how to make the pasta creamy without the sauce breaking.

Tomatoes and hot sauce are both acidic, which means they don’t always play well with dairy. I tested evaporated milk, but couldn’t keep it from curdling. The winner ended up being cream cheese! You can get away with 1/3 fat (Neufchatel), just make sure to turn off the heat before adding it.

#36101. Archaeologists discover 1,200-year-old mummified hairless dog in Peru

Archaeologists working at an ancient burial complex in Peru have uncovered remarkable evidence of hairless dogs that lived more than 1,200 years ago, long before the rise of the Inca Empire. The discoveries include naturally mummified remains, unusual skeletal features, and clues about what the animals ate and how they were treated by the people around them.

Peruvian hairless dogs are a distinctive Indigenous breed known for their slender bodies, pointed ears, and lack of fur. They frequently appear in pottery created by ancient societies along the Andean coast. Today, a variation of the breed is recognized by the American Kennel Club as the Peruvian Inca Orchid. The dogs are also an important national symbol, having been officially designated part of Peru's cultural heritage in 2000.

A study published in the Journal of Anthropological Archaeology reports the first physical evidence of Peruvian hairless dogs at Castillo de Huarmey, the only known Wari Empire site discovered so far on Peru's northern coast.

Ancient Dog Remains Discovered at a Wari Empire Site

Located approximately 190 miles north of Lima, Castillo de Huarmey covers 110 acres and once served as an administrative and burial center for the Wari Empire. The complex was occupied between 600 and 1050 CE, roughly 400 years before the Inca rose to power.

The site has already produced several extraordinary archaeological discoveries. In 2012, researchers uncovered the first undisturbed elite Wari tomb, containing the remains of 58 high-status women, six human sacrifices, and approximately 1,300 artifacts. Another important discovery followed in 2022, when archaeologists identified nearby burials of elite craftspeople accompanied by gold, silver, and bronze tools. Together, these findings have helped researchers understand how the Wari Empire governed its territories.

The newly examined dog remains offer a different perspective on daily life at the site, particularly the complicated relationships between people and animals.

"Our findings indicate that humans and dogs coexisted at this Wari site, but reconstructing their bond is challenging, as past emotions are difficult to capture through archaeological methods," says first author Weronika Tomczyk, a research associate in Dartmouth's Department of Anthropology and a postdoctoral fellow in its Ecology, Evolution, Environment and Society Program.

"The goal of our study was to not only present data on Castillo de Huarmey's dog burials, but also emphasize that people in the past had often contradictory relationships with the animals, just as they do today: a pet for one group of people could at the same time be considered a pest by their closest neighbors."

Remarkably Preserved Dog Mummies Found in the Peruvian Desert

Castillo de Huarmey lies in an extremely dry region of Peru's Pacific coast. The desert's exceptionally arid conditions have helped preserve materials that would normally deteriorate over time, including hair, bones, clothing, leather, pigments, and decorated pottery.

Among the artifacts recovered from the site is an unusual ceramic vessel depicting a seated Peruvian hairless dog with humanlike features. The animal appears to be holding an object resembling a musical instrument.

Between 2010 and 2025, excavations also uncovered numerous remains of South American camelids, probably domesticated llamas and alpacas. Some may have been deposited as offerings during funerary ceremonies, while others appear to have been discarded as waste. Archaeologists also recovered the skeletal remains of dogs.

However, interpreting these discoveries has been complicated by extensive damage and looting. A powerful earthquake in 1970 caused part of Castillo de Huarmey to collapse, exposing burial chambers containing pottery and other valuable objects. Looters subsequently disturbed large sections of the complex.

The deeper archaeological layers remained untouched and retained their original Wari context. In contrast, the upper layers, where most of the animal remains were discovered, contained materials that had become mixed together.

During excavations in the northern section of the main ceremonial area, three sets of dog remains stood out.

One was a naturally mummified skull that still retained visible hairless skin and attached ears. The skull had also been colored with cinnabar, a bright red pigment commonly used in ancient Peruvian funerary traditions.

Researchers discovered a second, larger dog skull, along with the naturally mummified skeleton of a male dog buried in a shallow pit. The male skeleton was almost complete, missing only the lower portions of its front limbs.

At first, the researchers suspected that the mummified dog might have been buried relatively recently, perhaps around 50 years earlier during the period of looting. But Tomczyk noticed two important details: the animal was hairless, and all three dogs lacked their first premolars.

Missing Teeth Reveal the Dogs' Ancient Identity

The missing teeth provided an unexpected clue that helped researchers identify the animals.

"What I noticed is that some of the dog skeletons were missing teeth, and not that they lost them during their lifespan; some teeth, especially first premolars and sometimes last molars, had just never erupted," said Tomczyk. "And then I found in the literature that the same gene which is responsible for hairlessness in dogs is responsible for a reduced number of teeth, indicating that these were Peruvian hairless dogs."

In other words, the animals were not necessarily missing teeth because of injury, disease, or old age. Their reduced number of teeth was linked to the same genetic trait responsible for their hairlessness.

This distinctive biological connection offered evidence that the remains belonged to Peruvian hairless dogs rather than other ancient dog populations.

To investigate further, the researchers applied standard zooarchaeological techniques, which involve studying animal remains recovered from archaeological sites. These methods helped them estimate the ages of the dogs and examine their physical characteristics.

The team also analyzed chemical signatures preserved in the animals' bones and teeth. Known as isotopic analysis, this technique can reveal information about what an animal ate and drank, as well as where it may have lived.

The researchers examined isotopes of carbon, nitrogen, oxygen, and strontium. These are different forms of chemical elements that can preserve clues about diet and environmental conditions. Strontium is particularly useful because its composition varies with local geology and can help scientists trace an animal's geographic origins or movements.

The results were compared with similar measurements from human and camelid remains recovered at Castillo de Huarmey.

"Isotopic analyses of a dog's tooth give a signal of its life as a puppy because dog teeth erupt so fast, and the bone gives a signal of more or less the last year of its life," said Tomczyk. "Such sampling strategy aimed to provide signals from distinct stages of a dog's life."

Ancient Puppies Buried Alongside Elite Members of Society

Altogether, the excavations produced 341 dog bone specimens representing at least 19 individual dogs, in addition to the complete mummified male dog.

Some of the remains were found in particularly intriguing burial contexts.

For example, the bones of a puppy estimated to have been just 6 to 8 weeks old were discovered alongside an elite craftsperson nicknamed the "Master Basketmaker." Other finds included the bones of an adult dog buried within the site's palace and part of a puppy skeleton buried with a male individual identified as XY, who was probably sacrificed to serve as a tomb guardian.

Most of the dogs were adults at the time of burial. However, their remains suggest that the animals did not all receive the same treatment.

Some appear to have been deliberately buried, potentially reflecting a special role in Wari society. Others were found among discarded materials. Only a small number of bones displayed butchery marks, suggesting that dogs were not commonly consumed as food.

Radiocarbon dating provided another important discovery. The complete mummified male dog was determined to be at least 1,200 years old, making it the oldest radiocarbon-dated specimen recovered from Castillo de Huarmey so far.

This result has important implications for understanding the site's chronology because it provides an additional dating reference for the ancient complex.

Ancient Hairless Dogs May Have Eaten Like Humans

The isotopic evidence also revealed surprising similarities between the diets of dogs and people living at Castillo de Huarmey.

Most of the dogs had consumed at least some maize, or corn, which was a major food source throughout the pre-Hispanic Andes. Their diets therefore overlapped with those of the human population.

An especially interesting pattern emerged among the dogs believed to have been hairless.

When these animals were puppies, their diets appeared more similar to those of human children. As they grew older, however, their food sources became more varied.

The findings raise the possibility that some puppies received particular attention or were raised under controlled conditions, potentially as part of an organized breeding practice.

"We do not know if the dogs were intentionally fed maize or if perhaps, they were just eating leftovers or trash," said Tomczyk. "Yet, the distinct dietary patterns of hairless puppies provide insights into what may have been organized breeding."

The researchers emphasize that the archaeological evidence cannot establish whether these dogs were cherished pets, working animals, or animals with other roles in Wari society.

Nevertheless, the differences in burial practices, physical characteristics, and dietary patterns suggest that the hairless dogs held a distinctive place within the community.

"While we will never know if any of the three dogs were pets or how people treated them, it is evident they received different treatment than other dogs," said Tomczyk.

The discoveries provide a rare glimpse into the lives of dogs and humans more than a millennium ago, showing that relationships with animals could be just as varied and complicated in ancient societies as they are today.

The study's co-authors include Miłosz Giersz, Wiesław Więckowski, and Roberto Pimentel Nita of the University of Warsaw, along with Claire Ebert of the University of Pittsburgh.

Materials provided by Dartmouth College. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#36102. This bird flies thousands of miles, but its wings have a catch

A bird that migrates thousands of miles has revealed a surprising secret about how wings work. Scientists have discovered that European nightjars sacrifice flight efficiency for agility and use an unexpected wingbeat technique previously associated mainly with bats and insects.

The European nightjar (Caprimulgus europaeus) is a nighttime hunter that makes an extraordinary annual migration between Europe and southern Africa. Yet the wings that carry it across vast distances must also perform a very different task: helping the bird catch insects while flying slowly and making sharp turns.

Scientists at Lund University have discovered that these competing demands come with a surprising trade-off. Although the nightjar is built to handle long migrations, its wings are not perfectly optimized for efficient flight at higher speeds.

Why Nightjar Wings Sacrifice Efficiency for Agility

To investigate how nightjars fly, researchers studied the birds in a wind tunnel, recording their movements at different speeds and measuring the patterns of airflow left behind their wings.

The experiments revealed that the nightjar's broad wing tips, which likely help it maneuver during slow flight, become a disadvantage as the bird flies faster. At higher speeds, its wings generate lift less efficiently. Lift is the aerodynamic force that helps keep a bird airborne.

The findings challenge the assumption that birds traveling enormous distances must have wings optimized primarily for efficient long-distance flight.

"This runs counter to the intuitive image of a long-distance migrant as a perfectly optimized flying machine. Instead, the nightjar's wings appear to be a compromise between several different demands. The bird can fly slowly and maneuver while hunting, but the trade-off is reduced efficiency in lift generation at higher speeds," says Christoffer Johansson, associate professor at Lund University.

Scientists Discover an Unexpected Wingbeat Trick

The researchers also uncovered another surprising feature of the nightjar's flight. Even as the bird raises its wings during the upward portion of each wingbeat, it continues to generate thrust, the force that helps propel it forward.

This is unusual because such an active upward wing movement, known as an upstroke, has previously been linked mainly to the flight mechanics of bats and insects rather than birds.

The discovery suggests that animals with very different evolutionary histories may have developed similar aerodynamic solutions to the challenges of flying.

"What's interesting is that the same type of aerodynamic solution seems to appear in quite different animals. This suggests that the upstroke does not have to be merely a passive or lift generating part of the wingbeat. It can be actively used to generate thrust when the wings cannot be folded very much," says Christoffer Johansson.

What Bird Flight Reveals About Evolution and Flying Robots

The results offer a broader lesson about how evolution shapes animals that depend on the same body structures for different activities.

Rather than producing wings perfectly suited to one purpose, evolution can favor designs that balance several competing needs. For the nightjar, that means combining the ability to migrate across continents with the agility required to hunt insects at low speeds.

Understanding these compromises could also lead to improvements in aviation technology, particularly drones and other robotic aircraft that use flapping wings.

Like birds, these machines may face mechanical restrictions on how much their wings can fold during each wingbeat. Learning how nightjars generate thrust during the upstroke could help engineers develop more effective ways to move through the air.

Researchers now hope to determine whether this unusual flight mechanism is widespread among other birds and flying animals or whether it represents a more specialized adaptation.

"We now want to investigate how common this way of generating force is among other birds and flying animals. It could help us understand which aerodynamic principles are specific to the nightjar and which are more general. This knowledge may also inspire the development of new types of flapping aircraft," Christoffer Johansson concludes.

Materials provided by Lund University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#36105. Más allá del campo de batalla: Veterinarios revelan el impacto oculto de la guerra de Ucrania en el bienestar de los animales

El 59,7% de los veterinarios encuestados percibe un empeoramiento generalizado del bienestar animal en el país, con problemas que van más allá de las lesiones directamente relacionadas con el conflicto

Un estudio basado en una encuesta a profesionales veterinarios que ejercieron en Ucrania antes y después de la invasión a gran escala de Rusia en febrero de 2022 apunta a un empeoramiento generalizado y multidimensional del bienestar de los animales de compañía en el país por la guerra, con problemas que van más allá de las lesiones directamente relacionadas con el conflicto armado.

La encuesta, anónima y transversal, se realizó entre el 12 de marzo y el 5 de junio de 2026. Entre sus resultados, el estudio identifica que el 59,7% de los 119 profesionales veterinarios encuestados percibió que el bienestar era peor o mucho peor que antes de la invasión a gran escala de febrero de 2022.

Entre los cambios percibidos con mayor frecuencia destacan el aumento del miedo o las fobias relacionadas con el ruido, señalado por el 89,9% de los encuestados que respondieron a esta cuestión; la ansiedad o hipervigilancia, indicada por el 88,1%, y la angustia relacionada con la separación, comunicada por el 86,8%.

Los profesionales también señalaron un incremento de las limitaciones económicas de los propietarios para acceder a atención veterinaria, en el 69,1% de las respuestas, así como de las lesiones relacionadas con el conflicto, en el 69,9%. Asimismo, el 64,9% percibió una mayor presentación tardía de enfermedades crónicas.

El estudio identificó diferencias entre regiones en aspectos como las lesiones relacionadas con el conflicto y con el vagabundeo, el miedo relacionado con el ruido, la angustia por separación y el cambio general percibido en el bienestar animal. Tras ajustar los resultados por el tipo de centro y el grupo de especies, los profesionales de la región de Kiev presentaron mayores probabilidades de informar de un empeoramiento del bienestar que los de Ucrania central.

Entre los factores que más contribuyeron al deterioro del bienestar animal, los veterinarios señalaron las dificultades económicas de los propietarios, mencionadas por el 87,4% de los participantes. También destacaron el desplazamiento de animales, señalado por el 61,3%, y el aumento de animales callejeros o abandonados, por el 58%.

Los autores señalan que estos resultados muestran que los efectos de la guerra sobre el bienestar animal “van más allá de las lesiones directamente provocadas por el conflicto”. En este contexto, consideran necesario atender el acceso a la atención veterinaria, el apoyo a los animales desplazados y al personal veterinario y la incorporación del bienestar animal a la preparación humanitaria.

El estudio advierte, no obstante, de que sus resultados deben “interpretarse a la luz del método de muestreo no probabilístico, las percepciones retrospectivas y las comparaciones regionales exploratorias”.

#36106. Los veterinarios coinciden sobre el Boxer: “No es una raza ideal para permanecer muchas horas sola porque necesita compañía”

Esta raza necesita compañía, actividad diaria y estimulación mental. Qué puede pasar si pasa muchas horas solo y cómo ayudarlo a adaptarse.

El Boxer es conocido por su energía inagotable, su carácter juguetón y el fuerte lazo que suele formar con su familia. Aunque puede adaptarse a distintos hogares, los veterinarioscoinciden en que no es una raza pensada para pasar largas horas aislada ni sin actividad, ya que necesita compañía y estímulos constantes para mantenerse equilibrado.

Según información veterinaria de VCA Animal Hospitals, los Boxers son perros muy orientados a las personas y tienen una tolerancia limitada a la soledad. Además, requieren actividad física regular para canalizar su energía y evitar el aburrimiento.

Una de las características más marcadas del Boxer es su carácter social. Disfruta de la interacción con sus dueños y de participar en las actividades familiares, por lo que la soledad prolongada puede afectarlo negativamente.

Esto no significa que deba estar acompañado cada minuto, ni que todos los ejemplares reaccionen igual cuando quedan solos. Sin embargo, pasar muchas horas sin interacción, ejercicio o estímulos suele ser problemático para la raza.

La American Kennel Club (AKC) explica que los perros son animales sociales y que el tiempo a solas debe enseñarse de manera gradual. Algunos toleran mejor la soledad que otros, pero el Boxer necesita aprender a quedarse solo de forma progresiva.

Además, el problema no es solo la ausencia de sus dueños: el Boxer es un perro de alta energía y necesita ejercicio diario. Paseos, juegos y actividades variadas ayudan a mantenerlo activo, pero también es fundamental ofrecerle estimulación mental, ya que es un animal inteligente que puede aburrirse si siempre hace lo mismo.

Un Boxer que permanece muchas horas solo y sin suficiente actividad puede mostrar aburrimiento, inquietud o conductas no deseadas. Esto no implica necesariamente que sufra ansiedad por separación, pero sí puede desarrollar comportamientos como ladridos o aullidos excesivos, destrucción de objetos, intentos de escape, jadeo o caminar de un lado a otro.

La AKC diferencia entre un perro aburrido o poco estimulado y uno con un verdadero cuadro de ansiedad por separación. Ante la aparición de estos signos, lo recomendable es consultar con un veterinario o un especialista en comportamiento animal para determinar la causa y buscar una solución adecuada.

Si el perro debe quedarse solo algunas horas, es importante acostumbrarlo de manera gradual. Se recomienda empezar con ausencias cortas e ir aumentándolas poco a poco, en vez de pasar de la compañía total a la soledad prolongada de un día para el otro.

La AKC sugiere proporcionar actividad física antes de salir de casa y dejarle juguetes o recursos que lo mantengan ocupado de forma segura. Un paseo o una sesión de juego previa puede ayudar a que el Boxer llegue al momento de quedarse solo después de haber gastado parte de su energía.

Sin embargo, el ejercicio no reemplaza la compañía. El Boxer necesita una combinación de actividad física, estimulación mental, educación e interacción social. VCA destaca que la raza puede volverse inquieta si no recibe suficiente ejercicio y que el entrenamiento también es una forma de mantenerlo mentalmente activo.

Antes de adoptar un Boxer, es fundamental considerar cuánto tiempo podrá pasar con su familia y qué posibilidades tendrá de salir, jugar y mantenerse activo durante el día.

#36107. حرائق أستراليا.. الهولوكوست التي دمرت الحياة البرية

مجلة عرب أستراليا-سيدني-فرانس 24- تعاني الحيوانات في أستراليا من الحرائق المستعرة إذ باتت أغلب فصائلها مهددة بالانقراض. وتشير التقديرات الأخيرة إلى نفوق أكثر من مليار حيوان، فعلى سبيل المثال وليس الحصر كانت أستراليا تضم نحو 46 ألف حيوان من الكوالا قبل اندلاع الحرائق، لكن لم يبق منها سوى 9 آلاف فقط. كما قامت السلطات بقتل أكثر من خمسة آلاف جمل  خلال حملة تهدف إلى تقليص التهديد الذي تشكله هذه الحيوانات على السكان بسبب انتشار الجفاف.

القضاء على أكثر من خمسة آلاف جمل بري لتلافي تهديدها البيئي

قضى قناصة من مروحيات على أكثر من خمسة آلاف جمل في أستراليا خلال حملة تهدف إلى تقليص التهديد الذي تشكله هذه الحيوانات على السكان مع استمرار انتشار الجفاف في مناطق البلاد الداخلية.وكان مسؤولون محليون في ولاية أستراليا الجنوبية قد أكدوا أن قطعانا “كبيرة للغاية” تقترب بصورة متزايدة من المناطق المأهولة طلبا للماء والغذاء، في تهديد لمخزونات هذه القرى إضافة إلى إلحاق أضرار وتشكيل خطر على السائقين.

حملة القضاء على الحيوانات هذه التي استمرت خمسة أيام انتهت الأحد في أراضي أنانغو بيتجانتجاتجارا يانكونيتجاتجارا، المنطقة الشاسعة الخاضعة لإدارة محلية من مجموعات سكان أصليين في أقصى شمال غرب أستراليا الجنوبية، وفق ما أوضح ريتشارد كينغ المدير العام لأراضي هذه المنطقة التي يعيش فيها نحو 2300 شخص.

نفوق أكثر من مليار حيوان.. والكوالا الأسترالي في خطر

تصل عشرات حيوانات الكوالا يوميا إلى العيادة الميدانية البيطرية في محمية الحيوانات في جزيرة كانغارو في جنوب ولاية أستراليا الجنوبية، محملة بصناديق لنقل القطط أو داخل سلال الغسيل بعد إصابتها بأضرار ناجمة عن حرائق الغابات.

ويشير رئيس فريق الطوارئ البيطرية في العيادة، ستيفن سيلوود، إلى أن أستراليا كانت تضم نحو 46 ألف حيوان من الكوالا قبل اندلاع الحرائق، لكن لم يبق منها سوى 9 آلاف فقط. ويتابع “إنه رقم مدمر”.تأذت النباتات والحيوانات الأسترالية، التي تعد من الأنواع النادرة في العالم، بشكل كبير جراء موجة الحرائق التي انطلقت في أيلول/سبتمبر الماضي. وتشير التقديرات إلى نفوق أكثر من مليار حيوان.

#36108. Digging For Mudfish

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

Mudfish is the most popular fish in places like Nigeria, Benin and Togo and Ghana. Mudfish are freshwater fish that love to live in wetlands or other soggy areas. What’s special about these torpedo-like wee fish is that they can exist without water for months at a time by burying themselves in damp soil, leaf matter, or under tree-roots until it gets wetter again. Mudfish truly are a “fish out of water”, by being able to exist without water for months on end, slowing down their metabolic rate and breathing oxygen through their skin (which, incidentally, isn’t scaly but smooth).

During drier periods, mudfish simply dig deep, wriggle into the soil and hang tough waiting for wetter times to come. They are able to dig deep, due to strengthened bones in their head and a flattened shape to their skull, making them perfectly adapted to shovel down into moist soil to rest until things get wetter.

This kind of fishy hibernation is called aestivation, and unlike hibernating animals who wake up fairly groggy, all it takes to kick these fish back into gear is being immersed in water again – at which point they wriggle away as if they hadn’t just spent the previous two months in suspended animation.

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon, SPNL, has officially released the seventh issue of Al Hima magazine, reaffirming its commitment to advancing community-led conservation and positioning Lebanon as a regional leader in nature-based solutions. This latest edition comes at a critical moment for environmental action in Lebanon and the wider region, bringing together scientific insight, traditional knowledge, and global perspectives under the unifying theme: “From Ridge to Coast, One Hima at a Time.”

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon (SPNL) is dedicated to conserving biodiversity, promoting sustainable development, and safeguarding Lebanon’s natural and cultural heritage. As the national partner of BirdLife International, SPNL embraces the Hima approach, a community-based conservation model that harmonizes nature, people, and culture. Through education, advocacy, and partnerships, we empower communities to protect vital ecosystems and species while ensuring livelihoods and well-being. Join us in our mission to preserve Lebanon's unique environment for future generations. Visit our website or follow us on social media to learn more and support our initiatives. Together, we can make a difference.

Horsh Kayfoun, Luc Hoffmann Hima Center،, Itani, Kayfoun, Mount Lebanon

#36109. A 24-year study uncovers a surprising climate benefit beneath forests

Forest soils are playing a surprisingly powerful role in removing methane from the atmosphere, and new findings suggest that certain effects of climate change could make them even better at it. Methane is a potent greenhouse gas that contributes to global warming, but microorganisms living in the soil can break it down before it builds up in the air.

Scientists from the University of Göttingen and the Baden-Württemberg Forest Research Institute (FVA) analyzed the world's most comprehensive collection of long-term measurements of methane absorption by forest soils. Their findings revealed an unexpected trend: under certain climate conditions that could become more common in the future, forest soils can actually increase the amount of methane they remove from the atmosphere.

The researchers examined measurements collected from 13 forest sites in southwestern Germany over periods of up to 24 years. On average, the amount of methane absorbed by the soils increased by approximately 3% annually. The researchers linked this rise to declining rainfall and warmer temperatures, which create conditions that make it easier for methane to enter the soil and be broken down by microorganisms.

The findings, published in Agricultural and Forest Meteorology, challenge previous research suggesting that climate change is generally reducing the ability of forest soils to absorb methane.

How Scientists Measured Methane Absorption Over 24 Years

To understand how methane absorption changes over time, the researchers examined an extensive collection of soil gas measurements gathered through the FVA's long-running monitoring program.

The measurements included soil gas profiles, which reveal the chemical composition of gases trapped in the soil at different depths. Every two weeks, researchers collected air samples through thin tubes installed beneath the ground and analyzed them in a laboratory. This repeated sampling allowed scientists to track changes in methane concentrations over many years.

To confirm their findings, the team also used an independent measurement technique involving airtight chambers placed directly on the soil surface. By monitoring how gas concentrations changed inside these chambers, the researchers could determine whether the soil was absorbing methane and calculate how much was being removed.

Combining these methods gave the scientists a detailed picture of methane uptake across the forest sites and how it responded to changing environmental conditions.

Why Warmer, Drier Forest Soils Absorb More Methane

Although methane absorption varied considerably between the 13 locations, a clear overall pattern emerged. Forest soils in southwestern Germany were particularly effective at removing methane from the atmosphere during periods of lower rainfall and higher temperatures.

"Our long-term data shows that climate change does not necessarily have a negative impact on how much methane forest soils absorb. While the largest study to date from the US found a decline in methane uptake of up to 80 percent due to increasing rainfall, our significantly more comprehensive field study in south-western Germany found the opposite, " explains Professor Martin Maier at Göttingen University's Department of Crop Sciences, who led the study and was previously involved in the FVA's soil gas monitoring program.

"We observed a significant long-term increase in methane uptake in the forest areas we studied."

The explanation lies partly in how water moves through the ground. When soil becomes saturated with moisture, water fills many of the tiny spaces between soil particles, making it more difficult for gases to move through them. Drier soil contains more pores filled with air, allowing atmospheric methane to travel deeper into the ground.

Once methane enters the soil, microorganisms use it as an energy source, breaking it down in the process. Rising temperatures can slightly accelerate this microbial activity, allowing the organisms to consume methane more quickly.

Together, these two effects help explain why warmer temperatures and reduced rainfall were associated with greater methane absorption in the forests studied.

Findings Challenge Previous Climate Change Research

The results offer a different perspective from several major international research reviews. These analyses, known as meta-analyses, combine findings from numerous individual studies to identify broader patterns. Many have concluded that methane absorption by forest soils is declining.

However, the German study suggests that the relationship between climate change and methane uptake is more complicated than those overall trends indicate. While increasing rainfall can reduce methane absorption by limiting the movement of gas through soil, drier conditions can produce the opposite effect.

The findings do not mean that climate change is beneficial for forests overall. Instead, they demonstrate that its effects on methane absorption can differ considerably depending on local temperatures, rainfall patterns, and soil conditions.

The researchers emphasize that these regional differences make long-term monitoring especially important. Shorter studies or analyses that combine measurements from widely different environments may miss gradual changes that only become apparent after years of observation.

"Our results make it clear that taking a series of measurements over many years and running monitoring programs are indispensable for assessing the real effects of climate change," says Maier.

Con la llegada de octubre, desde la protectora Amo los Animales de Río…

La protectora Amo los Animales de Río Grande, pidió extremar las precauciones al entregar animales domésticos en adopción durante octubre, ante el temor de que puedan ser utilizadas en prácticas rituales, particularmente cuando se trata de animales de pelaje negro o blanco.

Con la llegada de octubre, desde la protectora Amo los Animales de Río Grande lanzaron una advertencia dirigida especialmente a quienes tienen perros y gatos en adopción. El pedido apunta a extremar los cuidados antes de entregar una mascota, particularmente cuando se trata de animales de pelaje negro o blanco.

Marina, integrante de la organización, expresó su preocupación por la posibilidad de que algunos animales sean utilizados en prácticas rituales, una situación que, según advirtió, representa un riesgo para su integridad.

“Lamentablemente hay, no sé si te diría organizaciones, sectas o qué, que utilizan a los animales para hacer rituales, sobre todo gatos negros, perros negros, gatos blancos, perros blancos”, manifestó.

En ese sentido, pidió especial responsabilidad a quienes buscan un hogar para sus mascotas y recomendó no entregarlas sin conocer previamente a las personas interesadas.

“Siempre que haya animales en adopción, sobre todo en este mes, pedimos por favor conciencia de no entregárselo a cualquier persona y, si es posible, que los hagan pasar por la protectora”, sostuvo.

La proteccionista explicó que las organizaciones que trabajan en el rescate animal cuentan con información sobre personas que anteriormente demostraron conductas irresponsables en el cuidado de mascotas.

“También hay gente en lista negra. Siempre tenemos personas que no son responsables, entonces los anotamos para decir: a este no se le da”, señaló.

La advertencia busca reforzar las medidas de prevención, aunque durante la entrevista no se mencionaron casos concretos recientes en Río Grande vinculados con este tipo de prácticas.

Durante la entrevista, Marina también describió la difícil situación económica que atraviesa Amo los Animales, en medio del crecimiento de los rescates y la preocupación por los casos de moquillo entre los perros.

Según explicó, la protectora acumula una deuda superior a los cinco millones de pesos en atención veterinaria, que intenta afrontar mediante donaciones, rifas y aportes solidarios.

“Esto es un trabajo que hacemos nosotras a pulmón, nadie nos ayuda, no recibimos subsidio de ningún lado”, expresó.

También cuestionó la falta de responsabilidad de algunos propietarios, ya que muchos de los animales rescatados tienen dueño e incluso están identificados con microchip, pero sus responsables no se hacen cargo de los gastos veterinarios.

“Entonces el animal es atendido porque lo necesita y porque nosotros estamos para eso, pero la plata la tenemos que recaudar nosotros”, explicó.

Ante este panorama, la organización reiteró el pedido de colaboración a la comunidad, tanto para sostener la atención de los animales rescatados como para promover adopciones responsables.

#36111. Veterinarios coinciden sobre el apego seguro de los gatos: «Muchas veces se nos olvidan cuáles son los pasos que tenemos que dar para aproximarnos a un gato»

El veterinario y divulgador Carlos Gutiérrez recuerda que el vínculo con un gato no depende de gestos inmediatos ni de ideas romantizadas, sino de respeto, paciencia y una buena aproximación

No todos los gatos muestran afecto de la misma manera ni se acercan igual a las personas. De hecho, el veterinario y divulgador Carlos Gutiérrez insiste en una idea clave que a menudo se pasa por alto: para que un gato confíe, primero hay que saber cómo aproximarse a él. De ahí que recuerde que muchas veces se olvidan los pasos básicos necesarios para construir un vínculo sano con un felino.

Frente a la imagen del gato como un animal completamente independiente, Gutiérrez explica que los felinos sí desarrollan lazos con sus cuidadores, lo que se conoce como apego seguro. Este tipo de vínculo implica que el gato percibe a la persona como una figura estable y predecible, alguien que no supone una amenaza y que le ofrece seguridad. No se trata solo de alimento o rutina, sino de una relación basada en la confianza.

Ese apego, sin embargo, no se forma igual en todos los casos. Influyen factores como la socialización en las primeras semanas de vida, el carácter del animal o sus experiencias previas. Hay gatos que se muestran más abiertos desde el primer momento, mientras que otros necesitan más tiempo para observar y adaptarse. Esto se ve con claridad en refugios, donde los animales más tímidos suelen recibir menos atención, pese a tener el mismo potencial para generar un vínculo fuerte.

En este sentido, el especialista también cuestiona la idea extendida de que “el gato elige a su humano”. Aunque puede haber una conexión inicial, reducir la relación a ese momento puede llevar a ignorar a muchos gatos que no se acercan de forma inmediata. Según explica, estos animales no son menos sociables, sino que requieren una aproximación más pausada.

La llegada al hogar es otro punto clave. Incluso un gato que parecía confiado en el refugio puede mostrarse reservado en un entorno nuevo. Por eso, Gutiérrez recomienda no forzar el contacto y permitir que el animal explore a su ritmo. Lo más adecuado es ofrecerle un espacio tranquilo, con acceso fácil a comida, agua y refugio, y dejar que sea él quien dé el primer paso.

A partir de ahí, el vínculo se construye con pequeñas interacciones positivas: responder cuando el gato se acerca, ofrecer caricias suaves en zonas que le resulten agradables o fomentar el juego sin invadir su espacio. Evitar perseguirlo o insistir cuando se retira es fundamental para no generar rechazo.

El experto recuerda además que el afecto felino no siempre se expresa de forma evidente. Un gato puede confiar plenamente en su cuidador sin necesidad de buscar contacto constante. Entender esta forma de relacionarse ayuda a evitar frustraciones y a construir una convivencia más equilibrada.

Respeto, paciencia y atención a las señales del animal son, según Carlos Gutiérrez, los pilares para desarrollar un apego seguro. Más que esperar una respuesta inmediata, se trata de crear las condiciones para que el gato, a su ritmo, encuentre en su entorno y en su humano un espacio de confianza.

#36112. Investigación revela que la comida para gatos tiene metales como plomo y arsénico ¿qué significa y cómo afecta a tu felino?

Los animales son parte fundamental de nuestros hogares, por lo que es importante cuidar la salud de nuestros perros y gatos, así que toma en cuenta lo que reveló una reciente investigación la cual señala que casi todos los alimentos para gatos contienen contaminantes tóxicos derivados del plástico, el herbicida glifosato y metales pesados como…

Los animales son parte fundamental de nuestros hogares, por lo que es importante cuidar la salud de nuestros perros y gatos, así que toma en cuenta lo que reveló una reciente investigación la cual señala que casi todos los alimentos para gatos contienen contaminantes tóxicos derivados del plástico, el herbicida glifosato y metales pesados como plomo, mercurio y arsénico.

Molly Hamilton, autora del informe, destacó que muchas veces los tutores de gatos optan por etiquetas más «limpias» asumiendo que unos precios más elevados e ingredientes de calidad equivalen a un alimento más saludable, sin embargo, muchas veces no es así.

Los metales pesados ​​están presentes de forma natural en el suelo, el agua y el aire, pero la contaminación derivada de actividades agrícolas, industriales y mineras puede aumentar y propagar su presencia. El informe señala que los plásticos y otros contaminantes también pueden incorporarse a los alimentos durante el proceso de fabricación.

Dicho estudio detectó toxinas en todas las variedades de comida para gatos (húmeda, seca y procesada mediante secado al aire o liofilización), incluso en productos etiquetados como naturales y nutricionalmente superiores, según el análisis publicado por Clean Label Project, una organización sin fines de lucro que analiza productos de consumo en busca de contaminantes y certifica aquellos que cumplen con sus estándares.

La investigación analizó 100 de los alimentos para gatos más vendidos en busca de más de 122 contaminantes, incluidos plomo, arsénico, cadmio, mercurio, pesticidas, herbicidas y ftalatos (compuestos químicos presentes en plásticos asociados a un mayor riesgo de muerte en humanos).

Se encontró plomo en el 99% de las muestras, cadmio en el 96% y arsénico en el 94%, abarcando un total de 62 marcas. Los niveles de contaminación variaban según el tipo de producto (húmedo, seco, o bien secado al aire o por congelación) y el tipo de proteína, como aves de corral, carne de res o pescado.

El alimento seco para gatos contenía aproximadamente tres veces más plomo que los productos húmedos o secados al aire.

El informe reveló que el pescado y el marisco presentaban la mayor asociación con la presencia de mercurio y cadmio. Según la Organización Mundial de la Salud, el mercurio es tóxico para los sistemas nervioso, digestivo e inmunitario, así como para los riñones, los pulmones y la piel.

Los niveles de mercurio eran unas 18 veces más altos, y los de cadmio unas 2.5 veces más elevados, en los alimentos para gatos a base de pescado o marisco que en los elaborados con otras proteínas. Las versiones húmedas de los productos a base de marisco contenían la mayor cantidad de mercurio: casi 28 veces más que los alimentos húmedos elaborados con otras carnes, especialmente las de ave.

Casi el 85% de los alimentos a base de aves de corral cumplió con el estándar de pureza del Clean Label Project, frente al 25.7% de los productos de pescado y marisco.

La investigación de Clean Label analizó la presencia de glifosato, el herbicida más utilizado en la agricultura. Se detectaron niveles de glifosato en el 37% de los productos, y aquellos alimentos para gatos que incluían más ingredientes vegetales entre los diez primeros de la lista presentaban niveles más elevados de esta sustancia.

#36113. Los gatos no son tan solitarios como pensamos: qué explicó una especialista

Especialista en comportamiento felino explicó que los gatos pueden formar vínculos estrechos y tener compañeros preferidos, aunque no todos buscan la misma compañía. Los gatos suelen tener fama de ser animales independientes y de preferir la soledad. Sin embargo, esa idea no refleja toda la complejidad de su comportamiento. Mikel Delgado, especialista en comportamiento felino…

Especialista en comportamiento felino explicó que los gatos pueden formar vínculos estrechos y tener compañeros preferidos, aunque no todos buscan la misma compañía.

Los gatos suelen tener fama de ser animales independientes y de preferir la soledad. Sin embargo, esa idea no refleja toda la complejidad de su comportamiento. Mikel Delgado, especialista en comportamiento felino y doctora en Psicología, explicó que su vida social es más compleja de lo que muchas personas creen.

Según Delgado, los gatos pueden establecer vínculos con determinados individuos. Incluso pueden tener compañeros con los que prefieren compartir tiempo y desarrollar relaciones sociales. A estos gatos se los conoce como “preferred associates”.

Hay distintas señales que permiten reconocer cuando existe una relación amistosa entre dos felinos. Una de ellas es el acicalamiento mutuo, una conducta social que aparece entre gatos que tienen un vínculo.

Otra señal es que duerman juntos o permanezcan en contacto. Incluso pueden utilizar el cuerpo del otro como apoyo mientras descansan. También pueden jugar entre ellos y alternar diferentes roles durante esos momentos.

La especialista señaló además que los gatos pueden buscar pasar tiempo juntos. Cuando existe una relación positiva, también suelen mostrar pocos signos de conflicto. Estas conductas permiten observar que no todos los encuentros entre felinos tienen que estar marcados por la distancia.

Sin embargo, esto no significa que todos los gatos necesiten compañía. La investigación científica en la que participó Delgado muestra que existe una gran variabilidad entre los distintos animales. Algunos pueden preferir una vida más solitaria, mientras que otros toleran o disfrutan vivir con otros gatos.

Por eso, sumar otro felino a una casa no garantiza que el animal que ya vive allí vaya a sentirse mejor. La experiencia previa, los recursos disponibles y la personalidad de cada gato pueden influir en la convivencia.

El planteo de Delgado invita a dejar de pensar que todos los gatos se comportan de la misma manera. Más que asumir que necesitan estar solos o acompañados, la clave está en observar qué tipo de interacción busca cada animal y cómo se relaciona con los demás.
