#36114. 攻撃者がGMO利用者の個人情報を公開か、GMOがコメント「調査中」

GMOインターネットグループへの不正アクセスをめぐり、攻撃者のものとみられるサイトに漏えいした可能性のある利用者の個人情報が公開されました。

「声明」というタイトルで始まるサイトは、「我々は正午を期限として提示したが、GMOは交渉に一切応じていない」としたうえで、「予告通り、個人情報を公開する」と書かれています。

公開された情報の真偽はわかっていませんが、特定の人物の氏名や住所などの個人情報が記されています。

さらに、今後も毎日正午に分析が完了した情報から順次公開するとし、100万ドルを支払えば「直ちに、そして永久に活動を停止する」としています。

専門家は攻撃者のものとみられるサイトを閲覧しないよう注意をよびかけています。（ANNニュース）

#36115. Scientists discover a plastic-eating “Pac-Man” enzyme with a surprising secret

Plastic pollution is one of the world's most persistent environmental problems. Vast amounts of discarded plastic accumulate in the oceans, sometimes forming enormous garbage patches that threaten marine life. Most conventional plastics break down extremely slowly because microorganisms struggle to digest the synthetic polymers from which they are made. Instead of disappearing, these materials gradually fragment into increasingly smaller particles, including microplastics and nanoplastics.

Yet plastic waste is not completely beyond the reach of nature. Bacteria and other microorganisms can colonize its surfaces, forming communities known as biofilms. These communities create a distinctive microbial ecosystem that scientists call the "plastisphere."

Researchers at the University of Konstanz have now discovered an unusual enzyme that could reveal how some bacteria are becoming better equipped to break down certain types of plastic. The enzyme can digest particular polyesters and biodegradable plastics, but it has another unexpected ability: it can also break down penicillin, suggesting a possible connection to antibiotic resistance.

Scientists have nicknamed it the "Pac-Man enzyme" because its molecular structure features a remarkably wide opening where chemical reactions take place, known as the active site. The discovery suggests that bacteria may be adapting to digest certain plastic materials more quickly than researchers previously believed.

However, the findings also highlight an important limitation. For microorganisms to make a meaningful difference in reducing plastic pollution, more plastic products would need to be made from biodegradable materials that contain chemical bonds microbes can break apart.

Testing How Bacteria Break Down Bioplastics

To investigate how microorganisms digest biodegradable plastics, University of Konstanz biologists Harry Lerner and David Schleheck studied the entire process of microbial decomposition. They wanted to determine not only whether the materials could be completely broken down, but also which microorganisms were responsible and what biological mechanisms allowed them to do it.

The researchers examined the composition of the microbial communities involved and analyzed their collective genetic information (the metagenome). This approach allowed them to search for genes that might produce enzymes capable of breaking apart plastic polymers.

Their experiments focused on long-chain aliphatic polyesters (LCAP), a class of biodegradable plastic materials developed by a research team led by chemist Stefan Mecking. These materials contain chemical bonds that certain microorganisms can potentially break down, unlike the more resistant structures found in many conventional plastics.

The collaborative findings were published in The ISME Journal.

To observe how the bioplastics behaved in a natural environment, the team placed samples beneath the surface of forest soil at the university's botanical garden.

"We buried small pieces of LCAP bioplastic film in the upper humus layer in the forest at the university's botanical garden, about ten centimeters deep," explains Harry Lerner.

"This layer is where the breakdown of cellulose and other natural polymers, such as cutin - a plant-based polyester - takes place."

Humus is the upper layer of soil rich in decomposing organic matter, where microorganisms play a major role in recycling nutrients. Cellulose is the main structural component of plant cell walls, while cutin is a naturally occurring polyester that helps form the protective outer covering of plants.

The researchers selected this environment because its microorganisms already possess the biological machinery needed to break down complex natural materials.

Bioplastics Disappeared Within About a Year

Alongside the outdoor experiment, the scientists carried out laboratory tests using soil collected from the same forest. They mixed powdered bioplastics into the soil and monitored microbial activity over the course of an entire year.

One important measurement was the amount of carbon dioxide, or CO2, released from the samples. As microorganisms consume and metabolize organic materials, they produce carbon dioxide through respiration. Tracking this gas helped the researchers determine how actively the microbes were breaking down the plastics.

The buried plastic films remained in the forest soil for a full year, allowing the team to compare decomposition under natural conditions with the carefully monitored laboratory experiments.

To assess the results, the scientists also tested cellulose, other biodegradable plastics, conventional polyethylene plastic, and soil samples without added materials.

"Cellulose, other types of bioplastics such as PHBV and PCL, as well as high-density plastic (HDPE) and untreated soil were used as controls in the laboratory. We found that all bioplastic materials were completely degraded within roughly 250 to 330 days. Cellulose broke down after about 80 days, whereas virtually no degradation occurred for HDPE," says Lerner.

The difference was striking. While the biodegradable plastics were completely broken down within approximately nine to eleven months, conventional high-density polyethylene showed almost no decomposition.

These results demonstrate why the chemical makeup of plastic matters so much. Some polymers contain bonds that microbial enzymes can attack, allowing bacteria to dismantle them into smaller molecules. Others, including widely used polyethylene, are far more resistant to biological degradation.

Mysterious Holes Reveal How Bacteria Eat Plastic

When the researchers recovered the plastic films from the forest soil, they discovered something unexpected.

Using electron microscopy, which can reveal structures far too small to see with an ordinary light microscope, the team examined the surfaces of the buried materials. They found numerous tiny holes, each approximately matching the dimensions and shape of an individual bacterial cell.

The unusual pattern suggested that bacteria were doing more than simply attaching themselves to the plastic. They appeared to be breaking into the material itself.

"We hypothesized that bacteria are coated with plastic-depolymerases anchored to their cell surfaces. This would enable them to digest their way into the material and become embedded within the film, leaving behind microscopic holes of exactly this type," explains Lerner.

Depolymerases are enzymes that break large molecules called polymers into smaller chemical units. If these enzymes are attached directly to the outside of a bacterium, they could allow the microorganism to digest the surrounding material while remaining in close contact with it.

This would help explain the microscopic cavities observed in the plastic films. Rather than relying entirely on enzymes released into the environment, the bacteria might be able to break down the material directly at their surfaces.

Scientists Identify the Plastic-Eating "Pac-Man Enzyme"

To find the biological machinery behind this unusual behavior, Lerner extracted DNA from the entire community of microorganisms living in the soil samples.

He then sequenced the genetic material and carefully analyzed the extensive data to determine which microbes and genes became especially abundant as the bioplastics decomposed.

One gene stood out. It was strongly enriched specifically in forest soil containing LCAP, suggesting that it could play an important role in breaking down the biodegradable polyester.

Further analysis revealed that the gene produces an esterase, a type of enzyme capable of splitting certain chemical bonds found in esters. These bonds are present in many naturally occurring substances as well as in various biodegradable polyesters.

What made this particular enzyme especially interesting was how it was positioned on bacterial cells.

Its genetic instructions included a secretion signal that directs the enzyme outside the cell, along with a lipid anchor that holds it firmly to the cell membrane.

This arrangement means the enzyme can remain attached to the bacterium's surface while attacking nearby plastic molecules. Its unusually exposed active site also provides space for the enzyme to interact with polymer chains.

The researchers had identified the molecular mechanism they were looking for, along with the enzyme they named "Pac-Man."

A Surprising Connection to Antibiotic Resistance

The discovery became even more intriguing when scientists examined the enzyme's molecular structure.

Although it resembled esterases, which are known for breaking down ester bonds, it also shared structural similarities with another important family of bacterial enzymes: beta-lactamases.

These enzymes are well known for their role in antibiotic resistance. Some bacteria produce beta-lactamases that disable antibiotics such as penicillin by breaking open a critical part of their chemical structure, known as the beta-lactam ring.

"Its structure resembles that of esterases, but also that of beta-lactamases, which are bacterial enzymes that are capable of cleaving the beta-lactam ring of certain antibiotics, such as penicillin, thereby making bacteria resistant to antibiotics," says Lerner.

Laboratory experiments confirmed that the newly discovered enzyme was capable of performing both types of chemical reactions.

It could break polyester chains into their individual building blocks, known as monomers, while also cleaving penicillin.

This dual activity was particularly surprising because plastic degradation and antibiotic breakdown are usually considered separate biological functions.

The finding raises the possibility that some enzymes involved in plastic decomposition could also contribute to antibiotic resistance. However, demonstrating that an enzyme can chemically break down penicillin in the laboratory does not establish that it provides antibiotic resistance to living bacteria.

Could Plastic-Eating Bacteria Help Solve Plastic Pollution?

The discovery also raises a broader question about how rapidly microorganisms can adapt to synthetic materials introduced into their environment.

For most of Earth's history, plastic pollution did not exist. Microorganisms evolved enzymes to digest naturally occurring substances, including plant fibers and other complex organic molecules.

Modern plastics have introduced an enormous new collection of potential carbon sources into the environment, but many of these materials remain chemically inaccessible to microbes.

"The plastisphere is a new habitat in our environment," explains Schleheck.

"Humans have only been introducing plastic into the environment in significant quantities for around 50 to 75 years. Since then, it has theoretically been available to microbial communities - such as bacteria, yeasts and fungi - as an additional source of carbon and energy for their growth. By 'theoretically', I mean that they would certainly like to use the plastic as a growth substrate - but they cannot, because the materials are actually indigestible to microbial metabolism and are therefore hardly degraded."

The Pac-Man enzyme suggests that this barrier may not be absolute for every type of plastic.

Some bacteria appear capable of using specialized enzymes to attack biodegradable polyesters, potentially allowing them to exploit these materials as sources of energy and nutrients.

For scientists studying plastic pollution, the finding offers a promising clue about how biological degradation might be improved. It also suggests that microbial communities could be adapting to certain synthetic polymers more rapidly than previously anticipated.

However, the results do not mean that bacteria are suddenly capable of consuming the enormous amounts of conventional plastic already accumulating in the environment. The experiments showed that polyethylene remained largely unaffected, even as the biodegradable plastics decomposed.

Instead, the researchers emphasize the importance of designing materials that microorganisms can actually digest.

"I find this encouraging, because it seems that bacteria can adapt to breaking down polyester plastics more quickly than we expected. To tackle the environmental problem of plastic pollution, we humans need to work with the capabilities of microbes. Ideally, this would involve using only polymers with biochemical breaking points, such as the hydrolysable ester bonds in polyesters like LCAP or other types of bioplastics," concludes Schleheck.

The discovery offers a glimpse of a potentially more sustainable approach to plastic production. Rather than expecting microorganisms to break down materials that are naturally resistant to decomposition, scientists could develop plastics with chemical structures that make biological recycling possible.

The Pac-Man enzyme illustrates how bacteria can exploit these molecular weak points, offering new insight into the relationship between plastic pollution, microbial adaptation, and the future of biodegradable materials.

#36116. تعرف على خطة أستراليا للتخلص من القطط الوحشية بـ “النقانق”

مجلة عرب أستراليا- سيدنى – يورونيوز- ابتكرت الحكومة الأسترالية طريقة مثيرة لقتل القطط الوحشية، بدأت بإلقاء النقانق السامة عبر آلاف الهكتارات من الأراضي في محاولة لقتل ملايين القطط الوحشية.وتتبع أستراليا هذه الطريقة إلى جانب طرق أخرى، كنصب الفخاخ والقتل بالرصاص، للتخلص من مليوني قط وحشي – نحو ثلث العدد الإجمالي- بحلول عام 2020 سعياً منها لحماية وإنقاذ نحو 124 نوعاً من الحيوانات البرية المحلية المهددة بالانقراض.وتعمل الحكومة على تقديم 5 ملايين دولار لمجموعات محلية لتجنيدها في المعركة.

بحسب صحيفة نيويورك تايمز فإن القطط تموت في غضون 15 دقيقة بعد تناول النقانق المصنوعة من لحم الكنغر ودهون الدجاج والأعشاب والتوابل والسم 1080، وهو سم يستخدم في قطاع الزراعة في بعض بلدان العالم لمكافحة القوارض والتحكم بكثافة تكاثر بعض أنواع الحيوانات الغازية.وتسقط طائرات السجق المسموم في المناطق التي تتجول فيها القطط المتوحشة بمسافة فاصلة تبلغ 50 كيلومتراً بين كل نقطة إسقاط.ويبدو أن القطط لا تستطيع مقاومة النقانق لطعمها اللذيذ، يقول أحد مطوري وصفة السم، دكتور ديف إلغار، إنه اختبر طعم النقانق مع قططه للتحقق من النكهة، ولكن طبعاً قبل إضافة السم!

وفقاً لدراسة نشرت في مجلة “بايولوجيكال كونسيرفايشن “Biological Conservation فإن القطط الوحشية تتسبب بنفوق ملايين الحيوانات سنوياً في أستراليا.فتقتل 377 مليون طائر و649 مليون من الزواحف سنوياً في أستراليا، كل قط منها يقوم بالقضاء على نحو 1000 حيوان محلي سنوياً.

بحسب غريغوري أندروز، المفوض الوطني للأنواع المهددة بالانقراض لصحيفة سيدني مورنينغ هيرالد، فإنه منذ جلب المستوطنين الأوروبيين القطط الوحشية للمرة الأولى، والتي تشكل أكبر تهديد للحيوانات المحلية في البلاد، ساهمت القطط الوحشية بدفع ما يقرب من 20 نوعًا من الثدييات للانقراض.

تؤكد أستراليا أن المستهدف هو القطط الوحشية وليس الأليفة، وأن ما تقوم به الحكومة تقوم به لأنه ضروري وليس لأنها تريد ذلك.ومع هذا قوبل إعلان الحكومة عن حملتها للقضاء على القطط عام 2015 ردود فعل محلية ودولية غاضبة.فمحلياً تواجه الحكومة انتقادات من المحافظين الذين يزعمون بأن الحكومة تركز على مكافحة القطط عوضاً عن معالجة عوامل أخرى تقلل التنوع البيولوجي وتهدده كالتوسع الحضري وإزالة الغابات والتعدين.

في حين وقع أكثر من 160 ألف شخص على بضعة عرائض عبر الإنترنت تدعو أستراليا إلى التخلي عن الخطة، من ضمنهم الفنانة الفرنسية والناشطة في مجال حقوق الحيوان بريجيت باردو التي وجهت رسالة إلى الحكومة الأسترالية داعية إياها إلى وقف الإبادة الجماعية للحيوانات على حد تعبيرها.

#36117. We are not alone – alien plants & animals invade Europe

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

As of today, leading European experts will gather for three days of intensive discussion in Luxembourg to assess the spread of alien lifeforms among us. Top of the agenda will be an updated appraisal of the current alien invasion risk and a resolution outlining high priority security measures – biosecurity measures that is. For the menace tackled at the NEOBIOTA International Conference[1] comes not from Outer Space, but flora and fauna non-native to the EU.

We Are Not Alone. The consortium of scientists and environmental managers that form NEOBIOTA (the European Group on Biological Invasions) warn that the EU harbors up to 1800 different IAS (Invasive Alien Species) along with their rapidly growing populations: from the pesky South American coypu (Myocastor coypus) troubling Germany and the incorrigible North American grey squirrel (Sciurus carolinensis) infesting Italy to the burrowing Chinese mitten crab (Eriocheir sinensis) that has aggressively spread from the Kowloon Peninsula to the banks of the Thames.

But if these aliens seem less menacing than flying saucers and little green men, think again. In many cases, their negative consequences are harshly felt in at least three areas of major concern for European citizens today – the economy, human health and the environment.

As rude disruptors of local ecosystems, invasive species are one of the four great drivers of extinction (along with habitat change, over-exploitation and pollution). Alarmingly, they have been an essential factor in most bird extinctions over the past century and they contribute to the current plight of 44% of the IUCN’s Red List of critically endangered species. Many carry a Molotov cocktail of diseases with severe implications for native wildlife, livestock, domestic pets and, in some cases, humans. The European Commission estimates that invasions of alien species cause at least €12.5 billion worth of damage to the union’s economy annually.[2]

Nature knows no borders: the failure of any one Member State to tackle IAS puts our shared ecological homeland at risk. A solution has to be found at a pan-European level – after all, it makes sense that a common market needs common biosecurity. Fortunately, the EU – eager to meet its popular biodiversity targets by 2020 – has committed itself to supranational action. The recent launch in July of its list of 37 ‘Invasive Alien Species of Union Concern’ was enthusiastically welcomed.[3]

And none too soon. As researchers from the University of Turin will tell NEOBIOTA 2016, one of the list’s most notorious species, the yellow-legged hornet (Vespa velutina) – dubbed the ‘Asian killer hornet’ following several human fatalities – is rapidly spreading.[4]Since first docking in France in 2004 – a trade stowaway on ceramic pottery imports from southeast Asia – this venomous visitor has spread to neighboring Spain, Portugal, Germany and, more recently, Italy.

The Turin study hammers home the timely nature of recent EU regulations regarding invasion pathways and restrictions on the keeping, importing, selling and breeding of the black list ‘37’. However, as good as the laws are, they urgently need to be extended to cover many more, equally threatening species. NEOBIOTA is expected to call on the European Commission to add literally hundreds more than just the proposed additional 17 species to the list in 2017, though some contenders may prove too controversial.

In the case of the raccoon dog (Nyctereutes procyonoides), an increasingly popular exotic pet, cuteness is the enemy of reason. The species, as research recently published in PLOS One urgently reminds us, is one of the main vectors of rabies in Europe.[5] Yet, it has been allowed to continue its rampant march across the continent unabated. Its adaptability, reproduction rates and lack of natural predators have multiplied its numbers and if left unchecked, it is likely to become one of the most numerous, widespread and disease-ridden predators in Europe. If the ecological threat posed by the raccoon dog to endangered native species is not enough motivation to put it on the updated IAS list, then the EU should at the very least recognize that it’s all ‘fun, fur and games’ till someone gets lock-jaw.

Climate change, NEOBIOTA warns, will dramatically increase the negative impacts of raccoon dog invasions and those of other alien species. The time for action is now. With both nature and people at risk, the EU would be wise to listen. As the saying goes, ‘Once bitten, twice shy.’

View the NEOBIOTA RESOLUTION ON THE EU INVASIVE ALIEN SPECIES REGULATION: The Time for Action is NOW!

http://www.neobiota2016.org/resolution-neobiota-2016.pdf

[2] Kettunen M, Genovesi P, Gollasch S, Pagad S, Starfinger U, Ten Brink P and Shine C (2008), Technical support to EU strategy on invasive species (IAS) – Assessment of the impacts of IAS in Europe and the EU (final module report for the European Commission). Institute for European Environmental Policy (IEEP), Brussels, Belgium. 44 pp. + Annexes.

[3] http://ec.europa.eu/environment/pdf/13_07_2016_news_en.pdf

[4] Bertolino S, Lioy S, Laurino D, Manino A, Porporato M, et al. (2016) Spread of the invasive yellow-legged hornet Vespa velutina (Hymenoptera: Vespidae) in Italy. The Japanese Society of Applied Entomology and Zoology. DOI 10.1007/s13355-016-0435-2

[5] Drygala F, Korablev N, Ansorge H, Fickel J, Isomursu M, Elmeros M, et al. (2016) Homogenous Population Genetic Sructure of the Non-Native Raccoon Dog (Nyctereutes procyonoides) in Europe as a Result of Rapid Population Expansion. PLoS ONE 11(4): e0153098. DOI:10.1371/journal. pone.0153098

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon, SPNL, has officially released the seventh issue of Al Hima magazine, reaffirming its commitment to advancing community-led conservation and positioning Lebanon as a regional leader in nature-based solutions. This latest edition comes at a critical moment for environmental action in Lebanon and the wider region, bringing together scientific insight, traditional knowledge, and global perspectives under the unifying theme: “From Ridge to Coast, One Hima at a Time.”

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon (SPNL) is dedicated to conserving biodiversity, promoting sustainable development, and safeguarding Lebanon’s natural and cultural heritage. As the national partner of BirdLife International, SPNL embraces the Hima approach, a community-based conservation model that harmonizes nature, people, and culture. Through education, advocacy, and partnerships, we empower communities to protect vital ecosystems and species while ensuring livelihoods and well-being. Join us in our mission to preserve Lebanon's unique environment for future generations. Visit our website or follow us on social media to learn more and support our initiatives. #36118. Tui-Na para gatos: qué es el masaje chino y qué se sabe de sus beneficios

La técnica utiliza maniobras manuales sobre músculos, tejidos y puntos de acupresión. En veterinaria se la emplea como terapia complementaria. Qué dice la evidencia científica.

El Tui-Na es una técnica de terapia manual originada en la tradición médica china que también se aplica en medicina veterinaria. En gatos, utiliza distintas maniobras con las manos, como presión, amasado y deslizamientos, y puede trabajar sobre tejidos blandos y puntos utilizados dentro de la medicina tradicional china.

A diferencia de la acupuntura, no utiliza agujas. Su aplicación en animales pequeños está descrita en publicaciones especializadas de medicina veterinaria tradicional china y se plantea principalmente como una herramienta complementaria para determinados problemas musculoesqueléticos.

Patricia Paredes (MP 7387), médica veterinaria del equipo de Natural Life, explica que la técnica requiere movimientos suaves y progresivos. El gato, además, debe determinar en buena medida qué maniobras tolera y hasta qué nivel de presión puede avanzarse. El trabajo comienza de manera superficial y aumenta gradualmente, siempre que el animal lo acepte.

Este punto adquiere especial importancia en una especie que suele manifestar el dolor y el estrés mediante cambios sutiles de conducta. Las guías de manejo del dolor felino señalan que la observación del comportamiento es una herramienta central para detectar molestias.

Por eso, la especialista aclara que una sesión no debería plantearse como un masaje convencional aplicado de manera automática. La manipulación debe adaptarse al estado del animal y formar parte, cuando corresponda, de una evaluación veterinaria.

El término proviene de los vocablos chinos Tui (“empujar”) y Na (“agarrar”), dos de las maniobras fundamentales de esta práctica. A diferencia de un masaje relajante tradicional, el Tui-Na es una terapia manual orientada a reequilibrar el flujo del Qi (la energía vital) a través de los meridianos del cuerpo del animal.

Mediante combinaciones de amasado suave (Rou), presión digital (An) sobre puntos de acupuntura específicos y deslizamientos a lo largo de la columna vertebral, esta disciplina logra trabajar músculos, articulaciones y órganos internos sin la necesidad de utilizar agujas.

Dentro de la medicina veterinaria tradicional china, el Tui-Na se utiliza especialmente en cuadros musculoesqueléticos y como complemento de otros tratamientos. La literatura especializada también describe su uso sobre articulaciones, músculos y tejidos blandos.

En gatos con enfermedades articulares, el objetivo puede ser acompañar el abordaje del dolor y favorecer la movilidad. Sin embargo, no debe presentarse como un tratamiento probado para la artrosis ni como reemplazo de los recursos de analgesia y rehabilitación indicados por un veterinario.

La medicina felina actual recomienda planes de tratamiento individualizados y multimodales para el dolor, que pueden combinar medicamentos, modificaciones ambientales y otras intervenciones no farmacológicas.

La popularidad de las terapias manuales en veterinaria no significa que todos sus beneficios estén demostrados con el mismo nivel de evidencia.

Una revisión sistemática sobre movilización de tejidos blandos, masajes y estiramientos en animales de compañía concluyó que los estudios disponibles son escasos, heterogéneos y, en muchos casos, presentan riesgo de sesgo. Por eso, los autores consideraron que la evidencia todavía no permite establecer con solidez la eficacia clínica de estas técnicas.

Algo similar ocurre con otras prácticas de la medicina veterinaria integrativa. Las revisiones sobre acupuntura en animales señalan que existen resultados alentadores en algunas áreas, pero también remarcan la necesidad de ensayos clínicos de mayor calidad para determinar su eficacia.

Una de las principales particularidades del Tui-Na felino es que la aceptación del animal forma parte del procedimiento. La suavidad y la adaptación a la respuesta del gato aparecen como recomendaciones centrales en la literatura especializada sobre esta práctica.

La manipulación también debe realizarse con criterio veterinario, especialmente cuando existe dolor, una lesión, una enfermedad crónica o cambios recientes en el comportamiento.

Paredes remarca que nunca debe aplicarse la fuerza y que este tipo de terapias deben ser administradas por profesionales capacitados en acupuntura o fisioterapia veterinaria.

Uno de los movimientos más comunes del Tui-Na felino recorre el meridiano de la vejiga, que corre en paralelo a la columna vertebral. En la medicina china, este meridiano conecta con puntos que regulan directamente cada uno de los órganos internos. Por eso, acariciar suavemente esa zona suele provocar una relajación casi instantánea en los gatos.

Durante las sesiones de Tui-Na, es habitual que el gato comience a ronronear. Las frecuencias del ronroneo felino (entre 20 y 140 Hz) tienen propiedades curativas comprobadas para la densidad ósea y la reparación de tejidos, lo que genera un efecto sinérgico con el masaje.

Aunque su aplicación formal en felinos domésticos se ha popularizado en las últimas décadas, el Tui-Na como disciplina médica tiene registros en China que se remontan a la Dinastía Shang (alrededor del 1700 a. C.), donde inicialmente se utilizaba en humanos y animales de trabajo como los caballos.

Muchos gatos no toleran las agujas de la acupuntura tradicional debido al estrés que les causa la contención. El Tui-Na es considerado la alternativa perfecta, ya que permite estimular exactamente los mismos puntos pero utilizando únicamente la yema de los dedos.

Somos una organización no gubernamental sin fines de lucro abocada a difundir, proteger y promover los derechos de los animales

#36119. La enfermedad periodontal podría aumentar el riesgo cardiovascular en perros

Un amplio estudio observacional encontró una asociación entre la gravedad de la enfermedad periodontal y un mayor riesgo posterior de endocarditis y miocardiopatías en perros. Los resultados refuerzan la importancia de la prevención y el cuidado de la salud oral. La enfermedad periodontal es uno de los problemas de salud más frecuentes en los perros…

Un amplio estudio observacional encontró una asociación entre la gravedad de la enfermedad periodontal y un mayor riesgo posterior de endocarditis y miocardiopatías en perros. Los resultados refuerzan la importancia de la prevención y el cuidado de la salud oral.

La enfermedad periodontal es uno de los problemas de salud más frecuentes en los perros y su prevalencia aumenta con la edad. Además de afectar las estructuras que sostienen los dientes, la inflamación periodontal puede estar asociada con alteraciones sistémicas.

Para evaluar esta posible relación, investigadores analizaron los registros clínicos de 118.592 perros: 59.296 con enfermedad periodontal y otros 59.296 sin antecedentes de la enfermedad. Dentro de la cohorte periodontal, 23.043 perros presentaban enfermedad en estadio 1, 20.732 en estadio 2 y 15.521 en estadio 3.

El análisis encontró una relación significativa entre una mayor gravedad de la enfermedad periodontal y el riesgo posterior de determinadas afecciones cardiovasculares. La asociación más marcada correspondió a la endocarditis: los perros con enfermedad periodontal en estadio 3 presentaron aproximadamente seis veces más riesgo de desarrollar esta afección que aquellos sin enfermedad periodontal.

También se observaron asociaciones con miocardiopatía dilatada, miocardiopatía hipertrófica, insuficiencia de la válvula mitral y alteraciones del ritmo cardíaco, entre otros eventos cardiovasculares evaluados.

Los autores plantean que la inflamación crónica y la posible presencia de bacterias o productos bacterianos provenientes de la cavidad oral podrían participar en la conexión entre la enfermedad periodontal y alteraciones sistémicas. Sin embargo, el estudio fue observacional y no permite establecer por sí solo una relación causal.

El trabajo también señala importantes limitaciones. Entre ellas, la estadificación periodontal fue clínica y no requirió confirmación radiográfica ni medición de la profundidad de las bolsas, mientras que los diagnósticos cardiovasculares quedaron sujetos al criterio de los veterinarios durante la atención primaria. Además, por tratarse de un estudio retrospectivo, no fue posible evaluar de manera adecuada el efecto de los tratamientos periodontales posteriores al diagnóstico.

Por eso, los resultados deben interpretarse como una asociación epidemiológica que requiere confirmación mediante nuevas investigaciones, y no como una demostración de que la enfermedad periodontal cause enfermedad cardíaca.

Aun así, el estudio aporta evidencia para considerar la salud oral como parte integral del cuidado veterinario preventivo. Los autores destacan la importancia de los controles odontológicos, la higiene oral y la evaluación de la cavidad bucal durante las distintas etapas de la vida del perro.

Somos una organización no gubernamental sin fines de lucro abocada a difundir, proteger y promover los derechos de los animales

#36120. José Luis Puchol, veterinario (72 años): «En mi época los perros vivían 8 años o 10 como mucho; hoy todos los perros pasan de los 12 años»

La esperanza de vida de los perros ha cambiado de forma notable en las últimas décadas. El veterinario José Luis Puchol, con más de cuatro décadas de experiencia profesional, recuerda que cuando comenzó a ejercer muchos perros apenas superaban los 8 o 10 años. La evolución de la medicina veterinaria y de los cuidados ha…

La esperanza de vida de los perros ha cambiado de forma notable en las últimas décadas.

El veterinario José Luis Puchol, con más de cuatro décadas de experiencia profesional, recuerda que cuando comenzó a ejercer muchos perros apenas superaban los 8 o 10 años. La evolución de la medicina veterinaria y de los cuidados ha modificado esa realidad.

Puchol compara su experiencia clínica con la situación actual para explicar el aumento de la longevidad canina. Según relata, cuando era joven lo habitual era que los perros vivieran alrededor de 8 años y que fueran pocos los que alcanzaran los 10. Actualmente, afirma que la mayoría supera los 12 años y que muchos llegan a los 14 o 16.

El veterinario relaciona esta evolución con la mejora de los cuidados y de la medicina veterinaria. El diagnóstico de enfermedades, la disponibilidad de tratamientos y el seguimiento de los animales permiten intervenir ante problemas de salud que anteriormente podían tener consecuencias más graves.

El propio Puchol también señala que el tamaño del perro influye en su esperanza de vida. En su intervención en el pódcast Rompiendo el molde, resume esta diferencia con una afirmación directa: «Los pequeños viven más».

La relación entre tamaño y longevidad aparece como uno de los aspectos destacados por el veterinario. Puchol diferencia entre los perros de menor tamaño y los ejemplares grandes al hablar de los años que pueden alcanzar.

La genética también desempeña un papel relevante en la salud de los animales. Cada raza presenta unas características y una predisposición determinada a desarrollar ciertas enfermedades, por lo que la esperanza de vida no puede reducirse a una cifra idéntica para todos los perros.

A estos factores se suma la evolución de la atención veterinaria. Las revisiones permiten detectar alteraciones antes de que los síntomas sean evidentes y establecer tratamientos cuando todavía existe margen para controlar determinadas patologías.

La relación entre las familias y sus animales de compañía también ha evolucionado. Puchol explica que antes el perro ocupaba un papel diferente dentro del hogar, mientras que actualmente los propietarios conceden mayor importancia a su salud y bienestar.

Ese cambio social tiene consecuencias en la atención veterinaria. Los propietarios buscan diagnósticos, tratamientos y procedimientos que anteriormente no formaban parte de las posibilidades habituales para un animal de compañía.

La evolución tecnológica de las clínicas ha ampliado además las herramientas disponibles para atender problemas complejos.

José Luis Puchol es el fundador, director general y responsable del Servicio de Cirugía Avanzada y Traumatología del Hospital Veterinario Puchol. Se licenció en la Universidad Complutense de Madrid en 1979 y ha desarrollado su carrera en distintas especialidades veterinarias, entre ellas la oftalmología, la cirugía oncológica, la traumatología y la neurocirugía.

Durante 38 años dirigió la Clínica Veterinaria Puerta de Hierro, donde ejerció como especialista y compartió técnicas quirúrgicas con otros profesionales. También participó en la fundación de AMVAC en 1983, asociación de la que llegó a ocupar la presidencia.

En octubre de 2021 recibió el título de Presidente de Honor del Colegio de Veterinarios de Madrid. En 2023 obtuvo, además, el premio Salud Animal de España en la categoría de Animales de Compañía, otorgado por Animal’s Health.

#36121. A fungus is threatening the world’s food supply. Scientists just found a new defense

Scientists have uncovered a promising new way to protect some of the world's most important food crops from a destructive fungus that threatens global food security. By engineering the natural immune defenses of plants, researchers have developed an experimental approach that could eventually help wheat, rice, and barley resist fungal infections more effectively.

The international research team, led by scientists at the John Innes Centre in the United Kingdom, investigated a recently discovered group of plant immune receptors. They identified how these proteins recognize invading pathogens and demonstrated that they can be modified to detect multiple fungal threats. The findings could open the door to crops with stronger, more durable disease resistance.

"We've discovered something that could lead to a potential intervention on what is the worst fungal disease of cereals," said Professor Mark Banfield, a research group leader at the John Innes Centre and an author of the study, which involved collaborators at Kobe University in Japan.

A Devastating Fungus Threatens the Global Food Supply

The target of the research is Magnaporthe oryzae, the fungus responsible for blast disease. It causes the most serious fungal disease affecting cultivated rice and has also become a major threat to wheat and barley in parts of Asia and Africa.

The damage is enormous. Each year, rice blast destroys enough rice to feed approximately 60 million people. As the fungus spreads into additional agricultural regions, developing effective ways to protect crops has become increasingly important for global food security.

The fungus infects plants through a sophisticated biological strategy. It delivers specialized molecules known as effectors into cells in the leaves and stems. These molecules interfere with normal plant functions, allowing the pathogen to establish an infection and promote disease.

Plants, however, have evolved their own defense systems. Specialized immune receptors can recognize these foreign molecules and trigger a protective response. One important mechanism involves causing infected cells to die in a controlled, localized area, helping prevent the pathogen from spreading farther through the plant.

Scientists Discover a Hidden Plant Immune Defense

For more than three decades, much of the research into plant immunity has centered on proteins called Nucleotide-binding and leucine-rich receptors (NLRs). These receptors have played a central role in understanding how crops recognize and defend themselves against blast disease and other infections.

More recently, scientists have begun investigating another group of immune proteins found in cereal crops, known as tandem kinase proteins (TKPs). Although researchers are still learning how these receptors operate, they appear to offer additional opportunities for strengthening plant defenses.

Earlier studies revealed that some TKPs contain a specialized region called a Heavy Metal Associated (HMA) domain. Similar regions are also found in certain NLR receptors, where they help recognize pathogen effectors and initiate immune responses.

This shared feature attracted the attention of Banfield and his colleagues because it suggested that scientists might be able to redesign the receptors to recognize additional fungal threats.

"This piqued our interest," said Professor Banfield. "If we can engineer these integrated HMA domains for new properties by making amino acid protein changes where effectors bind, we can gain what we call novel recognition specificities - and potentially a new frontline for defense of plants against disease."

Turning Fungal Attack Molecules Into Targets

In the study, published in Science Advances, researchers examined precisely how HMA domains interact with the molecules released by the blast fungus.

Using biophysical techniques and crystallography, a method that allows scientists to determine the detailed three-dimensional structure of molecules, the team obtained a high-resolution view of how these plant receptors bind to fungal effectors.

The results revealed that HMA domains can function as biological bait. By binding to molecules the fungus uses during infection, these regions help plants recognize the presence of an invading pathogen and activate their defenses.

Armed with this structural information, the researchers modified TKP immune receptors so they could recognize two different fungal effectors associated with infections in wheat and barley.

This ability to detect multiple targets, known as dual specificity, is unusual in naturally occurring immune receptors. Demonstrating that it can be introduced through protein engineering suggests a promising way to expand the range of threats that crop immune systems can recognize.

The findings also provide a foundation for investigating other recently identified TKPs in different cereal crops. Scientists may eventually be able to design customized immune receptors that detect several fungal effectors simultaneously, potentially making plants more difficult for pathogens to infect.

Engineering Crops With Stronger Disease Resistance

Although the findings are encouraging, the technology remains at an early experimental stage.

The researchers conducted their initial tests using protoplasts, individual wheat cells that serve as a simplified model for studying how plant tissues respond. The next step is to determine whether the engineered receptors can provide effective protection in whole plants grown under controlled greenhouse conditions.

"Our study indicates that this new class of resistance proteins is amenable for engineering purposes. We now know that the previous tools we used in engineering NLR receptors may also work with this new class," said Dr. Daniel Yu, the first author of the study.

Understanding exactly how fungal effectors attach to plant immune receptors could also help researchers take advantage of other technologies. Precision breeding and artificial intelligence may provide additional ways to redesign HMA domains, allowing them to recognize pathogen molecules that previously escaped detection.

One particularly promising possibility involves combining different types of immune receptors within the same crop.

Because NLRs and TKPs appear to activate plant defenses through different mechanisms, using them together could create several layers of protection. This might make it harder for fungi to evolve ways around a plant's immune system.

"We think these two classes of immune receptors, NLRs and TKPs, are probably activated in different ways," said Professor Banfield. "That gives the potential for more robust immunity in the field because you are not putting all your eggs in one basket. Further down the road, we may be able to use techniques to stack genes encoding TKPs and NLRs together against the same disease, making a super-resistant crop. They will have different ways of activating immunity, so it is likely to be more difficult for a pathogen to evolve away from," he added.

A Growing Threat to Wheat, Rice, and Barley

The need for better protection is becoming more urgent as blast disease continues to threaten major food crops.

Wheat blast was first identified in Brazil during the 1980s and has since spread to parts of Asia and Africa. Although the disease has not been detected in the United Kingdom, climate change could eventually create conditions that allow it to threaten cereal production in northern Europe.

For farmers, the emergence of blast disease in new regions presents a serious challenge, particularly because the fungus can threaten crops that are essential to feeding large populations.

By revealing how a previously underexplored class of plant immune receptors can be engineered to recognize multiple fungal threats, the researchers have identified a possible new strategy for protecting future harvests.

Further testing will be necessary to establish whether these modified defenses work effectively in whole plants and under agricultural conditions. If successful, combining different immune receptors could eventually help scientists develop crops with more resilient protection against one of agriculture's most destructive fungal diseases.

Materials provided by John Innes Centre. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#36122. Book Review: Back from the Brink

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

• Success stories of animals threatened with extinction whose fortunes have been reversed • Tales of lifetimes dedicated to wildlife conservation • Features some of the most iconic animals from around the world

Back from the Brink is an antidote to a world that seems full of stories of wildlife doom and gloom. Amongst all the loss of habitat and the animals and plants that are in spiralling decline, it’s easy to forget that there are a huge number of positive stories too; animals threatened with extinction, such as the gigantic European Bison –  extinct in the wild – having their fortunes reversed and their futures secured.

This is the story of some of these successes. How the Humpback Whale, in seemingly terminal decline because of commercial whaling, is today recovering naturally, getting back to the numbers that swam in our oceans before they were viciously harpooned. Others have needed considerable help such as the enigmatic Arabian Oryx, the origin of the unicorn myth, that was reintroduced to the fabled Empty Quarter deserts of Arabia where over a thousand again roam.

These are stories of enormous personal courage, dedication and patience by those protecting animals like the Black Rhino; of reinstating damaged or destroyed habitats for predators such as the enchanting Iberian Lynx; and of reintroducing birds such as America’s tallest, the Whooping Crane, to places where they once thrived but had long gone. Back from the Brink recounts the struggle to win the support of local communities to accept and bolster the populations of some of our largest animals such as the Mountain Gorilla – one of our closest relatives – and the magnificent Siberian Tiger, both of which once seemed destined for extinction.

The re-introduction of the Wild Turkey, extirpated from most American states by early white settlers, was successful because of biologists’ ability to learn from early mistakes. The gorgeous Large Blue butterfly – extinct in England by the 1970s – would not be thriving today without the incredible investigation that unravelled its complex living requirements, a lesson in detection that would have challenged Scotland Yard’s finest investigators. And others, like the gentle, lumbering Florida Manatee, its numbers recovering very slowly in part due to enormous public support. It’s the kind of care and consideration that Man needs to share to make our planet a richer place for us all.

Dr. Smith is a biologist and former Chief Scientist at the Countryside Council for Wales and is currently a Board Member of The Environment Agency. He is a prolific writer on wildlife, environment and travel and has had numerous features published in a variety of magazines and newspapers.

“Malcolm Smith has provided us with a fascinating insight into the numerous threats faced by some of our most vulnerable wildlife and the resourcefulness of those who dedicate their lives to protecting it. These are uplifting tales of hurdles overcome, crises averted and disasters prevented.’ Extract from Foreword by Mark Rose, Chief Executive Officer, Fauna & Flora International

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon, SPNL, has officially released the seventh issue of Al Hima magazine, reaffirming its commitment to advancing community-led conservation and positioning Lebanon as a regional leader in nature-based solutions. This latest edition comes at a critical moment for environmental action in Lebanon and the wider region, bringing together scientific insight, traditional knowledge, and global perspectives under the unifying theme: “From Ridge to Coast, One Hima at a Time.”

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon (SPNL) is dedicated to conserving biodiversity, promoting sustainable development, and safeguarding Lebanon’s natural and cultural heritage. As the national partner of BirdLife International, SPNL embraces the Hima approach, a community-based conservation model that harmonizes nature, people, and culture. Through education, advocacy, and partnerships, we empower communities to protect vital ecosystems and species while ensuring livelihoods and well-being. Join us in our mission to preserve Lebanon's unique environment for future generations. Visit our website or follow us on social media to learn more and support our initiatives. Together, we can make a difference.

Horsh Kayfoun, Luc Hoffmann Hima Center،, Itani, Kayfoun, Mount Lebanon

#36123. Nació una condorita, hija de aves rescatadas que ya no pueden volar

Desde el Centro de Rescate de Fauna Silvestre Tatú Carreta, que se encuentra ubicado en Casa Grande, provincia de Córdoba, celebraron el conmovedor nacimiento de Achala, se trata de una hembra de cóndor andino que nació el pasado jueves 10 de septiembre. Según lo publicado por La Voz, la pequeña hembra de cóndor andino nació…

Desde el Centro de Rescate de Fauna Silvestre Tatú Carreta, que se encuentra ubicado en Casa Grande, provincia de Córdoba, celebraron el conmovedor nacimiento de Achala, se trata de una hembra de cóndor andino que nació el pasado jueves 10 de septiembre.

Según lo publicado por La Voz, la pequeña hembra de cóndor andino nació de forma natural, es decir, no requirió de intervención veterinaria. Sin embargo, todo el proceso se fue monitoreando a través de una cámara testigo remota.

Según lo que indicaron desde el organismo, Achala sería la cuarta cría de una pareja de cóndores que fueron rescatados, pero se trata de la primera hembra que tuvo la pareja después de Yastay, Camín y Yuspe.

Con respecto a la publicación del Centro de Rescate en su cuenta de Instagram, los padres son dos cóndores rescatados después de haber sufrido heridas de bala. Les dispararon y ninguno de los dos cóndores pudo volver a volar como tampoco se los pudo reinsertar a la vida silvestre. Desde que los encontraron heridos, los dos cóndores andinos se permanecen en el centro y más allá de que no pudieron recuperar su libertad, sí consiguieron reproducirse.

En la actualidad, Achala la pequeña cóndor recién nacida, se encuentra acompañada por sus padres y la van a mantener al menos por siete meses más en el Centro Tatú Carreta. En cuanto su desarrollo y todas las condiciones externas se lo permitan, la majestuosa ave sería liberada cerca del año de vida, del mismo modo que sucedió con los hermanos.

Con respecto al cóndor andino, se lo considera una especie que está amenazada y que se pueda reproducir es fundamental, tanto para la especie como para los ecosistemas argentinos. La reproducción de los cóndores es parte del trabajo que lleva adelante de manera continua este Centro de Rescate.

Más allá de que los padres de Achala no pueden volar, la pequeña tiene todas las posibilidades de hacerlo, como también, va a tener la posibilidad, en cuanto crezca, de vivir su vida en libertad.

#36124. As Attacks Mount, Shipping Faces an Uncomfortable Question: Are Safety Rules Fit for War?

Essential news coupled with the finest maritime content sourced from across the globe.

Vessels in the Strait of Hormuz, Iran, May 22, 2026. Majid Asgaripour/WANA (West Asia News Agency) via REUTERS

By Paul Morgan (gCaptain) – October has become the worst month on record for attacks on merchant ships in and around the Strait of Hormuz, and oil is now washing onto the beaches of the United Arab Emirates.

The engineering question that follows is uncomfortable: were the ships under fire ever designed, equipped or crewed to survive what is happening to them?

On 23 October, Reuters reported data from the Washington Institute for Near East Policy recording 28 confirmed and reported attacks in the Strait and surrounding waterways during October, and 113 since the US-Israeli war with Iran began on 28 February. The institute has tracked maritime security incidents worldwide since 2017, and October tops its records.

The IMO’s own list records around 120 incidents in the Strait and the wider Middle East since the conflict started, while UKMTO had logged 135 conflict-related incidents by 16 October. The totals differ because coverage and methods differ, and UKMTO reporting is voluntary, but the direction of travel is beyond dispute.

The damage to the tanker fleet is striking. A Reuters review of IMO and MarineTraffic data found that more than 30 very large crude carriers had been damaged, including 11 in October alone, equivalent to around 3.5% of the world’s VLCC fleet. Greek risk consultancy Marisks counted a dozen ships attacked between 5 and 11 October, the highest weekly total of the conflict.

The human cost has been heavy. By September the IMO counted at least 22 seafarers killed in 80 verified attacks around Hormuz since the end of February. Five more died after an attack on the Honour 25 off Somalia, from which survivors were rescued on 29 September, a reminder that the danger is no longer confined to one strait.

Commercial pressure keeps ships moving into harm’s way. Reuters reported daily freight rates above $1 million drawing tonnage to load inside the Gulf, even as IMF PortWatch recorded only four transits of the Strait on 18 October against a pre-war average of around 85 a day. Every one of those transits is a crew betting on probability.

The stakes explain why ships keep coming. The Strait normally carries around a fifth of the world’s seaborne oil and LNG, and each laden VLCC holds roughly two million barrels. That concentration of value and hazard in a narrow waterway makes it an ideal target, and it means every successful strike carries an environmental as well as a human price.

Then came the pollution. On 23 October, the UAE Ministry of Climate Change and Environment confirmed that teams were containing oil leaking from a tanker offshore. Umm Al Quwain closed its beaches and coastal areas, Sharjah closed Al Hamriya beach as a precaution, and the following day Ras Al Khaimah shut all of its beaches until further notice. There is also reports of oil pollution on some of the beaches!

The ministry did not name the vessel or confirm the cause. International maritime agencies have identified it as the Gem No.2, a 330-metre tanker struck in a suspected drone attack on 9 or 10 October, from which 22 Indian seafarers were rescued. A European Space Agency radar image from 21 October showed oil leaking from a tanker about 25 kilometres off Umm Al Quwain.

The IMO had already taken a political position. At MEPC 84 in the spring, member states adopted resolution MEPC.406(84), strongly condemning Iran’s attacks on merchant vessels in the Gulf and Strait of Hormuz region. Condemnation, however, does not seal a ruptured cargo tank, and the engineering consequences now landing on UAE beaches demand an engineering response.

It is worth asking what the international safety framework was built to do. SOLAS fire protection, detection and extinction requirements were developed largely around accidental fires: a leaking fuel pipe on a hot exhaust, an electrical fault, a cargo fire. The design assumption is an internal event, detected early, contained by structural boundaries and fought with intact systems.

An external strike breaks every link in that chain at once. A missile or drone can breach structural fire boundaries, rupture fuel and cargo lines, sever cable runs and damage fire mains in a single instant. Fixed systems may be compromised before anyone reaches a release station, and the compartment designed to contain the fire may now have a hole in it.

Electrical power is often the decisive weakness. Emergency generators, switchboards and their cabling are deliberately separated from main machinery spaces, but separation designed against an engine room fire may not survive blast and fragments. Loss of power means loss of fire pumps, ventilation control, steering and lighting at precisely the moment every one of them is needed.

Fuel isolation is another vulnerability. Quick-closing valves and remote shutdowns work only if their air, hydraulic or electrical controls survive the initial damage. On a tanker, holed cargo tanks can add flooding, progressive leakage and stability concerns to the fire, forcing a crew trained to handle one emergency at a time to manage three simultaneously.

The ISM Code requires companies to identify potential shipboard emergencies and to establish procedures, drills and exercises to respond to them. In principle, that obligation already covers attack damage. In practice, many safety management systems still treat fire, flooding and blackout as separate scenarios with separate checklists, rarely rehearsed together.

The ISPS Code, adopted in the aftermath of the September 2001 attacks, was built largely around preventing unauthorised access, stowaways and terrorism through security assessments, access control and security levels. It was never intended to tell a chief engineer how to restore power after a projectile has passed through the funnel casing.

That leaves a gap between security planning and damage control. Navies spend generations learning to fight fire, flooding and structural damage together, with zoned systems, redundancy and crews drilled to the point of instinct. Merchant ships carry a fraction of the crew, no dedicated damage control parties and equipment sized for accidents, and nobody should pretend otherwise.

Nor is turning tankers into warships the answer. The cost would be prohibitive and the principle wrong, because the protection of merchant shipping is ultimately the responsibility of states. However, there is a substantial middle ground between doing nothing and armour plating, and much of it lies within the gift of owners, managers and their technical departments.

Combined-casualty drills are the obvious starting point. Crews should rehearse a scenario in which fire, flooding and loss of main power occur together, with key personnel deemed injured and a primary control position deemed lost. Such drills are uncomfortable and often exposing, which is exactly why they are so valuable.

Engineering reviews should follow. Technical departments can examine the routing of emergency power cables, the location and protection of emergency fire pumps, the independence of local controls for quick-closing valves, and whether portable pumps, smoke extraction and hull patching materials are carried in quantities sized for damage rather than routine leaks.

Pollution response must sit within the same plan. A crew fighting to save a ship should know which tanks can be isolated, how cargo can be transferred within the hull, and how quickly salvage and pollution response can realistically arrive in a war zone where tugs and response vessels may themselves be targets.

Gas carriers, chemical tankers and ships carrying dangerous goods deserve particular attention. A breached LPG or chemical cargo presents hazards to the crew and to surrounding coastlines that a crude spill does not, and emergency plans written for collision or grounding may not reflect the speed and violence with which an attack can escalate.

Crew welfare is part of resilience too. Seafarers working under the constant threat of attack carry fatigue and stress that degrade decision-making precisely when clear thinking matters most. Rest management, honest briefings and genuine freedom to refuse unsafe passages are not soft extras; they are conditions for a crew able to fight for its ship.

Abandonment presents its own dilemma. Lifeboats and liferafts are designed for a ship that is sinking or burning, not one that may be struck again while crews are mustering. Masters need clear company guidance on when to abandon, when to shelter in a protected space, and how to communicate their position when satellite positioning itself may be unreliable.

Regulators have a role as well. Flag states and classification societies could usefully share anonymised damage data from this conflict, showing which systems survived, which failed and why. The industry learned hard lessons during the tanker war of the 1980s, much of that knowledge has faded, and it should not have to be relearned at the cost of further lives.

The SOLAS, ISM and ISPS framework remains sound for the world it was designed for, and most ships will never face a missile. But the ships in the Gulf this autumn are facing exactly that, and their crews deserve emergency arrangements tested against the threat in front of them rather than the one imagined when the rules were written.

Ships were built to survive the sea. This year has shown that some must now be prepared to survive the people on it, and the time to plan for that is before the next alarm sounds, not after.

#36125. Parque Nacional El Palmar cumple 60 años y los festeja con pájaros al amanecer y música clásica al atardecer

Este fin de semana largo, el Parque Nacional El Palmar se suma al mayor evento mundial de observación de aves y, al día siguiente, recibe a un cuarteto de jóvenes músicos santafesinos que estrenará en Entre Ríos una obra del compositor argentino Carlos Guastavino. Dos propuestas gratuitas para celebrar seis décadas de uno de los…

Este fin de semana largo, el Parque Nacional El Palmar se suma al mayor evento mundial de observación de aves y, al día siguiente, recibe a un cuarteto de jóvenes músicos santafesinos que estrenará en Entre Ríos una obra del compositor argentino Carlos Guastavino. Dos propuestas gratuitas para celebrar seis décadas de uno de los tesoros naturales de la provincia.

El Palmar está de cumpleaños, y lo festeja a lo grande. En el marco de las celebraciones por los 60 años del Parque Nacional, este fin de semana largo se desarrollarán dos actividades pensadas para toda la familia, que combinan naturaleza y arte en uno de los espacios protegidos más emblemáticos de Entre Ríos.

El sábado, a contar pájaros junto al mundo entero

La primera propuesta llega este sábado 10 de octubre, cuando el Área Protegida se suma al Global Big Day – Conteo Mundial de Aves, el mayor evento de observación de aves del planeta. Durante toda la jornada, El Palmar formará parte de esta edición de primavera, en lo que representa una oportunidad ideal para descubrir nuevas experiencias de turismo en la naturaleza y acercarse al avistaje de aves en un entorno único.

La segunda cita llega el domingo 11 de octubre, a las 18 horas, en el Centro de Visitantes, con la presentación del Cuarteto Jacarandá, un grupo integrado por jóvenes músicos de Santa Fe, estudiantes avanzados de la Licenciatura en Música de la Universidad Nacional del Litoral. El cuarteto está conformado por Emanuel Ramos y Juan Pablo López en violines, Samuel Segovia en viola y Milena Hedjan en violonchelo, y nació de la necesidad compartida de hacer música académica con una impronta joven, buscando siempre la belleza tanto en el repertorio europeo como en el latinoamericano.

El concierto promete ser un recorrido musical de alto vuelo: arranca con el cuarteto «Emperador», del compositor clásico Joseph Haydn, y continúa con «Andante Festivo», del finlandés Jean Sibelius, ya dentro del período romántico. La segunda parte del programa está dedicada enteramente a compositores argentinos, con un dato que vale la pena destacar: se interpretará el cuarteto para arcos de Carlos Guastavino, una obra de gran riqueza sonora que retrata los paisajes del Litoral y que, según explicaron desde la organización, no se tocó demasiado en el país y nunca antes se había presentado en Entre Ríos. El cierre llegará con «Changuito», una composición del joven músico tucumano Samuel Segovia, el mismo violista del cuarteto.

#36126. News in Photos: White Storks leave Germany already for the south

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International license. (2025)

A group of White Storks gather on a field in Hessen, southwestern Germany on September 2, 2013. Some migratory birds leave Germany already for the south.

Photo credit: AFP PHOTO / DPA / BORIS ROESSLER

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon, SPNL, has officially released the seventh issue of Al Hima magazine, reaffirming its commitment to advancing community-led conservation and positioning Lebanon as a regional leader in nature-based solutions. This latest edition comes at a critical moment for environmental action in Lebanon and the wider region, bringing together scientific insight, traditional knowledge, and global perspectives under the unifying theme: “From Ridge to Coast, One Hima at a Time.”

The Society for the Protection of Nature in Lebanon (SPNL) is dedicated to conserving biodiversity, promoting sustainable development, and safeguarding Lebanon’s natural and cultural heritage. As the national partner of BirdLife International, SPNL embraces the Hima approach, a community-based conservation model that harmonizes nature, people, and culture. Through education, advocacy, and partnerships, we empower communities to protect vital ecosystems and species while ensuring livelihoods and well-being. Join us in our mission to preserve Lebanon's unique environment for future generations. Visit our website or follow us on social media to learn more and support our initiatives. Together, we can make a difference.

Horsh Kayfoun, Luc Hoffmann Hima Center،, Itani, Kayfoun, Mount Lebanon

#36127. Inauguraron en Ciudad de México el primer Museo del Gato con actividades inmersivas

Inauguraron en Ciudad de México el primer Museo del Gato con actividades inmersivas

Días atrás se inauguró el primer Museo del Gato en la Ciudad de México, con experiencias inmersivas de realidad aumentada que prometen mostrarle al público cómo perciben el mundo estos animales semi domésticos, compañeros de tantos humanos a través de la historia.

El Museo del Gato está emplazado frente a la Glorieta de las Mujeres que Luchan, un monumento icónico de la colonia Tabacalera, barrio de la capital mexicana.

En realidad, la dirección exacta del museo es el cuarto piso de Paseo de la Reforma 87 y es «una probadita» de la visión que tuvieron sus creadores, los periodistas Esther Garcilita y Marco Sotomayor, cuando decidieron hacer realidad su sueño de un espacio dedicado específicamente a los felinos más cercanos al humano.

«Ya llevamos mucho tiempo dedicándonos a los gatos. Es sólo una probadita de lo que queríamos realmente hacer y de todas las ideas que teníamos, pero esperemos que lo disfruten», expresó Garcilita en declaraciones reproducidas por la Secretaría de Cultura de México.

Originalmente el proyecto tenía nueve salas pero en septiembre de este año abrió con tres.

Como no podía ser de otro modo, el museo recorre la historia de los gatos en la cultura universal a través de su «Génesis felina», que incluye los cultos dedicados a ellos en el Antiguo Egipto, pero también hace énfasis en su biología.

Ese es el objetivo del módulo Instinto Puro, que cuenta con un juego tipo arcade relacionado con las feromonas e invita al público a someterse a una experiencia de realidad virtual para percibir el mundo como lo hacen los gatos.

También está el Domo del Ronroneo, y en una tercera sala se desarrolló el módulo Conexión, donde está el ejemplar de Sissi, la gatita que acompaña al público a lo largo de la muestra, y donde los visitantes pueden sumar impresiones digitales de sus propios gatos para dejar su huella.

El primer paso de Garcilita y Sotomayor en el mundo de la exploración felina, después de años de escribir sobre estos animales, fue la tienda La Gatería, que abrieron en 2012 y devino en el primer «cat café» de Latinoamérica.

La recaudación de las entradas será donada para la compra de una unidad móvil de esterilización para perros y gatos en situación vulnerable.

Somos una organización no gubernamental sin fines de lucro abocada a difundir, proteger y promover los derechos de los animales

#36128. SAT-JAM: Ships must learn to navigate without GPS

A researcher in Denmark who has developed a new system for tracking satellite navigation interference from space has a stark warning for the shipping industry in today’s Maritime CEO interview: GPS can no longer be considered a dependable means of navigation.

Kian Bostani Nezhad, a PhD researcher at the Technical University of Denmark (DTU), believes the maritime industry needs a fundamental rethink of its reliance on satellite positioning.

“GNSS interference has become a perpetual problem,” he tells Maritime CEO. “Everyone who uses GNSS must change their mindset from GNSS being a primary and dependable navigation solution, to it being a supplement to other navigation methods.”

Nezhad is the creator of SAT-JAM, a freely accessible online platform launched in September that maps GNSS interference detected by satellites orbiting hundreds of kilometres above Earth.

The project began with an unexpected conversation last year.

“I heard a spacecraft operator complain about their spacecraft having problems with GNSS over Europe,” he recalls. Intrigued, Nezhad searched for publicly available navigation data collected by low-Earth-orbit satellites and discovered several European Space Agency missions providing the information. Every mission he examined showed evidence of interference.

Nezhad then submitted a research abstract to the Advanced Maui Optical and Space Surveillance Technologies conference in March, before developing SAT-JAM, inspired by GPSJam, a website tracking interference experienced by aircraft.

His findings demonstrate that disruption affecting ships in the Baltic, Black Sea and other maritime hotspots is not confined to the Earth’s surface. Satellites operating 400 to 600 km above the planet can also experience degraded positioning signals.

The platform uses publicly available European Sentinel satellite data, updated as new observations become available, to identify where spacecraft experience interference. However, Nezhad stresses an important distinction between detecting interference and locating its source.

“SAT-JAM does not show where GNSS interference originates from, but where spacecraft measure the GNSS interference,” he explains. “The actual source, and the measurement can be far apart, due to the high altitude of satellites.”

The distinction matters for shipowners attempting to understand the geographical spread of jamming and spoofing. Satellite measurements cannot automatically be interpreted as evidence of interference affecting vessels directly beneath them.

Nezhad’s research background also includes maritime surveillance. His master’s thesis explored applying facial recognition algorithms to satellite imagery to identify and re-identify individual ships.

Asked what practical advice he would offer shipowners facing increasingly unreliable satellite navigation, Nezhad identifies two priorities.

First, operators should keep pace with improvements in GNSS receiver technology, particularly controlled reception pattern antennas (CRPA), which can suppress interfering signals.

His second recommendation is more fundamental – seafarers must learn to navigate without GNSS.

“I know it is easier said than done, but GNSS interference has become a perpetual problem,” he stresses.

Britain warned shipowners earlier this month that satellite navigation can no longer be relied upon in major shipping regions, urging crews to practise operating without GPS. New guidance from the Maritime and Coastguard Agency highlights growing jamming and spoofing threats across the Baltic, Black Sea, Middle East Gulf and East Asia, affecting everything from ECDIS and AIS to autopilots and port systems.

Maritime intelligence firm Windward recorded an average of 5,131 daily jamming incidents during the third quarter. The MCA recommends regular navigation drills, alternative positioning methods and greater awareness of equipment vulnerabilities.

#36129. World’s Largest Battery-Electric Ship Begins Commercial Service in South America

Essential news coupled with the finest maritime content sourced from across the globe.

Incat Tasmania-built China Zorrilla, the world’s largest battery-electric ship, makes its maiden commercial crossing between Buenos Aires, Argentina, and Colonia, Uruguay. Photo: Incat Tasmania

The world’s largest battery-electric ship has officially entered commercial service, marking a major milestone for the maritime industry and the growing use of electric propulsion in large passenger vessels.

The 130-meter China Zorrilla, built by Australian shipbuilder Incat Tasmania for ferry operator Buquebus, completed its first commercial crossing between Buenos Aires, Argentina, and Colonia, Uruguay, on October 22.

The high-speed catamaran can carry up to 2,100 passengers and 225 vehicles across the River Plate, operating entirely on battery power without the need for conventional marine engines.

At the heart of the vessel is an energy storage system exceeding 40 megawatt-hours, made up of more than 250 tonnes of batteries. The system powers eight electrically driven waterjets, making China Zorrilla the largest battery-electric vessel ever constructed.

The ship is supported by dedicated high-power charging infrastructure at terminals on both sides of the river, allowing its batteries to be recharged between crossings.

The start of commercial service follows an official ceremony in Buenos Aires on October 21, attended by Incat Managing Director Craig Clifford, Buquebus representatives, and maritime authorities and government officials from Argentina and Uruguay.

Incat Chairman Robert Clifford described the achievement as one of the proudest moments in the company’s history, and credited hundreds of workers at Incat’s Hobart shipyard with overcoming significant engineering challenges to deliver the vessel, which represents one of the most ambitious projects in the company’s history.

“This ship proves that large, high-capacity commercial vessels can operate on electric power,” Clifford said.

Beyond its size and technical capabilities, China Zorrilla also offers a different passenger experience. Without conventional diesel engines, the vessel operates with significantly less machinery noise and vibration, while producing no direct exhaust emissions during crossings.

Craig Clifford, who traveled aboard the vessel during its maiden commercial voyage, said the difference was immediately noticeable.

He noted that passengers aboard a vessel of this size would normally expect to hear and feel powerful engines operating beneath them, but that familiar noise and vibration are largely absent.

The ferry also features extensive passenger amenities, including approximately 2,300 square meters of duty-free retail space.

The project’s completion is particularly significant given its evolution during construction. Originally designed to use dual-fuel engines capable of burning diesel and liquefied natural gas, the vessel was ultimately redesigned for fully electric propulsion, requiring substantial engineering changes.

Its entry into service demonstrates the growing potential for battery-electric technology in high-capacity ferry operations, particularly on fixed routes where shore-based charging infrastructure can support regular crossings.

For Incat, the milestone is also part of a broader shift toward electric vessel construction.

The Tasmanian shipbuilder is currently building three 129-meter battery-electric ferries for Danish operator Molslinjen, along with two 78-meter hybrid-electric vessels at its Hobart facility.

With China Zorrilla now carrying paying passengers, the company has moved one of the maritime industry’s most ambitious electric vessel projects from the shipyard into everyday commercial operation.
