#36131. Tanker Disabled in Strait of Hormuz Attack, Another Vessel Reported on Fire in Persian Gulf

Essential news coupled with the finest maritime content sourced from across the globe.

Vessels in the Strait of Hormuz, as seen from Musandam, Oman, July 31, 2026. REUTERS/Stringer

Two new maritime security warnings issued Monday point to continued dangers for commercial shipping in the Middle East, with one tanker confirmed disabled by a projectile in the Strait of Hormuz and another vessel reportedly on fire in the northern Persian Gulf.

UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) said it received a verified, time-late report of a tanker struck by an unknown projectile while transiting outbound through the Strait of Hormuz on October 23 at 0839 UTC.

The impact disabled the vessel, although all crew members were reported safe. UKMTO did not identify the tanker or provide details about the extent of the damage. No assessment of possible environmental impacts was available.

The incident was disclosed three days after it occurred, highlighting the continuing delays in reporting attacks against commercial vessels in the region.

In a separate warning issued Monday, UKMTO said it had received multiple third-party reports of a vessel on fire approximately 63 nautical miles southeast of Al-Faw, Iraq, in the northern Persian Gulf.

The vessel’s identity, crew status, extent of damage and potential environmental impact were unknown.

UKMTO classified the report as suspicious activity rather than a confirmed attack. The cause of the fire remains unclear, and authorities are investigating.

The two reports come amid continued attacks on commercial shipping in and around the Strait of Hormuz, with violence increasingly extending into the wider Persian Gulf.

In its latest advisory, issued October 25, the Joint Maritime Information Center (JMIC) maintained its SEVERE threat assessment for the Strait of Hormuz, warning that further deliberate attacks were highly likely.

The assessment also kept the threat level across the wider Persian Gulf at SUBSTANTIAL, citing five attacks inside the Gulf since October 7, including incidents near anchorages and ship-to-ship transfer areas.

JMIC reported 156 confirmed maritime security incidents across the region since March 1. That figure was published before UKMTO disclosed the latest verified tanker attack.

The advisory highlighted another recent incident involving an LPG tanker struck by an unidentified projectile on October 22, approximately seven nautical miles northeast of Limah, Oman. That vessel subsequently reached an anchorage off Khor Fakkan.

According to JMIC, Iranian Revolutionary Guard Corps activity continues to include attacks, attempted attacks, drone overflights, surveillance and radio communications directing commercial vessels toward Iranian-controlled routes.

The latest incidents also underscore the growing risks beyond the Strait itself, particularly in the Persian Gulf, where ships waiting at anchor or conducting cargo transfers remain exposed.

Despite efforts by the United States to restore shipping through the Strait of Hormuz, commercial traffic remains well below historical levels.

JMIC reported 101 U.S.-facilitated vessel transits between October 22 and 24, compared with a historical average of approximately 138 vessels per day in 2025. The figures use different reporting methodologies, with the U.S. tally covering assisted transits rather than all commercial movements.

Separate tracking data showed only single-digit daily transits outside the U.S.-facilitated system.

Congestion also remains severe. As of October 25, JMIC estimated approximately 492 vessels were waiting outside the Strait of Hormuz, with large concentrations near Fujairah, Khor Fakkan and other regional anchorages.

The continuing disruption comes as U.S. forces enforce a naval blockade of Iranian ports while attempting to maintain safe passage for other commercial shipping.

JMIC warned that vessels using the Strait face persistent threats from projectile attacks, possible renewed mining activity and electronic navigation interference.

UKMTO has advised vessels operating in the affected areas to exercise caution and report suspicious activity while investigations continue.

#36132. Shipping cuts training when it needs it most

Irene Rosberg, who runs the Blue MBA at Copenhagen Business School, returns to Splash today.

When business conditions become difficult, training is often one of the easiest budgets to cut. In shipping, that is becoming an increasingly dangerous habit.

The industry is asking its people to understand decarbonisation, artificial intelligence, cyber risk, increasingly complex regulation and geopolitical disruption, often all at the same time. Yet education and professional development can still be regarded as discretionary expenditure rather than part of a company’s ability to operate successfully. There is a contradiction here because shipping wants better leaders, more skilled seafarers and managers capable of making increasingly complex decisions, but those capabilities do not simply appear when they are needed.

Today’s maritime leader must be commercially savvy and technologically literate. Executives need sufficient understanding of AI and digitalisation to question the information put in front of them rather than simply accept it. They need to understand cyber risk, regulation and decarbonisation while remaining capable of responding when geopolitical events suddenly alter routes, costs and commercial assumptions. Operational experience remains enormously valuable, but experience alone cannot prepare someone for every challenge now entering the industry.

This is why I find the tendency to reduce training when companies come under financial pressure so short-sighted. Sometimes the lack of the right skills, knowledge or leadership capability may be contributing to the difficulty a business is experiencing in the first place. Cutting the investment that could address those weaknesses can make the problem worse. The same applies at every level of the industry. We rightly talk about training seafarers to handle new fuels, equipment and technology, but professional development cannot stop once somebody reaches shore management or the boardroom. Seniority should not exempt anyone from continuing to learn. If anything, the consequences of outdated knowledge become greater as responsibility increases.

A chief executive does not need to become an AI engineer or a cyber specialist, just as a board member does not need to become an expert in every new fuel. They do, however, need enough understanding to challenge specialists, recognise risk and make informed decisions about issues that may determine the future of the company. That requires investment and shipping has historically placed considerable value on experience, and rightly so. But the speed of change means that what someone knew ten or even five years ago may no longer be sufficient for the decisions they face today. Lifelong learning cannot be a phrase we use in speeches while treating the training budget as expendable when margins tighten.

Companies should also look carefully at what they mean when they say they cannot find the right talent. If the perfect candidate is expected to arrive with operational experience, commercial acumen, digital literacy, regulatory knowledge, leadership capability and an understanding of emerging technologies, perhaps the answer is not simply to keep searching. Perhaps companies need to build more of that capability themselves. That means identifying people with potential, exposing them to different areas of the business and continuing to educate them throughout their careers. It means developing seafarers for shore positions and managers for executive responsibility. It also means recognising that the future leaders of shipping may come from broader professional backgrounds than they did previously.

Education should not simply prepare somebody for their next job. Done properly, it creates organisations that are better equipped to deal with the unexpected. That matters enormously in shipping because unpredictability has become part of everyday management. A geopolitical event can alter trading patterns overnight. A cyber incident can affect operations across a fleet. New regulation can change investment decisions, while AI can alter established ways of working faster than traditional training structures can respond.

No company can predict every disruption. It can, however, decide whether it has people capable of understanding what is happening and responding intelligently. Human capital should therefore not be regarded as a cost to be cut when times are difficult. It is part of the infrastructure of a maritime business, every bit as important to resilience as technology, vessels and financial capacity.

Shipping will spend enormous sums preparing its fleets for the future. It should be equally serious about preparing the people who will be expected to lead them.

#36133. From Forest to Mill: Sharing Knowledge Across the Wood Supply Chain

National Forest Products Week | October 18–24, 2026

The forest products industry is built on connections. From the forests where timber is grown and harvested to the trucks transporting raw materials and the mills manufacturing everyday products, each part of the wood supply chain plays an essential role. Connecting the people, businesses, and expertise across this supply chain is central to FRA’s mission.

During National Forest Products Week, FRA celebrates everyone who makes this industry possible. It is also an opportunity to recognize something equally important: the knowledge and experience that help the industry navigate challenges, improve operations, and prepare for the future.

Insight is another pillar of FRA’s work, reflected in its quarterly Technical Releases (TRs), which bring together research, industry experiences, practical guidance, and emerging technologies to inform and support professionals throughout the wood supply chain.

Technical Releases are an FRA member benefit, and members must log in to the FRA website to access them. FRA also occasionally makes select releases publicly available when the information has broader value to the industry. One recent example is 26-TR-24: Update on Impact of Skyrocketing Fuel Prices on Forest Products Transportation, which FRA made available to the public because of the widespread impact of rising diesel costs on the wood supply chain.

FRA’s 10 latest Technical Releases highlight the breadth of issues affecting the wood supply chain, from rising fuel costs and mill viability to employee retention, forest management, and new approaches to industry communication.

Transportation Costs and Business Planning

26-TR-24: Update on Impact of Skyrocketing Fuel Prices on Forest Products Transportation

Rising diesel prices have significant consequences for transporting raw forest products. Based on the study’s assumptions, the increase in diesel prices between September 2025 and September 2026 is estimated to have added an average of $2.12 per green ton to delivered wood costs, equivalent to more than $920 million in additional annual transportation fuel costs across the sector. The findings demonstrate how energy costs affect businesses throughout the wood supply chain.

26-TR-28: Using the Futures Market to Budget for Diesel Price

Predicting diesel costs can be difficult, but futures market data can offer useful guidance. This release explains how businesses can combine publicly available diesel futures prices with historical regional pricing information to develop more informed fuel budgets. Although futures prices do not guarantee future costs, they provide an alternative to relying solely on current prices or guesswork.

People, Safety, and Industry Communication

26-TR-25: Telling the Forest Story: Social Media’s Role in Public Education and Engagement

Social media provides an opportunity for forestry professionals to engage directly with the public and share the realities of modern forest management. Drawing on the University of Idaho’s experience reaching millions of viewers, this release demonstrates how digital storytelling can educate audiences, address misconceptions, and introduce new generations to the forest products industry.

26-TR-26: Wood & Iron: A Logging Podcast, For Loggers. By Loggers.

Produced by the University of Wisconsin-Stevens Point’s Wisconsin Forestry Center, Wood & Iron brings firsthand logging experiences to an accessible podcast format. Covering business management, workforce development, equipment, safety, and changing market conditions, the program offers logging professionals an opportunity to learn from their peers and exchange ideas across regions.

26-TR-31: Promoting Camera Technology to Improve Safety in the Mississippi Log Trucking Industry

Truck-mounted cameras can provide footage to support driver training, document incidents, and identify driving practices that warrant attention. This release shares findings from three Mississippi workshops attended by 70 business owners and employees. It also identifies the installation and subscription costs that can discourage adoption, illustrating both the benefits and practical challenges of introducing new safety technology.

26-TR-32: Retention Strategies Leading Wood Products Employers Are Using to Keep Their Best People

Retaining experienced employees is increasingly important as wood products manufacturers face labor shortages and competition for skilled workers. This release outlines strategies beyond competitive compensation, including clear career pathways, stronger frontline leadership, employee development, cross-training, and preventing burnout. Its central message is that successful retention requires sustained investment in people.

Forest Management, Markets, and Innovation

26-TR-27: Forest Innovation Platform, a Forest Information Tool

Developed by American Forests, the Forest Innovation Platform provides free access to more than 40 datasets that can support forest management decisions. Users can evaluate information such as timber volume, forest conditions, wildfire risk, and projected environmental changes for specific geographic areas. The tool demonstrates how accessible data can help land managers better understand forest resources and potential risks.

26-TR-29: Techno-Economic Viability Assessment of the U.S. Wood-Consuming Pulp & Paper Industry

Pulp and paper mills serve as important markets for wood fiber, and their closures can affect the entire supply chain. Highlighting an analysis conducted by AFRY for the U.S. Endowment for Forestry & Communities, this release reports that AFRY’s assessment identifies nearly 20 U.S. pulp and paper mills at elevated or moderate risk of closure in the medium term, representing approximately 30 million green tons of annual wood demand. The findings underscore the importance of mill viability to forest management, logging operations, and other wood-consuming businesses.

26-TR-30: What to Do if You Encounter a Cemetery or Other Areas of Importance in the Forest

Forest management sometimes reveals historic cemeteries, former homesites, and other culturally or locally significant features. This release offers practical guidance for identifying, documenting, marking, and protecting these areas before and during forestry operations. It emphasizes communication with landowners, awareness of applicable requirements, and careful planning to preserve important sites.

26-TR-33: FY26 NEPA Efficiency and Timber Volume Positioning

The USDA Forest Service reports improvements in environmental review efficiency across Pacific Regions 5, 6, and 10. During fiscal year 2026, the regions signed 98 National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) decisions, with environmental assessment decision times improving by 34% compared with FY25. The release also highlights efforts to coordinate environmental planning with future timber sale schedules, helping position the regions to deliver more predictable timber supply.

While these releases cover very different subjects, they share a common purpose: making valuable information available to the people who can put it to use.

A logging contractor evaluating new safety technology, a mill manager considering workforce retention strategies, or a forester exploring digital mapping tools may face different day-to-day decisions. Yet each operates within a connected industry where changes in one sector can affect another. FRA’s Technical Releases help bridge those connections by sharing research, operational experiences, and lessons from across the country.

Technical Releases also reflect the diversity of expertise that supports the forest products industry. Researchers, foresters, logging professionals, manufacturers, transportation specialists, and industry partners all contribute knowledge that can help businesses make informed decisions, improve operations, and navigate changing industry conditions.

As we celebrate National Forest Products Week, we recognize not only the essential products, jobs, and economic contributions of the industry, but also the collaboration and shared expertise that support its continued success.

Explore these releases and more in FRA’s Resource Library.

Learn more about the industry through FRA’s Wood Supply Chain Schematic.

#36187. USFS Funding for New and Expanded Markets

Earlier this month, the USDA Forest Service announced over $105 million in funding for a range of projects that support the forest industry. Through the Wood Innovation Grant program and the Community Wood program, these grants are meant to “promote wood and energy infrastructure by increasing manufacturing capacity and operational efficiencies and support rural economic prosperity and jobs.”

“The greatest threat to forest landowners and those who use forests is the lack of critical markets in today’s timber and wood products industries,” said U.S. Forest Service Chief Tom Schultz when announcing this year’s awards. As markets change – often quickly – in so many parts of the country, these grant programs can offer critical support as forest industries seek to invest in expanded production, new uses of low-grade wood, or increased operational efficiency.

With this round of grants announced, now is a great time for FRA members to start thinking about whether they have projects on the drawing board that would be a good fit for this annual program. While there are modest changes to the funding focus each year, the following summaries – based on last year’s announcement and subject to change – can help provide some guidance and help you consider potential projects:

Based upon previous year’s timing, FRA expects the next funding opportunity to be announced in October, with applications due in December and grants awarded in May.

Examples of Wood Innovation Grants awarded this year include:

Examples of Community Wood Grants awarded this year include:

A complete listing of awarded grants is available at the link below, and reading the brief public summaries can help a company understand the types and range of projects that can be funded. When the next Wood Innovations Grant cycle opens – expected in October – FRA will be sure to let our members know.

Note: The FRA team has significant experience with these grants; both Western Region Manager Lindsay Warness and Northeast Region Consultant Eric Kingsley have previously served as grant reviewers for the USDA Forest Service. In addition, the FRA federal advocacy team has been working aggressively to ensure that both of these programs are reauthorized as part of the Farm Bill. Both WIG and Community Wood are included in the House and Senate Farm Bill reauthorization measures. We are also working to ensure that these programs are fully funded as part of the Fiscal Year 2027 Interior, Environment and Related Agencies appropriations bill.

Wood Innovation Program Grants Awarded 2023 – 2026 – https://www.fs.usda.gov/science-technology/energy-forest-products/wood-innovation/grants

Press Release – https://www.fs.usda.gov/about-agency/newsroom/releases/us-forest-service-invests-1055-million-grants-support-wood-industry

Program Website – https://www.fs.usda.gov/science-technology/energy-forest-products/wood-innovation

Eric Kingsley | FRA Northeastern Region Consultant
